GENERAL INTRODUCTION Reading Must Be Taught 

The old thought that reading is a subject to be taught only through the sixth grade has been properly and emphatically discarded. There has never been any proof that pupils had mastered, by the end of the sixth grade, the basic skills and abilities that would make them competent or superior readers. Possibly the urge to classify, the urge to terminate, the urge to make nice groupings, has been responsible for the false feeling that reading was, as the formula goes, a matter of, first, learning to read, and, second, reading to learn—the whole process coming to a triumphant close at the end of the sixth grade. Beyond that grade it has been largely and fallaciously assumed that the pupils should be permitted to sink or swim because no more could be done for them. 

On the other hand, it should not be assumed that pupils have learned nothing about reading by the time they reach the seventh grade or the junior high school. It is safe to assume that reading may proceed at this level to seek higher goals than were attainable at lower levels. As these goals are sought, the skills which permitted the attainment of lesser goals in earlier grades must be maintained and increased. 

Reading Is a Thought-Developing Process 

It is helpful to think of reading in these upper grades as a thoughtdeveloping process. Reading becomes a means of encouraging thought. 

It becomes not only a subject that requires thinking, but a process that stimulates thinking. Furthermore, it encourages systematic thinking along sequential lines, thinking which itself has goals inseparable from the goals of the reading that stimulates it. 

The most immediate general goal of reading as a thought-developing process is to find genuine enjoyment. It will be seen later, when specific sections of these books are presented for teaching, that the first specific goal to be sought by pupils is that of genuine enjoyment. It will be found then and must be indicated now, that enjoyment is not an empty, surface pleasure that the word genuine indicates the depth of possibility inherent in this word. Enjoyment is of many kinds, and it is set up here as a goal worthy of the effort its attainment requires. 

Considered in point of time, enjoyment implies the present, the pleasure one has as he reads; it implies the pleasure of anticipation, the enjoyment of looking forward on the basis of the past; it implies also the pleasure of retrospect, the enjoyment of looking back upon the satisfac- 3

tions of previous reading experience. The time clement of enjoyment means finally this: that pupils will like what they read as they read it, and that they will arrive at the end of their formal educative process with a deep love of the reading that they have done in school-a love that will stay with them and guide their tastes and interests in later reading. 

The Two Major Goals 

Reading as a thought-developing process must proceed, must lead to something. In general it leads toward each of two major goals: (1) Mastery of appreciation through training in appreciation techniques; (2) 

Mastery of information through training in fact-finding techniques. It is not helpful to guess which of these achievements is greater; it is necessary to realize that they should be and can be made concurrent. 

1. Reading for appreciation. The Cultural Growth Series assumes that the true value of reading and literature is its power to help pupils understand the world and the people who live in it. This series undertakes to lead pupils to an appreciation of this power through a planned program of contact with selections that encourage the type of knowledge that leads to culture.

Five areas of knowledge are listed here as the basis of a planned program for appreciation: Awareness of the Physical World, Social 

Awareness, Awareness of the Complexities of Human Nature, Awareness of Moral Values, and Awareness of Aesthetic Values. Selections in each book in the series permit and encourage understanding and appreciation of some or all of these aspects of cultural knowledge. The growth line of pupil progress in the absorption of this knowledge lengthens from book to book.

For example, three aspects of the physical world, pleasures of action, nature study, and pleasures of discovery in the first book of the series are supplemented in the second book by pleasures of accomplishment, and enjoyment of travel, and in the third book by recognition of the extent of the world, and recognition of the variety of world enterprise. 

The three aspects of the physical world developed in the first book are developed in the second book and the third book, in addition to the new aspects introduced.

The increase in the number of contributing selections from book to book provides an increase in opportunity for cultural growth through planned progress. The program for cumulative growth in the second book in the series is discussed on page 63 of this monograph, and for the third book on page 127.

As examples of selections in the first book, EXCURSIONS IN FACT AND 

FANCY, contributing to Awareness of the Physical World the following 

4

titles are listed: "Swimmers," "Going Down Hill on a Bicycle,” “An 

Adventure with a Giant Squid," "The Story of a Salmon," "The Great 

Blizzard," "An Indian Summer Day on the Prairie," "An Antelope 

Mother Faces Danger," "Sailing West to Find the East," "Byrd Swoops over the North Pole,” “A King in All His Glory," and "An Abode of Splendor."

Selections contributing to Social Awareness are: "The Spelling 

Match," "The Glove and the Lion," "The Inchcape Rock," "Yussouf," "Jo Makes a Call," and "Nobility."

Selections contributing to Awareness of the Complexities of Human 

Nature are: "The Substitute Pitcher," "The Dreamers," "How Potts 

Saved the Night Express," and "The Bird of Paradise.”

Thus through this Cultural Growth Series pupils are trained through a planned program of progressive interest to acquire appreciations which grow in significance with the pupils' time growth, which is automatic, and their cultural growth, which must be, and is in this series, a result of training. 

2. Reading for information. This reading goal is easier to reach. The finding of information is a project that requires the bringing together of the seeker for facts and the sources of facts. The seeker must be proficient in the methods of his search, in order to find what he needs. Before he finds his source, he must know how to find it. After he has found it, he will have to know what information to select from it. After he has found information, he will have to know how to use it, how to convert it into an increased ability to think independently. In general these are the necessary steps.

Specific exercises and studies designed to assist in the mastery of these steps appear in these three books. References to exercises that assist in this process appear at the end of each section presentation in this monograph. 

The winning of this goal is a matter of intelligent work. There is no doubt that a careful study of the material and exercise equipment for each of these books in this series will make it possible for pupils to achieve this goal.

Summary 

The six books in the Cultural Growth Series are designed to encourage a love of reading and the appreciation of literature, to stimulate an interest in reading as a thought-developing process, and to supply the content and exercise equipment which maintains and increases reading skills. This monograph is intended to serve a helpful part in making it possible for teachers and pupils to use the first three texts in the series with the greatest efficiency. 5

GENERAL TEACHING PROCEDURE 

Before taking up the teaching of each of the books in the Cultural Growth Series, the teacher should read and study the Teacher Preface for the book she is using. Here she will find, on page 5, a list of the objectives which form the basis for the achievement of the two basic goals mentioned above in the introduction to this monograph. In order that these objectives may be understood with complete clarity they are listed here with explanatory paragraphs. 

Objectives in Reading 

1. To create genuine joy in reading. Genuine joy in reading is the first step toward the true appreciation of literature. With genuine joy, the child proceeds to learn happily and readily the "best that has been known and said in the world." Keen interest is the basis of genuine joy in reading. From a vast body of material eligible through its literary excellence, the selections in each book in this series were chosen on the basis of pupilinterest. 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. Literature has the power to widen and deepen for readers the experiences of their everyday life. It brings to readers the enriching enjoyment of the actions, thoughts, hopes, and dreams of others. Selections were chosen for these books that guide the child to the broad new world of reading and literature in which he may live vicariously. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. The best writers for children, as well as for adults, are those who know the truth, and have the facilities for expressing it either realistically or imaginatively. The good teacher of such writings is the teacher who helps the child see the basic truths of society the author has interpreted, and the characters he has created. A large share of the selections in these books contain interpretations of life and encourage development of character. 

4. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. The child, like the adult, will pursue what he knows to have value. For that reason it is imperative that there shall be in books of reading and literature for young readers only those selections which have obvious and demonstrable excellence. The selections in this series have that excellence. 

5. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. It is likely that many pupils have not properly formed their fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. 

Thus there are throughout this series exercises directly bearing upon specific skills of both reading and speaking. 

6. To increase efficiency in both oral and silent reading. Through

study helps-preparatory notes, exercises, and section reviews-pupils are guided efficiently toward an understanding and appreciation of the material they read. 

7. To make definite and real correlation between good reading and good speaking. This correlation is made in each book of this series through fitting the speech improvement material to the selections and to the center-of-interest sections with which, and in which, each speech selection appears. The relationship thus established is in most instances an organic one growing out of a genuine correlation between the speech material and the other selections. 

8. To guide and elevate literary taste. Literary taste is the result of an interested acquaintance with good reading and literature. The selections in each of the books in this series were chosen to contribute their share to the guidance and elevation of literary taste. 

Returning now to page 5 of the Teacher Preface in the text, the teacher will find the bases for choice of the selections in each of the books. On page 6 she will find that each book is organized according to purposefully planned design. All these facts will be helpful to her in presenting and developing material. The list of Teaching Aids begins on this page of the Preface also, and should be carefully studied. On page 7 begins the material relating to the Program for Speech Improvement, which is an integral and important part of the work in each of these texts. The teacher will want to pay special attention to this speech material on pages 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11 of the Teacher Preface. 

On page 11 also will be found valuable information on the use of this text. The specific suggestions beginning at the bottom of this page and filling page 12 will be supplemented by material that follows later in this monograph concerning the specific suggested techniques for teaching each section in each book.

The teacher will note that these books are constructed around centers of interest, and that each selection within each section contributes to the center of interest with which the section is concerned. 

However, values of teaching through the use of strands of appreciation and following the plan of literary types are also recognized. Therefore, at the end of each book division of this monograph is a list of the content of the book arranged by strands of appreciation, and by literary types. This listing is made for the convenience of teachers who prefer to supplement or supplant the center-of-interest plan. 

All other appended material in this monograph is referred to specifically in the space allotted to the treatment of each section, and need not be mentioned here.

EXCURSIONS IN FACT AND FANCY SECTION ONE-THE FIELD OF SPORT AND PLAY 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading in the field of sport and play is immediately interesting to children, and inducts them with pleasure into the larger field of reading and literature. The section consists of the following 

Sportsmanship (p. 18) 

The Andover Game (pp. 19-30) 

Revelation (pp. 30-32) 

The Contest of the Archers (pp. 32-38) 

Swimmers (pp. 38-39)

The Little People (pp. 39-44) 

Going Down Hill on a Bicycle (pp. 44-45)

The Spelling Match (pp. 45-49) 

The Hundred-Yard Dash (pp. 49-50)

The Substitute Pitcher (pp. 50-58) 

Knute Rockne's Boyhood Games (pp. 59-63)

The Great Game of Speech (Speech) (pp. 63-66) 

Da Greata Basaball (pp. 67-68) 

The Race for the Silver Skates (pp. 68-75)

Section Review (pp. 75-76) 

Purposes. In presenting Section One the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To develop systematically the right attitudes toward reading and speaking.

It will be seen throughout this monograph that the first purpose in the teaching of each section will be to create genuine joy in reading. This purpose is first always in position and in emphasis because it is assumed that no real reading benefits can accrue in the absence of real enjoyment of reading. One of the main criteria in the choice of material for this book, and every book in the series, was that of pupil-interest. This standard was set up for the same reason that this first purpose is consistent in this monograph—namely, that interest and enjoyment must precede the achievement of all reading skills and abilities.

In the material that follows, the teacher will find assistance in helping the pupils gain from the study of the contents of this section the chief values that the selections offer. 

1. Use of the introductory selection—“Sportsmanship." The teacher will begin the presentation of the section by reading to and with the pupils the poem on page 18. She will indicate to them that this poem is to be thought of as a symbol of the meaning and content of the entire section. She will want to stress for the pupils such points as these: 

That the poem is called "Sportsmanship"; that sportsmanship is something everyone knows about; that everyone knows he is expected to show the qualities of sportsmanship in all the games that he plays; that people who have the qualities of sportsmanship are the ones who are liked best in the games they play. She will point out also that sportsmanship is something bigger than just a quality that is looked for in sports: that it should be present in all the contacts we have with other people. 

2. A preview of the section. Then, having discussed with the pupils an idea that has obvious merit, and which by its connection with sports has obvious interest, the teacher will lead them to a brief acquaintance with the various selections in this first section. No time should be taken on this occasion for pupil reading, nor for more than the briefest kind of discussion.

In conducting the preview the teacher may stress such points as these: That "The Andover Game" was a football game, but that it was more than an ordinary game; that on one of the teams there were several players who didn't like each other and therefore couldn't work together to win; that it will be interesting to see what happened. 

Concerning the poem "Revelation" she will want to indicate that it is a poem about boys and teams, and has a real point. She may want to indicate that the next story contains an old reading friend, Robin Hood, and that his activities here will be, as always, interesting to read about.

And so the teacher will proceed through the section, making brief remarks about each selection in a way that will include the pupils in her own apparent anticipation of reading pleasures to come. She must be careful never to give the plot away, but always to say just enough to indicate that in their own terms pupils are going to find interesting reading here. In establishing these preliminary contacts for the pupils, the teacher will find the illustrations particularly helpful in creating immediate interest. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. For the teacher's convenience in correlating selections with other curricular studies, a list of suggested

relationships appears at the end of the first book division of this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. Before presenting each specific selection to the pupils, the teacher will have studied it thoroughly in order to find any concepts unfamiliar to her pupils, and to anticipate any other difficulties that may interfere with their appreciation of the selection. In connection with such a presentation, the Preparatory Note should be read with the pupils. This Note, with whatever additional discussion the teacher may feel is required for her class, is intended to serve as an introduction to the selection. The pupils will then read the selection. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. After all the pupils have read the selection, the teacher will want to discuss with them the exercise materiai that follows. It should be remembered that the main purposes of the exercises are to help the child interpret and understand what he has read, and to give training in basic reading skills. The exercises should never be used merely as measuring sticks for the amount of material mechanically remembered. Detail questions, should, of course, be answered in detail. General appreciation questions should be answered more generally and at greater length. Frequently in the exercises, written material is called for. The teacher may ask that other exercises be done and other questions answered either orally or in writing, as the particular needs of her class dictate. It may be pointed out, however, that much may be gained through discussing these types of questions orally with the pupils. The teacher should control the repetition of oral answers to make sure that there are not so many pupils making the same answers that the process becomes tedious. 

Group activities should be carried out in a spirit of co-operation that will make contributions to the social relations in the classroom and to the social aspect of reading and literature. These activities appear frequently, following the individual exercises. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the teacher proceeds with the presentation and teaching of each selection in the section, she should be constantly aware of the goals sought in the section. She should be equally aware of the need, as she goes along, of making these goals obvious to the pupils, and of supplementing the text with whatever assistance she may think required to reach these goals and accomplish the purposes as listed at the beginning of this reading section. The material that follows will give the teacher aid in remembering these purposes, and indicate methods by which they may be achieved. 10

In working toward the accomplishment of the first purpose in this section, which is the first purpose of every section in the book, the teacher should not limit her intentions. to a mere surface joy that is immediate and impermanent. The adjective genuine was chosen with the hope that the joy aroused and encouraged in connection with this purpose would be fundamental, basic, and the beginning of a permanent attitude. It is not enough, for example, although it is the first thing to be looked for and found, that children read and enjoy "The Andover Game" merely for its plot and its surface development of characters. If the story were left there, joy becomes a rather cheap thing, easily found and easily forgotten. 

This joy may be deepened, and should be deepened, by assisting pupils to see that the real point of the story is that teams cannot succeed without teamwork, and groups cannot succeed without individual contributions. The greatest joy the characters in this story found was in the realization that they could work together and forget the reasons that had kept them apart. These are real joys; they were real joys to the characters in the story. They are even more significant to the readers who have identified themselves, as they read, with the characters in the story, and to those who find parallel situations in their own actual lives and observations. This is what is meant by creating genuine joy in reading. 

Another aspect of joy in reading that should be stressed by the teacher and understood by the pupil, is its variety. For example, in the poem "Revelation," genuine joy comes to the boy who has, after brooding and unhappiness, discovered the truth that floods his mind with deep joy. 

He had not in an obvious way helped win any games, as had the characters in the first story, yet he had contributed to victory in all the games. 

Here, then, is a different joy. In "The Contest of the Archers" it is possible for the pupils to find an affectionate joy in the triumph of one with whose triumphs they have many times before been associated. This is a variant of the general joy that reading may bring. 

In the poem "Swimmers" is the joy of action, as it is also in the poems that come later, "Going Down Hill on a Bicycle" and "The Hundred 

Yard Dash."

In "The Little People" one may find the joy of sharing enthusiasm and industry. In "The Spelling Match" is the joy of fair play, and in "The Substitute Pitcher" the joy of accomplishment. 

Thus throughout the section and throughout all succeeding sections, the teacher should stress the importance of enjoyment as a substantial goal, as something deep, varied, and lasting. 11

2. To develop systematically the right attitudes and skills in reading and speaking. a. Reading. The teacher will understand that right attitudes toward reading cannot be imposed from above. They must be established by the pupil himself through his own understanding that reading has for him both interest and value. The most satisfactory way to encourage this feeling is to assist the child in finding material that will be valuable to him, and in helping him appreciate it to its fullest extent. Beyond this encouragement, however, must go the realization that pupils cannot read by interest and anticipation alone. Their abilities are based upon more fundamental learning.

There are many pupils who reach this year in their reading training without being fully convinced of the deep and fascinating pleasures of reading. It is quite likely that such pupils have failed in their previous reading training to acquire such basic skills and abilities as would make them better readers. Good readers, for the most part, find reading enjoyable. It should be a part of the teacher's constant labor to assist those who do not enjoy reading because they do not read well. Extensive and intensive use of exercises and activities leading to an increase in reading skills should be followed by such pupils with the teacher's encouragement and discussion.

It should be remembered always that there is more to the enjoyment of reading than the mere saying of an adult that such and such a selection is interesting-it has no interest to the child who cannot read reasonably well. With these pupils such basic skills as those listed on page 6, Excursions in Fact and Fancy, should receive special study. Specific references to exercises that contribute to training in definite reading skills appear at the end of each section discussion in this monograph. b. Speaking. In connection with finding real values and establishing specific skills, the teacher should, in presenting and teaching each speech selection, bring out the fact that good speech is a very practical quality, a goal that it is extremely important for everyone to reach. 

She will also want to point out, in order to help pupils see that speech is an essential part of their reading, that the selection on page 63 for example, "The Great Game of Speech," takes its title and a large part of its content directly from the kind of material with which it appears in this section. There is in this selection a hundred-word dash, an account of the rules of the game, and a word contest. 

The teacher should always approach the speech material with the conviction on her own part that it is of immediate and practical value, and that it presents for her no special difficulties in handling. All this speech material is presented in such a completely common-sense manner 12

that it needs no particularized training to make it effective for pupils. 

Teachers and pupils alike should approach this material with the assurance that they can find its value with interest and without difficulty. The speech material throughout this series contributes sequentially through the years to the growth in speech improvement. 

In addition to the aspects of speech already mentioned, is the general value that speech material has in the oral interpretation of literature. 

It should be kept in mind throughout all the speech improvement lessons in this book and in this series that the main non-technical contribution of the speech material is to a broadened and enhanced appreciation of literature through oral interpretation. This contribution is made in many ways, and specific methods for particular use will be mentioned within each section where such techniques are applicable. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will find the section reviews, which come at the end of each section in this book, particularly helpful in guiding the pupils to certain conclusions of understanding and enjoyment. 

One of the main purposes of the section review is to take the child back over the content he has read to remind him of some of the qualities he has found and some of the characters he has met, and to round out for him the general picture of his experiences. The exercises included in each section review make it possible for each child to contribute to his own summing up of what he has read. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher should at this time review the goals and purposes of this section with these questions in mind: What should have been accomplished in this section? Has everything been accomplished? Such questions, and action in line with the answers, will make certain that pupil difficulties are not accumulating to cause serious trouble in future reading. 

3. Use of book list. Following the section review exercises at the end of each section is a list of books that pupils may enjoy and to which some of them may have access. These lists are appended as a part of the carrying out of the basic thought that literature texts, when properly compiled and properly used, serve as a core in current reading experiences and as a guide for further reading experience.

SECTION TWO-THE DRAMA OF EVERYDAY LIFE 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that there are in the lives of most of us experiences that are interesting to live and interesting to read about. The section consists of the following selections: 

Alive! (p. 78) 

Roofs (p. 79) 

The Hut in the Gorse (pp. 80-90) 

Just Ev'rything (pp. 90-91) "C'n I Have a Dog?" (pp. 91-99)

The Sleeper (p. 100) 

Fresh from the Conversational Mint (Speech) (pp. 101-102) 

Human Traits in the Farmyard (pp. 103-107) 

Evening at the Farm (pp. 108-109)

How Potts Saved the Night Express (pp. 109-113) 

The Engineer (pp. 113-114) 

Hanging a Picture (pp. 114-116)

The House with Nobody in It (pp. 117-118) 

I Remember, I Remember (p. 118) 

Maggie and the Gypsies (pp. 119-129) 

The Dreamers (p. 129) 

The Uniform Invisible (pp. 130-140)

Speech in Everyday Life (Speech) (pp. 141-143) 

Betty's Ride: A Tale of the Revolution (pp. 144-148) 

My Mother's Words (p. 149)

Section Review (pp. 150-152) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Two the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to understand social situations and to understand character. 

4. To make obvious the value of establishing worth-while reading interests.

1. Use of the introductory selection—“Alive!" In presenting the section the teacher will proceed as she did in Section One to introduce the pupils to the scope and content of the section, bearing in mind that the 15

introductory selection in each section symbolizes the content of the section. The teacher should read this poem aloud with the pupils and stress for them such points as these: That the poem "Alive" is intended to give some idea of the spirit of the selections in this section "The Drama of Everyday Life"; that the poem can give in advance a word picture of the kind of material in the section; that one question the child asks, “And is not life the same as seeing things, as feeling them, as hearing, smelling, tasting them?" implies, and shows in its answer, that much that is concerned with the drama of everyday life will be offered; that apparently this section contains poems and stories that show people living and enjoying life in a variety of ways that will be interesting to read about. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will want to make brief statements about each of the selections in the section, not allowing time in this preview for any of the pupils to read any selection, however short. 

For example, she may want to indicate that the poem "Roofs" is about people who, though they spend most of their lives roaming, would much rather have homes. In the next selection, "The Hut in the Gorse," the pupils may be intrigued by the illustration on page 86. The children will be interested in the title of the story beginning on page 91, "C'n I Have a Dog." Such interest should always be encouraged. 

The teacher will want to tell the children, when they reach the selection on page 101, "Fresh from the Conversational Mint," that this is a speech selection telling about the way in which words help make good stories, and that it is a part of the Speech Improvement Program that these books have for the purpose of making everybody's speech more interesting and more nearly correct.

And so she will continue with the pupils through the section, examining briefly and discussing the possibilities for interest in each selection, and the possibilities of finding much material that contributes to the basic theme of the section, “The Drama of Everyday Life." The teacher's main purpose in this preview will be to instill and encourage the enthusiastic desire of the pupils to read this material later with keen interest. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Not many of the selections in this section are correlated with other studies, but those that have such relationships are listed at the end of the first book division of this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the most efficient use of the Preparatory Notes for this section, supplementing them whenever it seems necessary for the adequate preparation of her particular group. Any concepts that she may think too difficult for her group should be discussed and clarified in advance of reading. However, it is not likely that average groups, for whom these Preparatory Notes were designed, will need more information than the Notes contain. 16

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the helpful use of the Study Exercises, making sure as the pupils progress that they are gaining from their reading a maximum increase in their appreciation and reading skills. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the pupils proceed through the section, the teacher will make sure that they are aware of the purposes and goals that are to be sought in this section. This awareness should serve to help pupils read systematically, finding out as they go along how the selections contribute to the section meaning. 

 In presenting and providing opportunities for the carrying out of the first purpose, the teacher should keep constantly in mind the fact that enjoyment can be extremely various. 

For example, pupils may be taught to enjoy such word pictures as there are in the poem, "Roofs." Such phrases as "the starlight on my face," "life is a highway, a golden town where golden houses are" help to increase the reader's joy in words and the pictures that words make. In explaining such types of reading enjoyment the teacher should make sure that such phrases are meaningful; she should, without diminishing the magic of the phrase, indicate in a suitable and natural way the ordinary picture that the extraordinary phrase brings more vividly to life. 

Using this same poem as an example, it should be the teacher's purpose also to bring out the joy in the understanding of thought. In this instance the joy will be in understanding that everyone in the class has the privilege that the poet says everyone seeks: namely, a home. 

Finding joy in such a poem as "The Sleeper" is not so obviously easy. 

Here it will be necessary for the teacher to indicate that it is possible to find joy in the pleasant and helpful activities of others in this case Ann's desire to withdraw from the room without interfering with her mother's rest.

Joy in the use of speech is an important purpose throughout this book. 

The speech selection "Fresh from the Conversational Mint" contributes to such joy. It should be pointed out that freshness of language adds much to enjoyment in the use of words.

And so the teacher, having studied thoroughly every selection in the section, will be able to bring to the pupils' awareness the infinite possibilities of gaining pleasure from reading. Every selection in the book has its special contribution to the enjoyment of reading. The teacher will help make this contribution apparent to every child. 17

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. It is extremely important that this purpose be carried out whenever the material makes it possible. The ability to project oneself into the activities of others read about is an ability that lies at the base of enjoyment and appreciation of almost all creative writing. The teacher should use every opportunity to point out that it is possible to identify oneself with people read about, and thus to increase one's knowledge of the world and people. For example, the teacher may ask, in connection with the poem "Roofs," if anyone in the class ever felt that he had reason to be really glad that he had a home in which to live and to which to go. She may ask, in connection with "The Hut in the Gorse," if anyone really thought of himself as being one of the characters in this story, or of being at the place where this action occurred. Such questions will bring out affirmative answers by some, and lead to the desire of others to so identify themselves with characters in their further reading. It is almost certain that many pupils will have had experiences that help them to see themselves in the story, “C'n I Have a Dog.” 

The purpose of encouraging this participation in vicarious experiences should be to increase the pupils' knowledge of the ways of life and people. 

3. To increase pupil's ability to understand social situations and understand character. Most reading about people and events can be used to encourage the ability that pupils have to understand social situations and characters. In the story "Maggie and the Gypsies," for example, a social situation of the family, and the children who temporarily feel that they must have some adventure of their own, is presented. This is a basic situation with which most children are familiar. It is not difficult for them to understand why the child wanted to go away; nor is it difficult for them to understand how quickly, in the end, Maggie wanted to get home. In order to bring out this point, questions relating to why Maggie found the gypsies disagreeable, why Maggie preferred her home to that of the gypsies, may be asked to bring out the contrast between the two social situations pictured. 

The story "How Potts Saved the Night Express" is an excellent one in which to present strong character as an admirable trait. It may be kept in mind in stressing this quality of the story that Potts, the engineer, when confronted with a terrible situation, had various opportunities to act, and that he acted as he did because he was a man of fine character. 

4. To make obvious the value of establishing worth-while reading and speaking interests. a. Reading interests. It should be kept in mind by the teacher that it is never enough merely to tell children that it is necessary for them to understand the value of establishing worth-while reading interests. This 18

is not a concept that is readily absorbed by children of this age. Helping pupils achieve this purpose should be a teacher's long-time occupation. 

It can be commenced by a presentation by the teacher of such worthless reading as she may know some children indulge in, as contrasted with a higher type of reading on the same subject that she may find in this book. 

She may want to point out that the less desirable class of reading matter is concerned only with violent action, and that the higher type of reading, though it too stresses action, is concerned at the same time with telling the truth about story situations and story characters. 

Perhaps the approach may be made through humor. The teacher may be able to contrast such a story as "Hanging a Picture" with a type of crude slap-stick humor found in some radio programs and in some comic strips. She may point out that there really is some basis of reality in the action that takes place in "Hanging a Picture," whereas the humor with which she contrasts this story may be of an entirely unreal and silly nature; or it may be that in the beginning of the teaching of this goal or purpose the teacher may want to choose from the comic papers such comics as do have real charm and interest, and contrast them with those that have nothing but the cruder attempts at humor. This is not an easy thought to instill in the minds of all young readers, but it is a purpose and goal which may be gained through long and persistent effort. b. Speaking interests. In presenting the need to reach this goal and accomplish this purpose, the teacher should stress the importance to everyone of speech in his everyday life. The teacher should show that good speech improves everyone in a variety of ways. She can point out that the person who speaks best is frequently the person who succeeds best in public life. It may be that the mayor, or judge, or leaders in other departments of community life are good speakers, and that good speech helped them gain their leadership. Bringing it closer to the school situation, it is likely that a good many of the school leaders are leaders because they speak well. Approached on this basis, good speech can be made a practical goal toward which everyone should immediately progress. 

As the teacher proceeds with the subject of speech she will want to develop the interpretative aspects as well as the wholly practical ones. 

She may, in this connection, read several poems aloud, and then have various children read the same poems or other poems with the purpose of bringing out, through oral interpretation, certain word and phrase qualities that may have been lost when the poem was read silently. 

Special attention should, of course, be paid to both these phases of good speech in the selections "Fresh from the Conversational Mint” and "Speech in Everyday Life." These speech selections should be taught with the same procedures that are used for the teaching of other material 19

in this section. This speech improvement material, because of its close correlation with the other reading material around it, requires no special techniques in the learning or the teaching. It should be taught by the ordinary methods of presentation and absorption. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. Before leaving the section the teacher should make certain, through use of the section review and her own summing up of the pupils' learning, that the pupils have really absorbed the spirit and purposes of the section; that they have learned adequately the skills and abilities that have been practiced; that they have appreciated and retained the concepts and reading interests that have been presented. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. Before proceeding with further work the teacher will want to make sure that pupils are not accumulating deficiencies which will cause them trouble in their future reading. If through such checking the teacher does find pupils whose skills are below levels they are capable of attaining, she should through further use of the available exercises or through exercises of her own devising, bring the deficient pupils up to their proper level. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will find it helpful to increase the book list with titles of books that are available to her particular group. 

The children should be encouraged to take part in this listing.

SECTION THREE-THE WAYS OF NATURE 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that the ways of nature are various and that a study of these nature ways is both pleasurable and informative. The section consists of the following selections: 

March (p. 154) 

Bringing Back a Live Elephant (pp. 155-166) 

Rose Marie (pp. 166-171) 

The Mother Bird (pp. 171-172) 

Joy of the Morning (p. 172) 

Old Slewfoot (pp. 173-182) 

An Antelope Mother Faces Danger (pp. 183-185) 

Four Little Foxes (p. 186) 

Flight in the Fog (pp. 187-190)

Nature's Finest Sound Machine (Speech) (pp. 191-194) 

The Great Blizzard (pp. 195-199) 

An Indian Summer Day on the Prairie (pp. 199-200) 

The Story of a Salmon (pp. 200-206) 

An Adventure with a Giant Squid (pp. 207-211)

Mux (pp. 211-216) 

Coaly-bay, the Outlaw Horse (pp. 216-223) 

Stars (pp. 223-224) 

A Day in June (p. 224) 

The Sea (pp. 224-225)

The Sun (p. 225) 

Fog (p. 226) 

Hills (p. 226)

Section Review (pp. 227-228) 

21

Purposes. In presenting Section Three, the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To give assistance in building systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. 

1. Use of the introductory selection-"March." Before presenting the specific selections in the section, the teacher, having thoroughly studied the material of the section herself, will escort the children on a brief tour of the material. She will begin with the poem "March" on page 154, read it with the pupils and suggest to them that this poem, like the previous introductory selections, appears as a kind of symbol of the material that the section will include. She will want, in this instance, to point out to the pupils that Wordsworth is a particularly good poet to introduce this selection on nature because he was so close to nature, and because he so beautifully and completely portrayed the moods and beauty and secrets of nature. She may want to point out that this poem contains a great many aspects of nature study, and she may want to emphasize, as she reads the poem, the variety of observable and commonplace aspects of nature such as the rooster, the stream, the small birds, the lake, the cattle, and so on. It will be helpful also to point out the contributions made by the small border pictures. 

2. A preview of the section. Then proceeding through the section, using her knowledge of what each selection contains, she will want to indicate to the children that some of these selections contain observations of nature that make exciting reading, as, for example, the first story in the section, "Bringing Back a Live Elephant." In this connection the illustration on page 164 will be helpful. She may, in coming to the next selection, indicate that in this story they are going to leave the great elephants of the Orient and consider a very small subject of nature-a bird. 

She may point out to the pupils that the next two poems are about birds. 

Then coming to the story "Old Slewfoot," the teacher who has read The Yearling, from which this selection comes, may want to speak of the excellence and distinction and popularity of that fine book. 

She will want to point out that "Flight in the Fog" is an airplane story; that "The Great Blizzard" tells of an element of nature that frequently makes itself felt in many of the states of our country; that “An 

Adventure with a Giant Squid” occurs way up north of our country and tells of boys fighting an unusual animal. "Coaly-bay," she may want to indicate, is written by one who wrote many stories that many pupils have 22

read and enjoyed in the past. Then glancing briefly over the collection of short poems at the end of the section, the teacher may indicate that all these little poems have word pictures that pupils like, and ideas that are easy to understand. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section there are several selections that have close correlation with the subject of general science. A list of such relationships appears at the end of the first book division of this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. Then returning to the first major selection in the section the teacher, using the Preparatory Note and expanding it if the needs of her particular group require, will supply to her pupils the necessary background for a complete understanding and appreciation of the selection. This use of the Preparatory Note will be made throughout the section. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The instructions covering the use of exercise material, appearing in connection with earlier units in this book, apply to the exercises and activities in this section and in all others equally. 

It should be remembered in connection with the exercises and pupil activities that the greatest basic reading ability is to read with understanding and appreciation—but that before this final ability can be gained much work is required on other basic skills. 

Specific references to exercises in this section that contribute to training in definite reading skills appear at the end of this section program. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher will remember the importance of keeping pupils aware of the goals and purposes of each section and of the manner in which each selection contributes to the section goals. 

 The first purpose as written above is far reaching. It involves the entire purpose of books of this nature, namely, that comparatively brief and selective reading is presented always with the hope that it will lead to later and more extensive reading along the same lines. It should be the primary purpose throughout this section to make pupils pleasantly and deeply aware that there is in nature more of interest than anyone can possibly read about or observe in a whole lifetime. It should be brought to their attention as work in the section proceeds, that here they are getting samples of the writing and thinking that has been done concerning nature. In making this information pleasant and informative to pupils, constant reference should be made to the fact that their experiences in many instances help them to understand and appreciate these selections. For example, in 23

teaching the first story, the teacher may helpfully point out that most children have seen circuses, that in every circus there are elephants, and that this story helps explain how elephants are brought to zoos and parks and circuses.

Many children will have seen bird and animal activities of the sort described in "Rose Marie." Many of them will be familiar with, and all of them will be interested in, the airplane incidents reported in "Flight in the Fog." And so it will go throughout the entire section. Everything in the section contributes, and will more markedly contribute with the teacher's enthusiasm added, to the knowledge the children may have that reading about nature is both pleasant and informative, that it leads to genuine joy in living. 

2. To give assistance in building systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. a. Reading. Most pupils have discovered that in reading they find pleasure and information. Such pupils need little if any training in good attitudes toward reading. Encouragement is always helpful, however. 

Those pupils whose attitudes toward reading are not satisfactory must be (1) improved through training in reading skills so that they find pleasure in reading, and (2) taught through reading experiences that they may find valuable information through reading. Every effort must be made to help pupils realize that reading has something worth while for them. 

Good attitudes follow an awareness of the values of reading. b. Speaking. In connection with attitudes toward speaking, the teacher need not limit her activities to the study of actual speech selections in this book. She will want to bear in mind that every poem permits opportunity for oral interpretation, and that many prose selections may be developed orally, particularly those containing conversation. In arranging for such presentations, the teacher should be aware of the particular goals of enjoyment and clarity that may be had through oral interpretation. She will want to make clear to the pupils the manner in which these goals may be reached, and the values gained in reaching them. 

Such values concern better understanding, better appreciation of rhythms and rhymes, and a keener appreciation of dramatic possibilities. Awareness of values assures good attitudes. 

The speech selection itself, "Nature's Finest Sound Machine," should serve as the focal point for the discussion of speech and definite work in speech improvement. It will be noted that in this particular speech selection a great deal of specific instruction appears. The teacher should, of course, master the meaning of every line of this speech material. It is all understandable, it is all effective, but it is necessary with a speech selection of this kind that the teacher do more than read it before present- 24

ing it to the pupils. When effective results are to be gained by material. so specifically helpful as this, the teacher must bring her assistance to the task. With such assistance this selection will easily find its way into the understanding of every pupil in the class.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. As in previous sections the teacher should make sure, through the use of the section review and her own check on the pupils' learning, that everyone has absorbed the main intent of the section, and that everyone shows improvement in the basic skills of reading. 

No section should be left until every pupil is satisfied that he understands the material he has read, and has learned the essential purposes of the study of the section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making her check at this time in connection with the section review, the teacher will want to make. sure that the pupils are not piling up difficulties that will make them feel unhappy and uncertain about their future reading. If the teacher finds that some of her pupils need special work at this time, she will arrange to give it to them through further use of the available exercises or through review work of her own planning. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will make the customary use of the book list, adding to it with the pupils such titles as may be available in the school and community libraries. It will be helpful also for pupils who have read books suitable to the section to supply the descriptive comments.

SECTION FOUR-THE THRILL OF NEW TRAILS 

The basic thought contributed by the study of this section will be that through new trails of experience, new, varied, and worth-while reading interests may be found. The section consists of the following selections: 

The Pioneer (p. 230) 

Sailing West to Find the East (pp. 231-238) 

The Landing of the Pilgrim Fathers (pp. 238-239)

An American Thanksgiving (Speech) (pp. 240-243) 

Daniel Boone Outwits the Indians (pp. 244-252) 

Johnny Appleseed (pp. 252-254) 

On the Oregon Trail (pp. 254-262)

Somebody Said It Couldn't Be Done (pp. 262-263) 

The Pony Express Rider (pp. 263-272) 

Christmas Eve at Topmast Tickle (pp. 272-280)

The Rediscovery of Choral Speaking (Speech) (pp. 281-284) 

Byrd Swoops over the North Pole (pp. 285-290) 

A King in All His Glory (pp. 290-295) 

New York to Paris (pp. 295-301) 

Travel (pp. 301-302)

Section Review (pp. 302-304) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Four the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

 26

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. 

3. To teach greater appreciation of character. 

1. Use of the introductory poem-"The Pioneer." The teacher will make the customary use of the introductory poem, reading it with the class, and pointing out that it is symbolic of the type of material they will study in this section. She will bring out not only the surface facts of the action of the narrator of the poem, but also the basic meaning of what the narrator says. 

2. A preview of the section. In speaking of the content of the section in this brief survey the teacher may point out that the first selection tells something in dramatic form about Columbus; that the second is about the pilgrims who came to our country; that the speech selection is a play about Thanksgiving; that Daniel Boone, an old favorite, appears here; that Johnny Appleseed is a famous and interesting old character; that the Oregon trail was one of the famous routes to the early West; that the pony express rider here is Buffalo Bill; that "Christmas Eve at Topmast

Tickle" tells in fiction some of the wonderful acts of a great doctor in the north country; that choral speaking is a subject they will all enjoy; that "Byrd Swoops over the North Pole" tells of a famous airplane exploration; that "A King in All His Glory" will bring to them a new world of exploration; that "New York to Paris" tells in Lindbergh's own words something of his famous trip; and that "Travel" by Stevenson will contain ideas that all of them will like.

The purpose of this preview conducted by the teacher will be to indicate by her enthusiasm that everything in this section is going to be highly interesting. As she conducts the pupils on this little tour, the teacher will give them time enough to glimpse a few facts or word pictures that will permit them to feel that they are sharing in this preliminary enthusiasm. 

It will also permit them to enjoy the anticipation of reading later everything now so lightly touched upon. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. The teacher will find in this section much material closely allied to some other curricular studies. A list of suggested relationships appears at the end of each book division in this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher in returning to the first selection in the section will present it to the pupils through the use of the Preparatory Note, through pointing out the various study helps—including in this instance the pronunciation of the words in the cast of char- 27

acters—and, if she feels it necessary for her particular group, any additional material that she may care to present. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher should keep constantly in mind the necessity of maintaining all the basic reading and speaking skills that are necessary in the encouragement and maintenance of good reading. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of speech goals. When the specific speech selections are taken up, the teacher must make certain that all the values presented in these selections are fully received by the pupils. The teacher will already have noted that the speech material carries its own equipment for proper appreciation, understanding, and learning. It is necessary for the most part only that the teacher, through her enthusiasm and discussion, see that the pupils derive from the material the value that it has for them. 

Specific attention should be paid, in teaching the speech selections, to the details of information. Whenever it is necessary in a particular class situation to present more preliminary information than is supplied in the Preparatory Note, the teacher will find it easy to supply such information, taking her leads from the material in the Note.

When the class arrives at the speech selection on page 281 the material should be approached with the thought that choral speaking is a means of making poetry more keenly enjoyable and more easily understandable. Never should the teacher or the pupils approach this subject with any fear of its complications, for it has none, nor doubts as to its easy use. The teacher will find the subject presented here in a simple manner. She will find the suggested techniques clear and helpful, and the poems so marked as to make their choral performance easy and obvious. Choral speaking should be adopted as an everyday method leading to heightened enjoyment of poetry. 

 In connection with seeing that pupils achieve this purpose it is necessary for the teacher to keep constantly in mind that reading, in order to become a satisfying pleasure, must be combined always of two phases, (1) sufficient skill to make the actual process pleasant, and (2) sufficiently interesting and worth-while reading matter to make the results satisfying. The situation must never arise which permits the pupil to ask, “What's the use of reading this?" and receive no convincing answer. If the teacher prepares in advance to indicate that the reading matter is worth while, and if the book that is being used contains only material that is worth while, such questions will, of course, never arise. 28

Of such a selection as "Sailing West to Find the East,” for example, it might be said that any material about Columbus is bound to be worth while to American readers, particularly when it appears in a new and interesting form, as it does here, and shows Columbus as a boy, a period of his life with which most of us have not before been familiar. The teacher will want to have the pupils present this play orally. When such oral reading takes place the teacher must insist that each prospective participant shall know how to pronounce all the words in his lines, and how to bring out the full meaning of all the speeches he is to make. The teacher should explain the necessity of everyone's speaking well in the oral presentation of the play.

Such a selection as "Daniel Boone Outwits the Indians" contains all the elements of satisfaction in reading. The characters are real and fascinating. Their actions are thrilling. The background is one of early America, and the conclusions make it possible for us to believe in the heroism of those we have learned to respect. The same qualities are a part of the story, "On the Oregon Trail."

One point is demonstrable throughout this section, namely, that everything written about here is interesting to read, and leads to information and conclusions that are worth while. This is a part of teaching the pupil that he need never read selections that are without value, of helping him realize that thrilling stories may still have truth, and that story heroes may still be true. 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. In this section boys and girls in reading practically every selection will be able to project themselves into the exciting and helpful activities of the people written about. Any boy, for example, may walk the docks at Genoa, seeing or being Columbus. Children have long identified themselves with the exploits of Daniel Boone. Many of them have in imagination trekked the Oregon trail behind the oxen and covered wagon, or dashed merrily past on a near-wild pony. They have in imagination swooped through the skies with the best of pilots, or seen themselves as the best of imagined pilots. Some selections lend themselves better than others to this kind of vicarious enjoyment. When, as in this section, many selections are available for the teaching of this lesson, the lesson may be deeply taught. 

3. To teach greater appreciation of character. In this section also are many whose admirable characters are well pointed out. From the pioneer in the introductory poem, to the lone flier in the last selection, the aspect of heroism is practically constant. The heroes are not all those of violence. The pioneer was not a violent hero, nor was Columbus. In the heroism of Daniel Boone was much quiet courage. Johnny Appleseed's 29

heroism was of a gentle kind. It is well to point out to pupils that heroism is not always a blood-and-thunder accomplishment. Selections in this section make it easy to impress that truth. 

Questions may be asked to show how Columbus displayed courage by holding out for his own views when others laughed at him, what some of the pilgrim fathers had to withstand in their early experiences, how Daniel Boone showed his courage and ability by using his brains instead of his muscle, how Johnny Appleseed can be called a hero even though he fought no pitched battles.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. In going over the summary material and the section review exercises the teacher should make certain that all the pupils have received from their study of the section helpful and lasting attitudes toward material of this nature. The teacher should look back over the purposes set up for this section and make sure that so far as possible these purposes have been achieved. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In addition to making the special check in connection with the section review, the teacher will want tc make sure also that pupils are maintaining and increasing their reading and speaking skills as well as maintaining and increasing their abilities in appreciation and understanding. In making such a check it is desirable that the teacher keep in mind not only the particular section which has just been read, but all the sections in the book which have so far been studied. This more extensive check should be made with an understanding that these improvements in reading ability, in order to have lasting value, must be the result of a long-time improvement program and must lead eventually to permanent competence in reading. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will encourage the pupils to realize that these book lists are appended in order to make it easy for them to see that much reading along the lines which they have found in each section may be found in other books. The teacher will find it helpful to add to this list such other books as she and the pupils may know are available in local libraries.

SECTION FIVE-DOORS TO READING TREASURES 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that enjoyable and profitable reading may come from stories and poems that have for many years been favorites with many readers. The section consists of the following selections: 

Old English Song (p. 306) 

Robinson Crusoe Finds a Companion (pp. 307-313)

Poetry Treasures (Speech) (pp. 314-319) 

How They Brought the Good News from Ghent to Aix (pp. 320-322) 

Jo Makes a Call (pp. 322-330) 

Nobility (pp. 330-331)

Snow-Bound (pp. 331-343) The Inchcape Rock (pp. 343-345) The Legend of Sleepy Hollow (pp. 345-371) 

Tom and the Painkiller (pp.

371-375) 

The First Psalm (pp. 375-376) 

The Nineteenth Psalm (pp. 376-377) 

A Boy's Song (p. 377)

Section Review (pp. 378-380) 

31

Purposes. In presenting Section Five the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. 

3. To guide and elevate literary taste.

In presenting this section the teacher will want to be particularly emphatic about the pleasures to be found in reading, and particularly enthusiastic about the selections found in this section. It is difficult to judge in advance the possible prejudices that pupils may have about material that they know to be old. Pupils who have been well taught in previous school experiences, and who have been adequately encouraged by home and other influences to seek beauty and find interest in these old favorites, will go forward happily now to a further acquaintance with selections of this sort.

But pupils whose past experiences with this type of material have been for the most part unhappy ones, will need to have it proved to them by their current reading experiences that such material can be and is interesting. It is a basic desire and goal of this series of books to encourage children to find pleasure in their reading. 

This intent is indicated most obviously by the fact that for every section in the book the first purpose has been to encourage the enjoyment of reading. This does not mean that all reading contributes to the surface joys of the reader. It does mean that by a combination of surface joys and deep pleasures, reading should be and must become a real pleasure for children. Otherwise, any and all skills learned are to no avail; any and all abilities in understanding and appreciation are to no purpose. 

Again it should be pointed out in teaching that reading is a pleasure, that this means all reading, because the pleasures of information and the pleasures of instruction are no less real than the pleasures of entertainment. 

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Old English Song." The intro ductory poem, "Old English Song," is particularly suitable as a symbol for the material of this section. It should be read with and to the children for the purpose of indicating what keen delights and deep pleasures may be had in reading. The teacher will want to stress the various aspects of reading inherent in this poem-that a book may be read anywhere and under any conditions, that both the new and the old may be read about. and enjoyed, that a book is to many, as it was to the writer of this poem, 32

better than gold. Discussion of the pictures in the border will help create interest in the reading to follow. 

2. A preview of the section. In making a brief survey of this section, the teacher will follow the usual procedure. She will find much material here to supplement her enthusiasm. Many children will be familiar, for example, with parts at least of the story of Robinson Crusoe. The teacher will need merely to indicate that the first story comes from that wonderful book and is one of its most interesting parts. 

In "Poetry Treasures," a speech selection, she will want to indicate that here, arranged for choral presentation, are three funny and interesting poems that are written in our own times. These might be called immediate treasures. They are the only selections in the section that are contemporary. She may say of the next poem that a very famous author wrote it and that it is full of heroic and exciting action as is indicated by the illustration opposite page 321. 

Most of the children will be familiar with Little Women, and the teacher will need merely to say that this story, "Jo Makes a Call," is an interesting part of that popular book. Of "Nobility" it will be enough to indicate that this poem tells about a quality that we would all like to have, and it would be interesting to find out what that quality is. The poem "Snow-Bound" should be mentioned in this brief preview as a story in verse of real actions and real people. "The Inchcape Rock" may be mentioned as a story in verse that has a good plot and a surprise ending. "The Legend of Sleepy Hollow" can best be previewed by looking with the children at the illustration opposite page 353, pointing out and briefly discussing the main character, the ludicrous Ichabod. The Adventures of Tom Sawyer, like Robinson Crusoe and Little Women, is a book that most children will know something about. It will be necessary at this time only to point out that one of the most amusing adventures in that humorous book appears here for them to enjoy. 

Of the two psalms it may be said that much of the loveliest writing that we have comes from the Bible, and that reading these psalms will prove that fact. "A Boy's Song," the teacher may indicate, tells about the pleasures that a real boy had long ago. The pupils will see that some of these pleasures may still be enjoyed. 

Having thus set the stage for the appreciation of this section, the teacher will return to the first selection and proceed. 

3. Use of Preparatory Notes. The Preparatory Note for the first story illustrates the fact that the Notes vary in length and kind with the material for which they prepare. In using these Notes the teacher should remember that they are contributions to reading readiness, and that for 33

most pupils they are sufficient. However, whenever the teacher thinks that her particular group needs more preparation, she should supply it. 

Using the Note as a beginning, more material of the same sort can be added as the teacher may see a need for it for some pupils. 

4. Use of Study Exercises. Also throughout the section the teacher should keep in mind that the exercises and activities are supplied for the purpose of assisting the child in the acquisition of reading skills, and increasing his ability to understand and appreciate what he reads. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

5. Awareness of goals. The teacher will keep the pupils aware, as they proceed with the reading, of the section goals and purposes and the manner in which each selection contributes to the achievement of these goals. This awareness will serve to integrate the reading and maintain its purposeful direction.

 It must be kept in mind in this section, as it has been in all others, that the child cannot enjoy reading unless he finds certain values in what he reads. In this section those values should be numerous and obvious. Regarding the story of Robinson 

Crusoe, for example, most pupils already know that it has keen adventure interest. And as they read they will find it possible to share with 

Crusoe the pleasure of finding a companion. 

Reading the speech selection containing the three contemporary poems will be a delight without question. Every effort must be made to see that the children follow the directions for successful choral speaking, and carry out with adequate voicing and in the spirit of the poems themselves the instructions given concerning the reading of these poems. 

Again it may be mentioned that choral speaking is basically a simple procedure, and that neither the child nor the teacher should feel that this method of presentation has any great difficulties connected with it. 

But the teacher and the pupils should be aware that this procedure leads to an increased appreciation of literature through oral interpretation, and that many of the values of reading that might be neglected otherwise, may through these means be found and appreciated. 

It will be necessary, however, in the longer poem, "Snow-Bound," to point out that enjoyment comes frequently from a realization that a series of word pictures is telling the truth about life as it was lived in some parts of the country quite a few years ago. The teacher must not expect the pupils to read this poem with the same avid and obvious 34

interest that they would have in Robinson Crusoe. She will need to give more assistance and encouragement as this poem is read. By showing the human qualities that Whittier portrays, by paraphrasing occasionally, and by asking pupils to paraphrase now and then, she will be able to help pupils enjoy these pleasant pictures that have survived the test of time. In this connection a class discussion of the story the illustration suggests will be helpful. 

The same techniques suggested for the long poem may well apply to "The Legend of Sleepy Hollow," with the reading of which many children will need assistance. It should be pointed out here that Ichabod 

Crane is the memorable character, that much of the story is written about exaggerated ideas and statements that are not to be taken as truth, and that pupils must enter into the spirit of the fun and foolishness that Irving so pleasantly presents. Frequent use of the footnotes is expected in the reading of this selection, and it may be that for some groups the teacher will find it feasible to explain other words, rather than stop the reading too frequently because of vocabulary inadequacies. 

The beauty of the psalms can best be brought out through oral interpretation, either by effective solo reading, or by the class in unison, or in an arrangement worked out by the pupils. 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. It has been mentioned that enjoyment comes in Robinson 

Crusoe particularly because readers may share with Crusoe the experiences that he has. This sharing-this vicarious identification with the characters-is one of the broadening aspects of reading. It should be kept constantly in the pupil's mind so that he may be encouraged to project himself. This projection is possible in Browning's poem, and in the excerpt "Jo Makes a Call."

The next four selections do not permit much opportunity for the sharing of experiences, but the opportunity appears again in the excerpt from Tom Sawyer. It appears similarly in the next poem in the section. 

It should be understood that this sharing of experiences is not only for the purpose of increased enjoyment. Its larger value lies in the fact that it opens up, almost as readily as actual experiences, a new life, a new acquaintance with parts of the world hitherto unknown, and with people new and fascinating. 

3. To guide and elevate literary taste. This section particularly lends itself to the carrying out of the above purpose. As has been said before, it is impossible to insist to children that they always like certain things because certain things are literary. It must rather be proved to them that those items which have interested people for many years have demonstrable literary virtues. It must further be pointed out that these 35

virtues are in large measure responsible for the fact that people have liked these things for so many years. For example, the quality of reality that pervades Robinson Crusoe is the most marked literary characteristic of the author, Defoe, who was famous in his century and has been ever since, for his skill in making fiction read like truth. 

In Browning's poem it may be pointed out that here is action, nobility, accomplishment and heroics without the foolish aspects that accompany similar action poems or stories heard sometimes over the radio or read in trashy publications. It must not be expected by the realistic teacher that she can at once convince all pupils that this type of controlled and literary action surpasses the kind for which they may have formed an affection. It must, however, be the purpose of all conscientious teachers, authors, and publishers to bring to the child an awareness that actually in all ways the stories and poems that have real value are the only ones on which reading time can profitably be spent. Only after the substitution has been made in the pupil's mind of value for violence, can he be convinced that selections he finds in a good book are more worth while than the inferior material he has hitherto found fascinating. 

In bringing about these changes the teacher should bear in mind that the child must not, and cannot, be bludgeoned into this change, nor should his former heroes be stamped with ridicule and scorn. The fact that a child was fond of some character he has found in reading indicates that he has learned, at any rate, that through reading he can find interest. Such children should be drawn away from their former tastes and brought gently but certainly to higher tastes. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, making certain through it that pupils are sufficiently aware of what the section meaning and theme have been and of the manner in which they have learned and understood this theme. They should be reminded in this connection of the contributions that the various selections made to the main interest in the section, and it should be pointed out to them that the doors to reading treasures lead always to other open doors. Through the use of the section study exercises the teacher will impress upon the pupils the general truths that have been brought out in previous discussions concerning the section. These exercises should not be used as an activity of meaningless labor, but as a contribution to complete understanding. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make the customary check at this time of the goals and purposes that have been 36

sought in this section. She will want to make certain that pupils have the proper appreciation of the purposes they have been seeking, and ascertain the degree to which they have achieved these purposes. If there are any who have not progressed adequately toward competence in reading skills, the teacher will want to give them further instruction and further exercises in order to remove any deficiencies which may interfere with their future reading success. 

3. Use of book list. It will be particularly helpful in this section to point out to the pupils the rich possibilities that exist for further reading along these lines. With the pupils she will be able to expand the list by adding books that are available locally.
