SECTION SIX-MYTHS AND TALES 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that familiarity with myths and tales opens readers' minds to new fields of thought and imagination. The section consists of the following selections:

The Shadow People (p. 382) 

A Myth of the Seasons (pp. 383-388) 

Thor's Visit to Jotunheim (pp. 388-393) 

Pattern for Peace (Speech) (pp. 394-397)

Geraint and Enid (pp. 398-403) 

The Glove and the Lion (p. 404) 

A Tale of Sinbad the Sailor (pp. 405-410) 

Hiawatha's Fasting (pp. 410-414) 

Brer Rabbit Grossly Deceives Mr. Fox (pp. 415-417) 

Forty Singing Seamen (pp. 418-421) 

The Emperor's New Clothes (pp. 421-424) 

Overheard on Mount Olympus (Speech) (pp. 425-428) 

The Phantom Mail Coach (pp. 429-430) 

Section Review (pp. 430-432)

Purposes. In presenting Section Six the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste.

In presenting the selections in this section the teacher will want to help the pupils realize the differences between the material of reality and the material of fancy. She may point out, for example, that such a story as "Tom and the Painkiller" concerns a boy who might have been real, in actions of a sort that could be real. Then, anticipating some of the material in the section, she may speak of the visit of a god who never lived, to a place that could never have existed. She will then point out to the pupils the differences, through the use of these two examples, between what could have happened and what could never have happened. She will then want also to point out that this difference has nothing to do with interest, that the basically real is no more interesting than the openly fanciful-but that they differ in subject matter and the way in which the material is treated. 

1. Use of the introductory poem "The Shadow People." In sup port of these general differences that she will have discussed, the teacher may use the poem on page 382 to introduce this idea of interest in the 38

unreal. She will want to read the poem to and with the children, stressing the last line in which Bridget says to the child, "It is just your fancy, child." The teacher will point out that it is the child's fancy that permits him to hear all the odd and fascinating sounds that Bridget cannot hear.

The teacher will point out, too, how much richer the child's experience is for being able to use his imagination. 

2. A preview of the section. Then proceeding with the quick tour through the selections, the teacher will indicate by brief remarks concerning each selection the variety and interest that these selections have. 

She may want to point out in connection with “A Myth of the Seasons” that before the study of nature was a science it was explained by fanciful tales such as this. People knew, for example, that the seasons changed. 

They did not know why. They wanted explanations for these changes. 

This is one of the explanations that these ancient people had. 

She may want to say of the second selection that the pupils have probably read about Thor before, that he was the ancient god from whom our word Thursday comes, that here we shall see this powerful god on a trip to a strange kingdom. Of "Geraint and Enid" she may make references to the fact that the pupils have long known stories of knights in the days of old, and that this is one of them. In connection with the poem, "Hiawatha's Fasting," she may speak of Hiawatha as one of their old reading friends, and point out that this selection is probably one that they have not read before.

Probably the most appealing approach to be made in the Brer Rabbit story is through the illustration between pages 416 and 417. Similar means of approach may be used for the other selections in this section. 

The teacher should bear in mind always that the preview is made with enthusiasm to arouse interest. 

3. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, adding to them whatever may be necessary for the satisfactory preparation of a particular group. It would be helpful in this section for pupils to discuss other myths and tales that they may be familiar with, in order to develop and clarify concepts common to myths and tales. 

4. Use of Study Exercises. The customary use will be made of the 

Study Exercises, as pupil helps to understanding, and as practice material leading to increased skill in reading and speaking. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

5. Awareness of goals. The teacher will help the pupils remain aware of the section goals and purposes, and point out to them from time to time in what ways the selections contribute to the main thought of the 39

section.

Through this type of awareness pupils learn the values of sequential reading. 

 This purpose is in line with the basic desire throughout this book to help pupils believe deeply that reading is not an empty pleasure but a great one. It has been pointed out before and should be stressed always, that reading must indicate the way to substantial values the child can understand before he can become deeply appreciative of its importance. The first purpose in this section is devoted primarily to the bringing of proof to the idea that reading is an important pleasure, that its joys are genuine.

With imaginative children this will be an easy task. With those less imaginative, however, it is likely to be a hard one. The approach should be made in such a way as to indicate that without imagination a large share of possible experiences is cut out of our lives. The child who is too intent upon reality should be gradually led to an appreciation of this new kind of imaginative experience. In connection with this increase in the child's experience, no attempt should ever be made by the teacher to read reality into these things that are unreal. If these imaginative selections are accepted by the teacher and taught to the children as material that is openly and completely away from reality, the children will not be confused by excessive merging of the real with the unreal. 

It will be noted, for example, that the valley described was the home of Ceres, the earth mother, a goddess, one who could not possibly have lived in reality. With her is her little daughter Proserpine. Her friends were the daughters of the valley nymphs, who could not have lived. Let the pupils understand clearly that Proserpine and Ceres are living in an unreal world, and that the things that happen to them are unreal, as are all the other characters who appear in the story except a little peasant child and her father. And yet, in order to indicate the valuable pleasure that may be had from such tales, there is, over and above the delight that may be had in the story itself, the emotional values that the story is successful in creating. The relationship, for example, between Ceres and her daughter, between the goddess and the peasant, between all the other gods and Ceres, all help show the importance that is attached even in fanciful stories to the creation of character and the presentation of events.

These same kinds of values may be brought out in the teaching of every one of the selections in the section. Some distinction may well be made between the myths and the tales. The story concerning the knights, 40

and the poem "The Glove and the Lion," are tales, and have some connection with reality. When they are taught, the opportunity should be taken to indicate the differences between one of these selections and such a myth as the first selection in the section. 

The pupils will read both of the speech selections in this section with joy, and find in them more proof of the fact that reading is an important pleasure. From their experience in the reading of these speech selections they will gain further skill and practice in the finding of joy in literature through oral interpretation. The joy will be found here in the group reading and the part speaking, which when done well leads to the pleasing feeling of accomplishment. Pupils will find joy also in the actual content of these speech selections. "Pattern for Peace" contributes many thoughts pertinent to current thinking. "Overheard on Mount 

Olympus" encourages an increased understanding of an old and famous tale. 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste. Myths and tales create excellent opportunities for guiding and elevating literary taste. In the first place, they almost always have active and interesting plots. Also, they have characters upon whose exploits the adventures of some of the current exaggerated heroes of children may have been based. Many mythological characters were in many ways supermen and superwomen. Therefore, the inevitable requirement of material that is to elevate taste is present, namely pupil-interest.

Also it is true of most material of this nature that it is well written. 

This is partly true because such things would never have been made up in the first place except by people who had a marked ability in creative thought and imagery. Usually, also, people who rewrite and translate myths are interested in excellent writing. Therefore it is almost always observable that myths and tales of a simple and natural sort are almost invariably in good literary form. In presenting the purpose, and in carrying it out with the children, the teacher will indicate from time to time and when it is understandable, such portions of each selection as she finds will emphasize the matter of literary skill. It is by these means, both general and specific, that the teacher will gradually, and at times without perceptible results, guide and raise the literary taste of her pupils. 

There can be no gauge of this improvement, but it can nevertheless be very real.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, discussing with the pupils the lines written above the sec- 41

tion study exercises, and making certain through such discussion and the exercises themselves that the pupils understand and appreciate the meaning of the section. The exercises should be used to bring pupils' thinking about the section to satisfactory and understandable conclusions. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make the customary check on the pupils' achievement of the goals and purposes of this section, making sure so far as possible that everyone has reached the level of understanding and appreciation of which he is capable. In making her check the teacher may find that the learning of some of the pupils in this section should receive further attention. It should be remembered, however, that tangible evidences of growth are not always. possible in this appreciative side of reading. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will want to point out that many books have been written containing myths and tales because for so many thousands of years people have been attracted by interesting explanations of nature and by portrayals of heroic action. With the pupils she will add to this list books that are locally available, and help the pupils decide which of the books they will find most interesting.

SECTION SEVEN-DELIGHTFUL NONSENSE 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section is that reading can be fun. The section consists of the following selections: 

Jest and Youthful Jollity (p. 434) 

The Man in the Moon (pp. 435-436) 

Alice Meets Humpty Dumpty (pp. 436-443) 

The Whirling Dervish (p. 444) 

Let's Talk Nonsense (Speech) (pp. 445-446) 

Little Gentleman (pp. 447-453) 

The Owl Critic (pp. 453-454) 

Mending the Clock (pp. 455-459)

Four Famous Limericks (p. 460) 

Section Review (pp. 461-462)

Purpose. In presenting Section Seven the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purpose: 

To create genuine joy in reading.

In presenting this section the teacher will tell the children that there is much reading that is just plain fun, and that that kind of material, when it is well written, has a place in a book that presents many varieties of good reading and literature. 

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Jest and Youthful Jollity." The introductory poem will be used here as such poems have been used in previous sections. It will be helpful also to look over with the children the border pictures on page 434. The small illustrations will serve as well as brief comments about the selections themselves in arousing enthusiasm to read this section. 

2. A preview of the section. The presentation of this section will follow the procedures outlined for other sections, though the time spent in preview may be even briefer than it has been previously. 43

3. Use of Preparatory Notes. Through the use of the Preparatory 

Notes and whatever other preparation may be necessary, the teacher will direct the beginning of the reading and follow it through the section. 

Even in reading nonsense and humor the children should be encouraged to get from the selection all there is to be had. 

4. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the use of the exercises following the selections to help all the children understand what they have read, and to point up for them the ideas contained in these humorous selections. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

5. Help in vocabulary. The story "Little Gentleman," though it will appeal to all children and be understood by all children, has some vocabulary difficulties that the teacher may want to prepare the children for before they read the story, or as they read it. The footnotes will be found particularly helpful in this connection. 

To create genuine joy in reading. In developing further proof of the joy to be found in reading it should be made apparent to the children that humor, like all other qualities of literature, is of great variety. The teacher will want to encourage the children to point out just what it is in each selection that makes it funny. In this way the children will be helped to see for themselves how various humor is. However, it is very essential in teaching this material that the attitude toward the reading be kept bright and cheerful. The selections should not be made the basis for laborious effort. 

For example, in the poem "The Man in the Moon" the dialect helps contribute to the humor, and the children should be able to point this out. 

In teaching a piece of classical humor such as the Humpty Dumpty story, the teacher should be careful not to overestimate the reputation that this book once had and still has. Let the children find out for themselves whether they think this material is amusing. Most of them will think so without urging.

Such a poem as "The Whirling Dervish" may well be used in pointing out that humor is assisted sometimes just by the use of words that show action, speed, and interesting sounds.

The speech selection, “Let's Talk Nonsense," provides practice material for speech improvement, and should be taught for its value as a speech lesson. However, the selection also has its possibilities for fun, and these should be brought out as a means of contributing toward good atti-` tudes. Through such development of this speech selection the pupils will 44

come to an added understanding of the basic thought of the speech improvement contribution that through oral interpretation many aspects of literary appreciation are enhanced. When pupils find that oral interpretation makes even fun and humor more enjoyable, they will be more than ever convinced of the value of their speech work. "Little Gentleman" shows the humor of character and situation, and these points may be brought out by the children and discussed. In teaching the selection "Mending the Clock," the teacher may want to say that Barrie's humor is rather quiet and that its effects are gained by the quietness and the funny situation. In considering the limericks it may be well to point out that the tricky patterns of words contribute to the humor of each limerick.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. Before the pupils do the exercises in the section review, it will be well for the teacher to point out the definition and distinction made in the material that introduces the section study exercises the difference between humor and nonsense. This sort of distinction, though it may not be easily appreciated by all children, will help lead them toward elevated taste and the appreciation of literary differences. In directing the exercises it should be kept in mind that the delightful experience of the reading should not be impaired by too rigorous a test of the learning. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making the customary check on the achievement of goals and purposes in this section the teacher should bear in mind that the material does not permit of minute checking, and that the single purpose to be gained was that of giving further evidence that reading may be enjoyable. It is quite probable that everyone has achieved this purpose. However, the check should be made in order to make certain that pupils continue to keep their reading skills up to the level which they are capable of attaining. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will make the customary use of the book list in this section, adding with the pupils the books that may be available in local libraries.

Exercises for training in reading skills-Section Seven-Book One

SECTION EIGHT-FAVORITE LEGENDS OF THE PAST 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that legends have narrative qualities and contain character values that help pupils appreciate reading and literature. The section consists of the following selections:

Jason and the Golden Fleece (pp. 465-473) 

David and Goliath (pp. 473-476) 

King Solomon and the Bees (pp. 476-478) 

Robin Hood and the Knight (pp. 478-483) 

A Song of Sherwood (pp. 484-485) 

St. Patrick in Ireland (pp. 486-489) 

Yussouf (pp. 489-490) 

Joan of Arc (pp. 490-498) 

Storytelling (Speech) (pp. 499-504) 

William Tell and the Apple (pp. 505-511) 

Section Review (pp. 511-512)

Purposes. In presenting Section Eight the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To encourage permanent worth-while reading interests. 

3. To guide and elevate literary taste.

In presenting this section the teacher will want to point out that legends have a particular importance because they are always written about someone who, real or imagined in the past, attracted the great love, admiration, or interest of many people. Children in reading most legends will not find it difficult to see why the chief characters in them did attract these feelings on the part of the people of those times. 46

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Heroes." The section may be well commenced by a reading of the poem on page 464, which presents the idea of heroes and heroism. The border pictures may also be used to particularly good advantage because each of the small drawings shows in dramatic form a glimpse of action to be seen later in the book. 

2. A preview of the section. In her brief survey of the material in the section the teacher may follow the general technique used in presenting each of the sections, indicating points of high interest that the children will later discover for themselves. The teacher may want to indicate also that legends differ from myths in that they frequently, though not always, have grown up around either actual people or composite pictures of the deeds of actual people. This cannot be made an absolute distinction, but it will help in creating a useful working distinction.

In presenting the speech selection in this section the teacher will want to point out the connection that exists between this storytelling speech selection and the section composed of stories. If the Preparatory Note for this speech selection and the exercises following it are adequately presented by the teacher and understood by the pupils, the material of the selection itself may be easily and effectively carried out. 

3. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, adding for any groups that need special preparation such information as she may be able to supply along the lines indicated by the Preparatory Notes. No further information should be required for average groups. 

4. Use of Study Exercises. In guiding the pupils' use of the exercises the teacher will make it possible for them to gain understanding of what they have read and contribute to their competence in reading skills. 

Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

5. Awareness of goals. In keeping pupils aware of the goals and purposes of this section the teacher will point out from time to time the value of this awareness in giving direction and general meaning to the selections pupils are reading.

 The interest that children will have in most of the selections here will be almost immediate. The teacher may assist in encouraging this interest, and preserving it, by indicating that these stories are but a few of many that are similarly told of these same heroes and of other heroes whose deeds are equally interesting. 47

The speech selection in this section, "Storytelling," should be used not only in the continued development of speech skills, but also as evidence of another way in which speech competence can lead to increased enjoyment of literature. The child who possesses an adequate technique for the telling of stories possesses an ability of great value in the oral interpretation of literature. Through a familiarity with this technique he can effectively entertain and inform not only himself, but others, and bring to himself and others fine points of interest, understanding, and appreciation. 

2. To encourage permanent worth-while reading interests. The teacher will want to point out that other books containing legends are available, and bring such books to the classroom. By indicating that there are, for example, many stories about Jason, some of them just as good as the one in this book, and by reminding pupils that they have already read many stories about Robin Hood and that there are many more, the teacher will effectively encourage the children to seek these other stories and add to their knowledge of the favorite legends of the past. 

3. To guide and elevate literary taste. This purpose, which has been sought directly and indirectly in other sections, should receive added impetus here. The procedure for reaching it will be similar to that used in the section containing myths and tales. The same desire and accomplishment in good writing exists in this section. It may be pointed out, for example, that Jason's actions were just as brave and wonderful as those of the favorite exaggerated heroes of some of the children. The difference in the presentation of Jason's actions and the presentation of the actions of some of the children's heroes may be pointed out, with the stressing of the advantage that this presentation has. 

Again the teacher should be cautioned to realize that this type of appreciation and improvement is seldom rapid, and frequently unobservable, and that little is to be gained by open criticism of the type of enjoyment that children find exciting in inferior books and spectacular radio programs. The taste improvement program is slow, but can be made effective by constant attempts to substitute what is good for what is not good.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. In making the summary of this material the teacher will find the remarks in the section review on page 511 helpful. 

The subject of heroes is a popular one with children, and through this final discussion of the subject the pupils should be given an opportunity 48

to talk over their ideas about the heroes and heroines they have met in this section, as well as the heroes and heroines they would like to read about in other books that may be available. The section study exercises will help bring the pupils to a satisfactory understanding of the subject of legends and heroic tales. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher should make at this time her usual check on the achievements of the pupils to see that they have achieved the section goals and purposes. It will not be easy to judge the extent of appreciation that all pupils have for this type of reading, and in making her check the teacher should remember that progress in appreciation is not easy to measure. She will, however, be able to judge the competence of her pupils in maintaining and increasing their reading skills, take steps to eliminate deficiencies, and give added help to those who are not up to their own proper levels of reading. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will make the customary use of the book list, adding with the children such other books as she and they know are available in home, school, or community libraries. 

Exercises for training in reading skills-Section Eight-Book One

SECTION NINE-MODERN TALES OF WONDER 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that we live in an age of ideas and accomplishments really as wonderful as the imagined ages of the past that supplied so many tales of fanciful wonder. The section consists of the following selections: 

I Keep Wondering (p. 514)

Making Jules Verne's Dream Come True (pp. 515-519) 

Silver Ships (pp. 519-520) 

Bambi Encounters Man (pp. 520-524) 

The Bird of Paradise (pp. 525-529)

Modern Magic of Film and Radio (Speech) (pp. 529-532) The Little Green Orchard (p. 533) 

An Abode of Splendor (pp. 534-540) 

At the Theater (pp. 540-541)

The Bee-Man of Orn (pp. 541-550) The Christmas Guest (pp. 550-556) Section Review (pp. 557-558) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Nine the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To increase pupil understanding through vicarious experiences. 

3. To continue the systematic development of good attitudes toward reading and skills in reading.

1. A special review. It has been observable throughout this monograph that the first purpose in the presentation and teaching of every section has been to instill and develop in the pupils an interest and a joy in reading. That is one purpose also of this final section. At this time, before proceeding with the specific section, the teacher should review with the pupils this initial purpose in reading. She may briefly go back with them to preceding sections to remind them of the pleasure they have found in most of the reading they have done in this book, and to reconstruct with them some of the values they have attained from their pleasure in reading. She will want also, before the study of this final section, to make a rather thorough check on the general abilities and specific reading skills of the pupils in her class. This check will be for the definite purpose of discovering weakness, so that plans may be made specifically in this section to overcome such weakness, with the hope that at the end of this book-reading experience every child will be equipped with the skills and abilities in which he has had practice. 50

It should not be expected that any child of normal general ability will be found at this time deficient in the normal reading skills. Therefore it should not be necessary to install such a rigorous program of exercises and review material that any of the reading enjoyment for this final section will be unhappily lessened. However, if it is found that several of the pupils are markedly deficient in these skills, it will be necessary for the teacher, using the available exercises and supplementing them with exercises of her own making, to bring these pupils up to levels properly expected of them. Having made this preliminary testing, and having developed specific procedures for taking care of the results of the testing, the teacher will return to the regular procedure for the section. 

2. Use of the introductory poem-"I Keep Wondering." The poem on page 514 should be read to and with the pupils for the purpose of indicating that this section will contain much that has caused them to wonder. The small drawings in the border will be of particular value also in showing, as at the top of the page, man's conquest of the air, and as at the bottom of the page, man's study of life under the waters of the seas. 

3. A preview of the section. In making a preview of the material in this section the teacher will want to proceed as usual to make a few pertinent remarks about each selection, indicating that these modern tales of wonder are all easy to understand, that they contain great variety, that wonder itself is of many kinds, and that in this section they will read about interesting things that they may never have thought about or read about before. 

4. Use of helpful correlation. In this section are several selections that may be correlated with the subject of general science. A list of suggested study relationships appears at the end of this book division in the monograph. 

5. Use of Preparatory Notes. In introducing each specific selection the teacher will proceed as in previous selections, through the use of the Preparatory Note and any supplementary material that may be required by her particular group, to the reading of the selection itself. The speech selection will be taught as another modern tale of wonder, then for its specific contribution to speech improvement. Note that as the pupils read this selection, they are expected to find suggestions for judging values in both motion picture and radio programs. It is expected that the teacher and pupils will recognize the need to make such judgments, and that they will recognize the need to study the subject in order to find training in arriving at good judgment. 

6. Use of Study Exercises. The selection Study Exercises will, as usual, point up the specific skills and appreciations that the pupils are 51

expected to learn from the reading of any given selection. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

 In reading material of this nature most pupils will not need any specific coaching in their finding of reading pleasure. Here again, however, it will be helpful for them to see that one of the joys of reading lies in the limitless scope of reading and literature, that in this great field tremendous variety exists, and that in it are found great joys somewhere for everyone. This idea may properly be stressed, not only in the variety that exists in this particular section, but also through suggestions that many other books exist that contain selections of this sort and that pupils should get them, if and when they are interested, and continue their reading along these lines. 

In this section the speech selection makes a particularly vital contribution to the possibilities of adding to the enjoyment of literary experiences through oral interpretation. Knowledge of the technique of the motion picture and the radio brings knowledge of the most important public use of speech in our day. Such knowledge adds to the pupils' awareness of the current and constant importance of good speech, and adds as well to their individual abilities in the interpretation and presentation of thought and literature through their own speaking. Thus the joys of spoken literature are enhanced at the same time that information is given in a very practical and helpful field of present-day activity. 

2. To increase pupil understanding through vicarious experiences.

This purpose, though it has not always been mentioned specifically for each section, is one which runs almost automatically through practically all reading. It is not always necessary for it to be stressed. It appears here because now and then it should be pointedly emphasized, so that pupils may be aware of this contribution of reading. It is likely that every reader identifies himself to some extent with the characters who appear in a story that he reads. It is not always true that the pupil is consciously aware of this kind of identification. It is desirable, from time to time, to point out to him that when he reads a story in which he extends himself imaginatively into the being of the person he reads about, he is almost actually having these experiences himself. Pointing this out is one way of helping children understand that reading has for them great and tangible values, and it is this fact in turn which leads pupils to see the great use of reading, and the need to read well. 

Probably the best selection to be used in making this vicarious experience value obvious is the story "An Abode of Splendor." Many children 52

will easily see themselves in the thoughts and activities of the boy in this story. They will share with him the hopes of anticipation and the thrills of the completion of his hopes. They will share with him the new interest in the brave new world of the theater. They will share with him the affection of the family relationship that accompanied the activities of this story. 

3. To continue the systematic development of good attitudes toward reading and skills in reading. The need for reviewing and building the skills and abilities in reading has already been pointed out in the procedure for this section. It is necessary here only to suggest that the specific skills and abilities to be sought in this section be developed systematically in this section. The development of good attitudes should be particularly stressed at this time in an attempt to make certain that the child, after looking back with pleasure on the reading that he has done in this book, will look forward with equal pleasure to the reading he is to do in books to come.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. In making a summary of the material covered in this section the remarks introducing the section study exercises should be carefully read and discussed with the pupils. This brief paragraph will supply the impetus for looking back over the content and for helping the child come to conclusions about the material he has been reading. 

Then through the use of the specific section study exercises the work on this last section as such will be complete. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make the usual check on the section goals and purposes and the degree to which her pupils have mastered them. She will find it desirable, now that the end of the book has been reached, to sum up for her particular groups the skills that they have been working on throughout the book, and to ascertain the degree of success that they have had in acquiring them. 

It will be particularly necessary at this time to eliminate any serious deficiencies that the pupils indicate. They should not leave their class in reading and literature with weaknesses that will interfere with the growing confidence they have gained throughout the year in their ability to read well and enjoy reading thoroughly. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will want to supplement the book list with titles available to pupils in particular localities. She should emphasize books that contribute to an understanding of our present age of scientific wonders. 53

STRANDS OF APPRECIATION 

Attitudes of thought and feeling resulting in habits of conduct are the outgrowth of appreciation. Appreciation, itself, is based upon awareness and understanding.

To call the pupil's attention to opportunities for appreciation, and to lend cumulative emphasis to desirable attitudes and habits of conduct, the selections in Excursions in Fact and Fancy have been assembled into strands of appreciation. YOUR WORLD IN PROSE AND VERSE Introduction 

Before proceeding with the teaching of this second book in the Cultural Growth Series, the teacher will want to review the introductory material found on pages 3 to 7 in this monograph. Here will be found the statements of the basic philosophy of this course in reading and literature, and the suggested procedures for the most effective use of the program. The material in the following paragraphs supplements the information contained in the part of the general introduction called Reading for Appreciation. As examples of the cumulative growth toward literary culture throughout this series, the following titles of selections contributing to the areas of knowledge mentioned in the general introduction are listed:

Awareness of the Physical World. "A Cub Pilot's Experience,” 

"A Message to Garcia," "A Swing through Texas," "The Travel 

Bureau," "The Carolinas and the Low Country," "John Muir and 

Stickeen," "Aerobatics, Thirty Minutes," "With the Ice Patrol," "Gay- 

Neck in the Himalayas," "With Helmet and with Hose," "When the

Stars Come Out," "The Romance of the Wheel," "The Man Who 

Conquered Hunger," and "The Wonderland of the Moviemakers."

Social Awareness. "The Hunger for Self-Education,” “Our Lady of the Red Cross," "Young Fellow, My Lad," "How Abe Lincoln Paid for 

His Stockings," "The Man without a Country," "Out to Old Aunt 

Mary's," and "Abou Ben Adhem."

Awareness of the Complexities of Human Nature. "A Surprise for 

Hank Banta," "The Heyday of the Blood," "Smoky Chooses His 

Master," "Joseph and His Brethren," and "A Double Harvest."

Awareness of Moral Values. "On Trial for His Life," "The Story of the 

Good Samaritan," "The Great Stone Face," and "What the Bees Told 

Henri Fabre."

The teacher will note that the three areas of knowledge developed in the first book in the series are developed more extensively here, and that a new area of knowledge has been added. These facts make evident the opportunities pupils have through this series to progress efficiently toward the ultimate goal of literary appreciation and cultural growth. 63

YOUR WORLD IN PROSE AND VERSE SECTION ONE-THE JOY OF ACHIEVEMENT 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading of the achievements of others leads to the enjoyment of reading, to the development of right attitudes, and to the encouragement of pupils to take part vicariously in the commendable actions of others. The section consists of the following selections: 

Achievement (p. 18) 

If (pp. 19-20) 

A Man's Task (pp. 20-21)

The Hunger for Self-Education (pp. 21-27) The Right Word at the Right Time (Speech) (pp. 28-29) 

Opportunity (pp. 30-31) 

A Cub Pilot's Experience (pp. 31-37) 

A Lone Star (pp. 37-47) 

Martin (pp. 47-48) 

To Make a Prairie (p. 48)

The Scythe Song (Speech) (pp. 49-50) 

A Message to Garcia (pp. 51-54) 

Columbus (pp. 55-56) 

The Little Cares That Fretted Me (p. 56) 

A Surprise for Hank Banta (pp. 57-61)

Streamline Your Speech (Speech) (pp. 62-63) 

The Whittler of Cremona (pp. 64-68) 

For the Fun of It (pp. 69-73) 

Nevertheless (pp. 73-81) 

Section Review (pp. 82-84)

Purposes. In presenting Section One the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To develop systematically the right attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others.

It will be seen throughout this monograph that the first purpose in the teaching of each section will be to create genuine joy in reading. 64

This purpose is first always in position and in emphasis because it is assumed that no real reading benefits can accrue in the absence of real enjoyment of reading. One of the main criteria in the choice of material for this book, and every book in the series, was that of pupil-interest. 

This standard was set up for the same reason that this first purpose is consistent in this monograph—namely, that interest and enjoyment must precede the achievement of all reading skills and abilities. 

In the material that follows, the teacher will find assistance in helping pupils gain from the study of the contents of this section the chief values that the selections offer. 

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Achievement." Each of the introductory selections found in the illustrated border preceding each section in each book in this series was chosen to symbolize the content of each section. The teacher will always find this introductory selection helpful in giving the pupils an immediate and accurate understanding of the type of material that will follow in the section. 

The poem "Achievement" should be read to and with the pupils and its full meaning brought out for them. In connection with the reading of this poem the various small pictures in the borders should be discussed with the children. The teacher may point out to them that in these pictures are boys making airplanes, a boy at the wheel of a boat, a boy and a man in discussion, a bee at work on a flower, a girl and a boy thinking and dreaming, and a man working on a violin. The teacher may say to the pupils that every one of these little pictures foreshadows some action in the section. Thus through a discussion of the introductory poem and the drawings, the pupils will gain a clear picture of the type of material they are to read. 

2. A preview of the section. The purpose of the preview, a phase of procedure that will be constant throughout the book, is to take with the pupils a rapid tour of the section content with the intention of heightening their interest in reading later what here is lightly touched upon. For this reason a preview, though it may cover each selection in the section, should be brief. At this stage of the procedure no time should be taken for pupil discussion of material unless some pupil in his enthusiasm has some pertinent comments to make. If these comments help stimulate interest they are acceptable and desirable. Otherwise they should not be permitted, as they are likely to slow down a process which should be rapid.

The teacher may make such comments as will bring out the facts that the poem "If" is a famous one, built entirely around the little word if; that "A Man's Task" will help them see how important it is to use certain little daily qualities of decency; that "The Hunger for Self-Educa- 65

tion" will show them in an interesting way how a foreign boy made himself successful in this country; that "The Right Word at the Right Time" is a selection in the Speech Improvement Program that this book offers, and that they will find it helpful in improving their speech ability; that the poem "Opportunity" will show them how important it is to use what they have and make the most of it; that "A Cub Pilot's Experience” is a very funny story that they will like.

Thus the teacher will proceed through the section, saying something pertinent and enthusiastic about each of the selections in this section. 

Throughout the book she will find it particularly helpful to use the illustrations in stimulating interest. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Teachers who find it helpful to correlate the material of a reading and literature book with other curricular studies will find that in this section several selections may be put to pertinent use. For the teacher's convenience in correlating selections with other curricular studies, a list of suggested relationships appears at the end of this book division in the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. After conducting the preview with and for the pupils, the teacher will return to the beginning of the section and proceed to direct the study of the selections. Having read each selection carefully herself, she will know whether or not there are concepts in it that will prove troublesome to the pupils in her particular group. If there are such concepts, or for her pupils vocabulary difficulties that are not taken care of in the footnotes, she will want to discuss in advance these possible handicaps to successful reading. In this connection she should use the Preparatory Note for each selection, read it over with the children, and in some cases discuss it with them before they continue with their reading. These Notes are intended to supply adequate information to average groups as a background for a successful reading of the selection that follows. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises will frequently lead to helpful discussion, will take care of the normal needs for composition, and provide adequate opportunity for oral participation. They will contribute systematically to an improvement in reading skills and the formation and maintenance of good attitudes toward reading. 

Frequent group activities provide opportunity for co-operation in the classroom and an understanding of the social aspects of literature. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the teacher continues the teaching of the selections in each section she should be constantly aware of the goals and purposes that can be achieved in the section. Each selection should be 66

taught, not only for itself and its contribution, but also for its part in the pattern of the section and for its contribution to the general understanding of the basic thought of the section. It is necessary not only for the teacher to maintain this awareness, but for the pupils also to realize constantly that they are making progress toward the achievement of definite goals and purposes. This awareness is especially important in encouraging pupils to see value in their reading, and to look upon it as an activity that is not only interesting but worth while. 

The material that follows will give the teacher aid in remembering these section purposes, and suggest the methods by which they may be achieved.

 Throughout the book the teacher will understand the special significance of the word genuine as used in this purpose. Joy should not be thought of in this connection as a little thing, applicable only to the recreatory aspects of reading. Joy is considered throughout this book as something deep and lasting. It forms the basis for all true appreciation of reading. 

The teacher will note that the name of this section is "The Joy of Achievement." Yet even within this section that presents a special kind of joy she will want to point out the great variety that exists. 

In order to do this she will want to indicate that each selection offers its own kind of joy of achievement—that the poem "If" shows that people who possess and use certain qualities and attitudes can achieve certain desirable ends; that the brief paragraph "A Man's Task" lays out further requirements for achievement; that "The Hunger for Self- 

Education" shows very clearly the immense and lasting joy that Edward Bok had in his early achievements; that "The Right Word at the Right 

Time" gives further proof in different form of this same fact of Bok's joy; that "Opportunity" tells of the sincere joy possible in an unusual kind of achievement. And so the teacher will proceed, using as many selections as are pertinent in this section, to indicate the variety of joy to be found in reading and from reading. 

The fanciful poem by Emily Dickinson on page 48 is expected to contribute imaginatively to the pupils' picture of joy and achievement. 

The poem should be taught as a little contribution to the truth of joy in fancy. "The Scythe Song," in addition to being taught as a selection making its own contribution to the thought of the section, should be specifically presented as a means of discovering an interesting joy in the oral presenta- 67

tion of poetry. The teacher will find that all speech selections in the book are so clearly presented that they require no specialized knowledge for teaching procedure nor for pupil learning. Such a selection as this one contributes to joy the pupils may have in carrying out correctly and well a procedure that brings them pleasure and satisfaction. 

The kind of joy that is found in “A Message to Garcia" can be made very real to children—that few joys are so great as those which come from knowing that we have done our duty efficiently and cheerfully. 

And so the teacher may proceed through the rest of the section, stressing the values that genuine joy can provide. 

2. To develop systematically the right attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. The teacher will, of course, understand that the purposes in each section are not achieved separately, but along with others. They are presented separately here merely that they may appear more obviously. a. Reading. Probably the most lasting contribution to good attitudes toward reading can be made through encouraging pupil awareness of the values that reading has. In this connection no special phase of reading should be stressed-the child should be equally aware of the values of the pleasures of reading for enjoyment, and of the values to be had through reading for information and useful knowledge. And in addition to these two aspects of pleasure and assistance, the child should be encouraged to understand the values of reading and literature that lead to a deeper understanding of himself and the world. This point should not be stressed unduly at this time, but helpful suggestions of this truth should be made occasionally. But attitudes alone are not enough to create good reading. The specific reading abilities of children are based upon more fundamental learning. 

It cannot be assumed that all pupils reach this year in their reading experience with their basic skills well mastered. Therefore it must follow that many of them have not yet been able to find the highest appreciation of reading, because they have not yet mastered adequately the means of getting the most out of their reading. Most good readers like to read. 

Part of the teacher's effort should be constantly directed toward helping those who do not like reading because they do not read well, to acquire and practice the skills that will permit them to read better. 

The exercise program in this book will serve as a basis for pupil work leading to specific reading skills. If the teacher feels that more exercises are required for the improvement of certain pupils in her class, she may construct and administer exercises similar to those which appear in the book. b. Speaking. It has been pointed out that pupils, in order to appreci- 68

ate what they read, must find in it some value. It is particularly easy to help them find in the speech material the kind of values that they see most readily. The speech selection "Streamline Your Speech," for example, is concerned definitely and entirely with material that adds to the reader's immediate practical improvement. This material is specifically concerned with bringing about a type of speech improvement that leads to better opportunities in job finding and job keeping in these times. 

What was said concerning "The Scythe Song" is true of "Streamline

Your Speech" and of all the speech selections that follow-that it must be approached by the teacher as a selection dictated by good sense, unaccompanied by frightening specialized techniques. Teachers and pupils alike should approach this speech material with complete assurance that they can find its value with interest and without difficulty. It should be understood that in addition to the use of the speech improvement material as a source of instruction in practical speech techniques, it should be used also to increase appreciation of literature through oral interpretation. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. The achievement of this purpose is of high importance. 

It is essential for real appreciation that the reader participate as fully as possible in the activities and thoughts of the characters about whom he is reading. When there is no participation of this sort in material that should lend itself to such participation, the reader is gaining from his reading only a small portion of the gains that should be his. 

Participation in vicarious experience is for readers an extension of knowledge and activity. This is the aspect of literary appreciation which permits everyone who reads to take part imaginatively in the activities of a larger world than the limited one in which he actually lives. Thus this participation is essential not only to specific appreciation of a certain selection-it also adds materially to the literary and cultural growth of the participant.

In this first section of the book there are many selections in which pupils will find it easy to participate vicariously. Two such selections will be used here as examples. In the story "A Lone Star," one of the chief characters is a girl. The adventures of this girl in the exciting early days of Texas are full of adventure and action of the sort that every reader would like to take part in as he reads. The fact that the chief character in this story is a girl makes it particularly appealing to girls, and particularly appropriate as a basis for encouragement of vicarious participation. 

In Mark Twain's story, “A Cub Pilot's Experience," the chief character is a boy. Here the boy is doing many things that most boys would be thrilled to do. Therefore this story is a good one to use in encouraging vicarious participation. 69

The teacher should keep in mind that this purpose is not a difficult one for the pupils to achieve to some extent, and that her chief emphasis and assistance should be directed to making it possible for them to participate to a fuller extent in the actions and thoughts of people they read about in stories.

Summary Activities 1. Section review. The section review has several purposes. The chief and most immediate purpose is to bring to a satisfying conclusion the ideas and materials that have been studied in the section. The teacher should remind the children at this time that the name of the section, "The Joy of Achievement," serves now as a guide for them on their quick return journey through the material. This review should be as brief as the preview was. It should be conducted with the purpose of showing how the various selections contributed to the meaning of the section.

In using the section review exercises the teacher will find opportunity for further stressing the basic skills and abilities toward which she has been leading the pupils throughout the section. Use should be made also of these exercises in encouraging the children to come to their own conclusions, and to express their opinions on the extent and value of what they have learned in basic skills and reading abilities. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher has been constantly aware, and will continue throughout the book to be aware, that certain goals and purposes are basic. At this time she will want to make sure that the pupils, in addition to the obvious goals such as general efficiency in reading, have made good progress toward the goals of appreciation of literature. In this connection she will want to check with them to evoke as many as possible evidences of the ways in which their experiences and capacities for appreciation have been widened and deepened. She may, in making this check, encourage the children to discuss a few things that they know about now that they did not know about before reading this section. Such discussions easily become general and lead to an expression of some sort by most of the pupils in the class. 

These expressions should be accepted without criticism and the teacher may, when the expressions are inadequate, suggest ways in which they may be made broader, and lead the child to an awareness that he has learned more things than he has mentioned. 

3. Use of book list. The book list at the end of each section is intended to encourage pupils to extend their reading beyond the scope of their text in literature. The teacher will doubtless find it possible to tell 70

the children which of the books on this list are available to them through local library facilities, and to add to this list such books as she may know of that contribute to the thought of this section. It is expected that the book list will be used in this manner in connection with each section in this book.

SECTION TWO-ANIMAL FRIENDS 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading in a congenial field of subject matter will make it easy for pupils to find genuine joy in their reading and to increase their ability to identify themselves with story characters. The section consists of the following selections: 

Will James Says: (p. 86)

A Boy and His Dog (pp. 87-88) 

On Trial for His Life (pp. 88-96) A Monkey in Manhattan (pp. 96-100) 

Smoky Chooses His Master (pp. 100-106) 

Riding Song (p. 106)

Can Animals Speak (Speech) (pp. 107-108) 

The Thoroughbred (p. 109) 

The Elephant Remembers (pp. 110-125)

The Mysterious Cat (Speech) (pp. 126-127) 

John Muir and Stickeen (pp. 128-132) 

My Dog (p. 133) 

Gay-Neck in the Himalayas (pp. 134-141)

From The Pelican Chorus (Speech) (pp. 142-143) Section Review (pp. 144-146) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Two the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others.

1. Use of the introductory selection-"Will James Says." Few people have felt deeper affection for animals than has Will James. In using this introductory selection to indicate the type of content in this section, the teacher will want to tell the children something about Will James. It would be well in this connection to turn to page 100 in the text and read with the children the letter that Will James wrote for the readers of this book. In the letter they will find material and the manner of expressing it which will make the author very real to them. The experience of reading his letter addressed to them will make the pupils feel more keenly that what he says in the introductory selection is the truth from a man who feels keenly about the subject. 

The pupils will find it interesting and helpful also to identify the animals and the bird found in the border pictures. All these animals are subjects of stories to be read in this section. 72

2. A preview of the section. The purpose of the preview will be the same as that in the first section and each of the sections that follow-to proceed briefly with the children through the section, pointing out to them certain highlights of interest in the selections that they will soon read for their pleasure and information. 

Of "A Boy and His Dog," for example, the teacher may point out that Edgar Guest is a friendly poet whom many people like, and that he understands some of the common experiences that people find enjoyable; that the story "On Trial for His Life" tells about a poor boy and his love for his dog, and that it has some very exciting action in it; that “A 

Monkey in Manhattan" is an unusual true story about a monkey that the wife of a famous explorer kept for a while in her New York home; that "Smoky Chooses His Master" is a story by Will James, whom they have already met as a lover of animals; that the "Riding Song" will help them see what joys some people have in riding horses; that the speech selection "Can Animals Speak?" is closely connected with the other selections in the section, and that in addition it will give them some valuable information on how to improve their speech. And so the teacher will proceed through the section, mentioning briefly points that she knows will catch the interest of the pupils. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section there are fewer opportunities for correlating material with other subject studies, but some of them are suitable for this purpose. These are listed in the general correlation grouping at the end of the second book division in this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The use of the Preparatory Notes should be consistent throughout the book along the lines suggested in Section One. From time to time it may be desirable that the teacher meet the needs of her particular group by adding information of her own to the preparation in the text. In supplementing the Preparatory Notes she may want to explain in advance some concepts in the selection that she thinks her pupils may find difficulty in understanding. It is unlikely that any such difficulties will be present for any group, since the Preparatory Notes and the footnotes have been designed as adequate preparation for average classes.

In connection with the Preparatory Note, the occasional autobiographical letters should be read as part of the preparation. These letters were written by the authors exclusively for the readers of these books in an attempt to tell something about themselves that would be interesting to pupils and help them appreciate the selection. The letter on page 

96, for example, by Mrs. Akeley, gives the background for her writing about the little monkey. The letter on page 110 gives the background 73

for Marshall's India story. The children can be helped to a feeling of intimacy with authors when they realize that these letters were written specifically to and for them. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. Throughout this section and the book, the teacher should remember that the purposes of the exercises are in general two-fold-to help the pupils gain an over-all understanding of each selection, and to help them remember such details as will add to their comprehension and help them improve their reading skills. The exercises should be done purposefully to meet these ends, and not laboriously merely to write unthinking answers. Page references to exercises contributing to training in basic reading and speaking skills appear at the end of this section in the monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. Throughout the section the teacher should remain actively aware of the general goals and purposes toward which she is guiding the pupils. She should encourage the pupils also to share this awareness and realize the purposes for which they are reading and studying. The general purpose is always one of improving reading skills that lead to deeper understanding of the material of reading and literature. The specific goals and purposes are always those listed, as below, under specific section procedure. It is toward the accomplishment of these specific goals that the teacher will direct her work within each section.

 This section lends itself particularly well to the enjoyment of reading. Pupils will find all these selections keenly interesting. The teacher will need, in guiding pupils toward this purpose, merely to assist them in finding the real values of the poems and stories, and to make it possible for them to see that there is in nearly every one of the selections an opportunity for more than mere surface joy.

For example, in the story "On Trial for His Life," the teacher may point out that although this is an interesting and even exciting story, just for the story's sake, it also contains ideas that contribute to enjoyment in the understanding of character and appreciation of the fine feelings that people may have. She may, of course, point out also that we find enjoyment in the triumph of the right and the defeat of the wrong. This story presents a clear-cut conflict involving moral values, and can be of great use to the teacher in pointing out this battle between right and wrong. This kind of enjoyment—in the satisfactory solution of an understandable problem-can be made to add materially to the pupils' basic 74

ability to appreciate the larger aspects of the enjoyment of literature. 

The speech selections in this section contribute also to genuine joy in reading. The first selection, "Can Animals Speak?", presents to pupils an interesting idea which is developed briefly but effectively. It will lead pupils to an interested new field of observation for themselves. Furthermore, on the basis of material that they have read concerning animals, they will want to continue the selection to get from it the specific instruction that it offers to them in the improvement of their speech work. 

It should not be forgotten that the enjoyment of work well done is one of the greatest pleasures to be gained from reading. 

The second speech selection, "The Mysterious Cat," will be more obviously enjoyable. Here again the pupils must be encouraged to take pride in doing a job well. When this poem is spoken well chorally, it will give joy to those who present it and to those who listen to it. This same type of enjoyment will be found in the third speech selection, "From the Pelican Chorus." In presenting such material the teacher should be constantly aware of the fact that added joy and understanding of literature may be acquired through oral interpretation. 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. Just as this section lends itself well to the achievement of the first purpose, so it does to the achievement of the second purpose. 

In practically every selection in this section, children will find it possible to identify themselves with the characters and real people presented. 

Again, just as their joy in this section is practically automatic, so their identification with characters will be. Also, just as the teacher brought out the deeper values of enjoyment in helping the children achieve the first purpose, so now she will want to help them be constantly aware of the values of identification as they go through this section. It should be remembered always that all these appreciations and abilities which are encouraged easily should also be encouraged deeply. This type of encouragement goes beneath the surface of readily achieved ends and builds up a fundamental background of lasting abilities and appreciations. It is to these lasting abilities and appreciations that the teacher, through encouraging constant awareness on the part of the pupils, can make her most significant contribution. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. In this section, as in the first, and as it will be throughout the book, the teacher will use the section review to help the pupils reach certain understanding conclusions about the material that they have read in the section. The paragraph above the exercises will be 75

helpful in providing a brief review for the pupils, and reminding them of some of the subjects about which they have been reading. The exercises themselves will assist in this general review, helping the child go back over what he has learned, and helping him to go forward with more confidence to what he has yet to learn. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. At this time, in conjunction with and following the section review, the teacher will want to make certain that all her pupils have reached, within their capacity, the goals and purposes set up for this section. If there are pupils in any particular class who have not within this section contributed adequately to their improvement in reading and speaking skills, the teacher may find it necessary to review with them certain aspects of their reading that will bolster these skills; or it may be that she will find it necessary to improvise for such pupils special exercises that will help bring them up to their proper level. The main necessity at this time is to see that no large numbers of pupils are accumulating reading deficiencies which will continue to grow and finally cause pupils to fail in their reading and speaking program. This failure for normal pupils need never occur if the teacher takes care that deficiencies do not accumulate. 

3. Use of book list. Keeping in mind that an anthology does not complete reading, but acts rather as a stimulus to further reading, the teacher will call attention to the book list at the end of each section and recommend books that are available to her pupils. Pupils and the teacher will, particularly for this section, be able to add other titles and descriptive sentences to this list of books. Such an addition is in itself a splendid exercise, permitting as it does co-operation among the pupils and the teacher toward the improvement of something designed for all.
