SECTION THREE-THE SPIRIT OF CITIZENSHIP AND SERVICE 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading matter centered around the ideas of citizenship and service will lead children to find joy in reading material that is worth while, and will promote among them an understanding of peoples' duties and privileges and of the importance of good character. The section consists of the following selections:

Four Things (p. 148) 

The American's Creed (pp. 149-150) 

Pierre and Marie Curie (pp. 150-153) 

Sam Pasco and Napoleon (pp. 153-154) 

Young Fellow, My Lad (pp. 154-155)

The Story of the Good Samaritan (pp. 155-157) 

Two 'Mericana Men (pp. 157-158) 

America for Me (pp. 158-159)

How Abe Lincoln Paid for His Stockings (pp. 159-172) By the Dawn's Early Light (Speech) (pp. 173-176) The Star-Spangled Banner (pp. 177-178) The Name of Old Glory (pp. 179-180) 

The Man without a Country (pp.

181-200) 

Clara Barton-“Daughter of Destiny" (Speech) (pp. 201-202) 

Abou Ben Adhem (p. 203)

Our Lady of the Red Cross (pp. 204-212) 

Work (p. 213) 

Makers of the Flag (pp. 214-215) 

For Others (p. 216)

Section Review (pp. 216-218) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Three the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading habits and interests. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 77

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Four Things." In suggesting the tone and general feel of the subject matter of this section, the teacher will as usual find the introductory selection and the border pictures of value. She will want to read the poem "Four Things" to and with the pupils, to discuss with them the various ideas that the poem contains, and to indicate that in this section the pupils will find selections which are in line with the thoughts of this introductory poem. 

2. A preview of the section. Before making a preview of the section with the pupils, the teacher will want to indicate that this entire section, its whole content and spirit, differs from the two sections that they have already read in this book. Here, she will point out to them, are selections that will appeal to their appreciation of what it means to be citizens of our country, and what it means to serve others. She will indicate generally that this section is no less interesting than the others, but that the interest they will find will be of a different sort. 

She may point out briefly concerning "The American's Creed” that they are perhaps already familiar with it, but that their later discussion of it here will help them find a deeper appreciation of it; that the story "Pierre and Marie Curie" will introduce them to two wonderful workers in science; that "Sam Pasco and Napoleon" is a poem that shows how an unknown man can be as important as a great one; that "Young 

Fellow, My Lad" tells of service to one's country; that "The Story of the Good Samaritan" is also a story of service, in which Jesus played a part; that "Two 'Mericana Men" is a humorous poem which nevertheless contributes to the section meaning; that "America for Me” is a lovely and patriotic expression of the importance of love for one's country; that "How Abe Lincoln Paid for His Stockings" is a fascinating tale of Lincoln as a lawyer; that "By the Dawn's Early Light" is a speech selection they will like to read aloud because it tells about the song that is now our national anthem. And so the teacher may proceed through the section, arousing the pupils' interest and laying out for them a pattern for their reading that will contribute meaning to the spirit of citizenship and service. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section all but two or three selections make definite and worth-while contributions to the correlated study of other curricular subjects. These selections will appear in the general correlation list at the end of the second book division in this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. Before asking the pupils to proceed with the reading of any selection, the teacher should make certain that 78

they have read and understood the background material presented in the Preparatory Notes. Frequently in connection with establishing background facts, the illustrations will prove of value. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The exercises in this section, as in all sections, are devised to make certain that the pupils who do them successfully will gain practice in reading and speaking skills, and acquire increased ability to appreciate and comprehend what they read. References are made at the end of this section to the exercises that contribute to the increase of reading skills and appreciation abilities sought. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher should remember that awareness is important in the learning process in order to assist in making real understanding take the place of rote learning. This awareness also contributes to the very important need of making reading purposeful—of helping pupils constantly realize that reading has real value, particularly when it is a part of a pattern of thought. 

 Creating joy in reading in this section will be to a large extent encouraging the kind of enjoyment that comes in sharing the enthusiasm of others for acts of good citizenship and service. This is the kind of joy that children can be encouraged to appreciate, and the kind of joy that will help them to understand the real values of reading and literature. Thus again the teacher can help the pupils realize that joy is not an empty thing, not a surface quality which, taken lightly, is held for a moment and then dropped. The kind of joy that is to be encouraged here and in all the sections of this book is a deeper joy than that. Children are quick to realize that one of the greatest joys is in doing for others acts of kindness that provide pleasure for both the giver and the receiver. This is a kind of joy in line with the previous training, in school and out of school, of most pupils. 

Part of the joy of reading in this section will come through the group oral presentation of the two speech selections, "By the Dawn's Early 

Light” and “Clara Barton-Daughter of Destiny." Both these speech selections contribute to the pupils' general absorption of the section basic thought. More than that, they provide opportunity for active participation in the presentation of material that contributes to this thought. The teacher should take every opportunity to make use of the added values to be found in the oral interpretation of literature, whether in the speech improvement material, or with other suitable selections. Through taking part in these two presentations, pupils can be helped to feel a closer tie with the development of the theme that runs through the section. 79

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading habits and interests. 

This purpose is in line with much of the thinking that is inherent in the achievement of the first purpose above. In the process of reading improvement the first step must be an opportunity for some kind of enjoyment. Also in this process must come the realization on the part of the teacher that she must cultivate worth-while reading habits, and on the part of the pupils that they must acquire such reading habits. Here again the matter of literary values comes in. Some children will, of course, be almost automatically good readers, but the majority of children require training in reading, not only in the acquisition of skills but in the acknowledgment and certainty that all good reading now, leads to the formation of worth-while reading habits that will bring them information, pleasure, and success in the future. 

In making a persistent attempt to bring about these worth-while habits in pupils, the teacher will want to provide frequent opportunities for helping them see that what they gain from their reading is worth while. Valuable in this connection are discussions of facts they have learned, and ideas they have gained, in their current reading. Reports from pupils which give them an opportunity to indicate to others what they have learned are of value. The exercises following the selections in this section will help make it possible for the teacher to permit this type of discussion and to bring about this kind of pupil participation. In general it is enough to say that if pupils are constantly reminded of the values that they seek and find in reading, they will cultivate permanent worth-while reading habits and interests with assurance that their efforts will be rewarded. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. The connection between the type of material in this section and this third purpose is obvious. It can be made equally obvious to pupils. In regard to the development of character, for example, the story about the Curies may be used. The pupils, having read it once for an over-all understanding, may go through it again to estimate the character development of these two people. In making his character estimates the pupil may ask such questions as these: (1) What joy did the Curies find in their work? (2) What evidence is there that the Curies did not care for fame? (3) What evidence is there that the Curies were not jealous of each other? (4) What evidence is there to show that the Curies thought more of giving their discovery to the world than in keeping it for their own benefit?

In connection with the story about Abe Lincoln, the pupils, in discussing character, may consider the kind of obligation that Abraham Lincoln felt for the woman who had once done him a kindness; they W1 th ab ce st

th 80

will consider other similar facts about Lincoln brought out in this story that help to show the character he possessed. The material of the story about Clara Barton contains much also to contribute to the pupils' conception of good character.

In regard to the development of social understanding these same stories may be used to advantage, as others may. "The Story of the 

Good Samaritan," for example, encourages social understanding on the basis of one's need, because he lives in a social world, to contribute to the welfare of others. Another phase of good social understanding can well be developed through a study of "The Man without a Country." 

Discussions may be had concerning the subject of why it was so important to punish severely one who spoke ill of his country, of why it was natural that this man should finally change his mind and deeply regret that he had so spoken, and of why it was understandable that in the end he should be forgiven and accepted, by those who knew him, as a man of loyal beliefs. The selection "Makers of the Flag" can bring very closely to the pupils' minds the contribution that everyone makes to his country by being a loyal part of it. The poem "For Others" brings out well also the thought that people should not live merely for themselves and their own interests.

In achieving this purpose pupils will appreciate the power of reading and literature to help them understand people and the world. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The purpose of the section review is as usual to assemble for all the pupils the basic thoughts of the section. Particularly in this section it is desirable that the pupils read with care and understanding the three paragraphs preceding the actual section study exercises. The teacher will want to direct discussions concerning the various thoughts which appear here as summaries of the thoughts more fully developed in the preceding selections. There are ideas here which will appeal to the children and help them see the everyday values that reading of this kind has for them. It will help them also to take their part in a world which is bigger than they are, and to feel that though their part may be a small one it is the combination of small contributions that makes their country and their world great. 

The actual exercises taken up after these general discussions will serve to crystallize the thoughts that have been expressed by the pupils and the teacher, and to form the final impression for them of the ideas that they and others have expressed and thought and studied in this section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. If the teacher knows that some pupils have lost sight of the section goals and purposes, or that 81

they have not acquired or maintained sufficient competence in their reading skills, she will want at this time to help them find the knowledge or skills that they lack. This final check is for the purpose of making certain that pupils are not piling up difficulties which will impede their progress in good reading and good speaking. 

3. Use of book list. The book list may be used, as in previous sections, as the basis for further reading in books that are available locally. 

Pupils and the teacher may add titles to this list, and write comments to indicate the nature of the material.

SECTION FOUR-BOOK COMPANIONS 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that literature brings to readers new friends, and new companions, and a deeper appreciation of living and thinking. The section consists of the following selections: 

A Real Book (p. 220) 

Velvet Coat (pp. 221-231)

What I Heard in the Apple Barrel (pp. 231-236) Word Companions (Speech) (pp. 237-238) 

The Courtship of Miles Standish (pp. 239-269)

The Great Stone Face (pp. 269-282) 

"His Words Had Power" (Speech) (pp. 283-286)

The Song of Our Syrian Guest (pp. 287-292) 

Joseph and His Brethren (pp. 293-300) 

A Song about Myself (pp. 300-302)

Section Review (pp. 302-304) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Four the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste.

1. Use of the introductory selection "A Real Book." In reading the sentences by Morley to and with the pupils, the teacher will want to emphasize first of all the difference between a book as a mere package, and a book as a container of the qualities Morley says it may have. In establishing this emphasis, the teacher will find it easy and helpful to remind the pupils that in their book so far they have found only poems and stories that make up what Mr. Morley would call a real book.

She will want also to make use of the border pictures, suggesting to the pupils that each of the small pictures has a reference to the material that they will read. And she may want to discuss with them why it is particularly appropriate in a section of this kind to have on each side of the top border little pictures showing a desk, a reading lamp, and writing material. 

2. A preview of the section. Before making the actual preview with the pupils, the teacher may point out the significance of the title of this section, and indicate her belief that in this section all readers will find new friends and companions that they will meet with pleasure, and gladly and easily remember. She may want to discuss with the pupils 83

the importance of having companions, and the desirability of finding them, not only in real life, but in books. She may want to discuss with them such book companions as they may already be familiar with, such as King Arthur, Sir Lancelot, Robin Hood, and others she knows most of her pupils remember happily. 

Then in conducting the pupils briefly through the material of this section, she will want to indicate that "Velvet Coat" is a biographical article about Robert Louis Stevenson, whom they may have heard of before as a writer of wonderful tales for children; that the illustration between pages 224 and 225 will help them appreciate one of the scenes described in the article about Stevenson; that the story "What I Heard in the Apple Barrel" is part of the longer story, Treasure Island, that young readers have for many years considered one of the best stories they ever read; that "Word Companions" is a speech selection which will interest them, and also help improve their speech work; that “The 

Courtship of Miles Standish" gives an interesting picture of the background of the early days of the Plymouth colony, and tells a tale of love and warfare; that "The Great Stone Face" is a story about a quiet and honest man who won a great honor.

And so the teacher will proceed through the section, indicating with enthusiasm the sorts of stories and poems that the children will read in this section, and giving as she goes along suggestions about the section purposes and goals. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Not many of the selections in this section can profitably be used in correlation with other subject studies. 

Those that may be so used are listed at the end of this second book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes: The teacher will continue to use the

Preparatory Notes as introductions to specific selections, supplementing them as she sees fit for any particular group. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises will be used as they have been used in previous sections to insure understanding and contribute to practice in reading skills. References are made at the end of this section to the exercises that contribute to the increase of reading skills and appreciation abilities. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the teacher proceeds in her direction of pupil study, she should remember the special purposes for which the section is being studied, and she should help the pupils remain aware of these goals and purposes. It is well for the teacher to remember, and help the pupils remember, that many goals and purposes are constant throughout all sections, though they are not mentioned. 84

The special kind of joy to which the material of this section contributes is the joy of finding friends in literature-book companions. This can be made a very real joy to pupils. As has been pointed out before in this section, pupils may be reminded of the friends they have already found in books, and helped to see how important these friendships can be to them. Much time can profitably be spent discussing the joy that pupils have had in previous book companions and literary friends. Every child will have something to offer in a discussion of this kind. Every child should be encouraged to be on the lookout for further book companions. 

The teacher should continue to encourage the children to find genuine joy in their speech selections. In "Word Companions" the children should be helped to see how the title of the selection fits in with the section title and how the material itself, through its use of the names of people they have read about, follows the interests they are now developing in this section. They should be led also to find joy in improvement of their speech abilities, and to realize that when they learn something new they are building up enjoyment for the future as well as for the present. Study of the power of words is a very practical means to various ends that pupils can readily appreciate. 

The speech selection "His Words Had Power" may serve, in general, as an opportunity to help the children realize the joy that may be had in approaching, from new directions, a story they have enjoyed reading. 

This selection is closely connected with the story "The Great Stone Face." All the speech material in the selection is based upon facts and interpretations coming from the story previously read. This selection may serve also to indicate to the pupils how pleasure may be had in the thought that no story read once can give up all its enjoyable possibilities —that sometimes further thinking about a story brings from it added pleasure. This procedure provides a valuable lesson in the contributions that can be made through the oral interpretation of literature. 

In addition to these joys that come through the connecting of the speech material with the other material read, is the specific speech training that the teacher must be sure the pupils are gaining from these selections and all speech selections in the book. 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste. If there are some children whose reading habits in the past have made it possible for them to find reading friends only in the wild tales of their unguided reading, the teacher should be careful not to dash these temporary friends too fiercely to the ground. The fact that these children have such friends, however 85

imperfect they may be, shows that they are susceptible to the basic thought that friends may be found through reading. It is then up to the teacher to guide such pupils to an appreciation of better friends to be found elsewhere.

Guiding and elevating literary taste is not an easy or brief task. The process frequently has no observable results, but the teacher may feel certain that through her gradual ministrations along these lines the pupils' tastes will be improved. She will realize that for many pupils a transfer from the inferior to the good will not be easy, and that some pupils may for a time resent any encroachment that new, and in their minds less virile friends, may make upon those friends they have made through pulp paper books and the radio. 

An approach to the subject of guidance and elevation of literary taste can be made through an appeal to pupils to read only what has value for them. It can be pointed out that the only attraction some reading has is immediate surface pleasure. Pupils may be reminded that throughout this book they have learned that genuine enjoyment has real depth, that it contributes to the improvement of their pleasure, their personality, and their character. A pleasant but pointed contrasting of these contributions with those made through less desirable reading will help pupils realize the difference between what has value and what has not.

Another approach to the subject can be made through stressing the fact that most of the authors in this section are people who have spent their entire adult life and even part of their childhood in the work of becoming good writers. In this connection the study of the biographical notes will be helpful. It will be noted, for example, that Katherine 

Cather was interested even as a child in reading and in writing, that she was a teacher, a newspaper writer, and is the author of many biographies and books for young children; that Christopher Morley has been from his youth a student of literature and a writer; that Stevenson even as a child was a great reader, and became as a youth a student of writing; that Longfellow was a teacher of English, a lifelong student of writing and literature; that Hawthorne was one of our greatest American writers; that John Keats was one of the finest of English poets; and that the 

Bible in this translation is considered one of the most beautifully written books the world has ever seen. By stressing the background of these writers, and indicating the difference between them and others who write for the crude purpose of exciting readers, the teacher will impress pupils with the fact that writing for publication is a serious and honorable job, and that those who do it best do it as a life work, putting all their best efforts into attempts to do it well. 86

These and other methods the teacher may provide will help pupils elevate their literary taste, though results may not be immediate or obvious.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The prefatory material above the section study exercises will help pupils form conclusions regarding all the material and teaching that they have experienced in this section. It is particularly desirable in this section that pupils read this material with special care, and that the teacher discuss with them all the points that are made here. 

In directing the use of the section study exercises, the teacher will continue to stress the point that the exercises are to serve the purpose of drawing together all the various aspects of learning that have been gained through the study of this section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will want to make sure at this time that all her pupils have made adequate progress toward the mastery of the section goals and purposes. She will want to make sure that none of her pupils are accumulating general or specific inadequacies which will cause reading trouble later. For any who may be deficient at this time the teacher will devise special means of review, and further work to bring them up to their proper levels. 

3. Use of book list. As in previous sections it is recommended that the teacher indicate to the children books from this list that are available, and to add with the children the titles and descriptions of other books that they may know are available in school or community libraries.

SECTION FIVE-SIDE LIGHTS ON EVERYDAY LIFE 

The basic thought contributed by the study and content of this section will be that reading about a variety of activities that bear upon everyday existence will lead to an increased appreciation of the social and personal values of reading and literature. The section consists of the 

Happy Thought (p. 306) 

Who Walks the World with Soul Awake (p. 307)

The Wonderland of the Moviemakers (pp. 307-311)

The Common Street (p. 357) The Railway Train (p. 358) 

Section Review (pp. 358-360)

Purposes. In presenting Section Five the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 88

1. Use of the introductory couplet "Happy Thought." The teacher will make the customary use of the introductory selection, reading it with and to the pupils and developing the thought contained in the two lines. She will want to present to them and stimulate in them thoughts more general and more far-ranging than those they have had in the past in connection with this familiar couplet. The lines should be discussed in connection with the section title "Side Lights on Everyday Life." So also should the border pictures be used, to point out the variety of everyday occurrences that are depicted in them. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will make the usual preview, conducting the children on a brief but complete tour of the selections in this section. As she proceeds the teacher will keep the children aware of the section title and all that the title implies, indicating to them in what ways the selections contribute meaning to the section theme.

She will want to point out that the poem "Who Walks the World with Soul Awake" invites them to be always aware of all there is in life that is beautiful and interesting; that the "Wonderland of the Moviemakers" is an article by a man who has made a special study of how motion pictures are made in the "movie capital of the world," Hollywood; that "The Banks of the Sacramento" and "The Old Chisholm Trail" are poems that have been arranged for pupils to speak chorally as part of their speech training; that "The Travel Bureau" is a little poem with a surprise ending that they will like; that "The Heyday of the Blood" is an unusual story of everyday heroism that will help them understand how common people can make themselves heroic. 

And thus the teacher will proceed through the section, pointing up each selection with enthusiasm in preparation for later thorough reading. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. There are in this section several selections that may helpfully be correlated with other studies. These selections are listed at the end of this second book division in the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the same use of the Preparatory Notes that she has made in previous sections, remembering that the occasional autobiographical letters can be used in this connection to great advantage. In preparing for the choral presentation of the poems, in connection with the speech work, the teacher should remember to point out that choral presentations help readers find greater pleasure in poetry, and that there is nothing difficult about the procedure if they know in advance how it should be carried out. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises will play the same 

89

role that they have had in previous sections, namely, to assist in making certain that pupils comprehend the main ideas, remember facts with fair accuracy, and acquire skill in appreciation. References are made at the end of this section to the exercises that contribute to an increase in reading skills and appreciation abilities sought. 

6. Awareness of goals. Throughout the study of the section, the teacher should occasionally remind the pupils that their reading is directed toward certain specific goals which they should keep in mind so that their work may have definite purposes. 

1. To create.genuine joy in reading. The aspect of joy to be stressed in this section is that joy lies all about us, and that it is necessary only for us to be aware of many things to enjoy many things. As before, joy should be stressed as a quality with depth and value, and not as a matter of mere immediate surface pleasure. 

In discussing "The Heyday of the Blood," for instance, the teacher will indicate, and pupils will be quick to'realize, that this story is one of immediate and compelling interest; but that over and above the enjoyment it provides in the mere narrative, it contains a definite and worthwhile thought, namely, that it is well to "live while you live and then die and be done with it." She may indicate that the article, “Aerobatics, Thirty Minutes," is on the surface a thrilling account of air action, but that basically it is a factual and serious account of one of the means by which aviators are trained to take their part in the all-important task of protecting our country. Other specific selections will serve this same purpose of showing that joy is more than a surface pleasure. 

The variety of joy, and the fact that we have only to look about us to find it, may be stressed through the teacher's pointing out the many different kinds of selections in this section that contribute to the side lights of everyday life and appeal to the reader's capacity for enjoyment. She may list, for example, such subjects as these that are touched upon here: the motion picture, travel, going to fairs, visiting loved relatives, enjoying home, airplanes, making the most of what we have, getting the best out of our voices, enjoying word pictures, enjoying information. Further discussions of the variety of enjoyment possible in reading may be encouraged on the basis of other selections read in this book, and other stories and poems read elsewhere. Pupils will be quick to join in such a discussion, and though they may not remember precise titles or authors, nearly all of them will have examples of reading that has given them varied enjoyment. 90

2. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. This purpose, though it is not always specifically listed, is one that appears at least by implication in every section procedure. It is a goal that the teacher is constantly striving to help the pupils reach. However, from time to time it is well to devote specific and concentrated work to this purpose. a. Reading. Pupils who read well, and whose past reading experiences have been happy, present no problems in the development of right attitudes. It is for those whose attitudes are not right that work along this line is necessary. It is quite probable that the teacher will need merely to point out that those things are enjoyed most which are approached with the expectation of pleasure, and that toward their reading pupils should have a feeling of assurance that their reading will bring them information, enjoyment, or both. To make pupils aware of the need for continued and good attitudes is probably enough for the average reading group, because for most of them the purpose may be superfluous, and therefore undesirable, as a subject for lengthy discussion. 

By bringing the matter up from time to time, however, the teacher can discover those whose attitudes may not be entirely good. To any who are found deficient in this regard, the teacher will need to offer additional help.

The subject as it regards habits of reading is different. Even good readers frequently need improvement in their actual reading habits. In this connection the teacher may encourage a discussion led by those who are good readers as to the habits which lead to the best reading. 

She may encourage the pupils to speak of such aspects of good reading habits as the ability to concentrate, the ability to get at the real meaning of whatever is read, the ability to absorb details with sufficient retaining power to use them if necessary, and the habit, which is also partly attitude, of approaching something to be read with the feeling that something worth while can be gained from it.

After the discussion is ended, the teacher may want to sum up what the pupils have said in this connection, and then to indicate certain means whereby some of these mentioned habits may be gained by those who need them most. Regarding concentration, for example, she may point out that it means removing all forms of interruption while one reads of absorbing oneself in reading to such an extent that nothing else is thought of, nothing else is looked at, nothing else is done for the time that one reads. This habit is very difficult for many pupils to gain, and it is one which the teacher will need to spend much time on, with confidence that eventually gains will be made. b. Speaking. General techniques as mentioned in connection with 91

reading above apply here equally. However, special techniques are of value in improving attitudes and habits of speaking. Through the use of the Speech Improvement Program in these books, and through observation of her pupils' work in the speech selections and their normal speech, the teacher has by this time in the school year a thorough understanding of the speech problems of her pupils. Some of them may be deficient in their attitudes and habits of speech. At this time it will be desirable for the teacher to concentrate with these pupils upon specific speech instruction.

With a knowledge of the types of difficulties she is to help her pupils overcome, she will return to the preface of the text itself, page 7, Program for Speech Improvement, and re-read and re-absorb all the speech material given there. It may be that special attention should be paid at this time to the phonetic test chart which appears on pages 10 and 11 of the text. Possibly some of the children are still having difficulty in pronouncing the actual letter sounds. If so, the teacher should give them special practice in the lists that appear on pages 10 and 11 of the text preface.

To supplement this kind of specific work, it may be desirable at this time for the teacher to conduct all pupils on a review of the speech selections that call for pupil participation. Those pupils who enjoyed and did this work well the first time will not find it tedious in review now. They will do it better and find pleasure in doing it better. The main contribution of this activity will be to those who in the first instance did not do well, but who now may add to their assurance and competence in speech by doing better.

In establishing and maintaining good habits of speech, the teacher should realize the need for constant alertness in detecting speech deficiencies, and for constant checking of pupils to make sure that they speak carefully and adequately every time they speak, and not just when they are working on speech selections. 

Here, as in establishing good habits of reading, the results will not always be immediate or substantial. They will nevertheless be forthcoming if diligent effort is used consistently. 

In addition to the aspects of the speech subject stressed above is the equally important fact that good speech work makes it possible, through oral interpretation, for pupils to gain new insight into the appreciation and understanding of literature. It should be understood that throughout these upper grades this aspect of the speech work should be constantly in mind and constantly applied by the pupils. Furthermore, the instruction and practice in this direction supplied by the actual speech material should be transferred by the pupils, under the teacher's direc- 92

tion, to all selections that lend themselves to oral interpretation. Thus pupils may be made aware of the fact that many of the speech techniques they will learn in school have values of a general nature, values which fit them for greater efficiency and lead them to greater enjoyment in experiences outside their actual school work. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. Many of the selections in this section contribute to this purpose. The poem "The Travel Bureau," for example, makes it possible for children to see that many people have many dreams which they cannot or do not realize. This is a type of understanding which encourages appreciation of the fact that society does not permit people to have everything they want. 

The story "The Heyday of the Blood" contributes notably to social understanding and a knowledge and appreciation of character. Pupils are helped to understand the fact that nearly everyone feels from time to time that the world is not very satisfactory, and that he has been singled out for misfortune. They will learn that people have passed through this stage of thought and arrived at conclusions that give them hope and confidence. They will find in reading about the family in this story that certain restrictions must be placed upon people who are not capable of doing everything that they want to do. They will understand also, in connection with the grand old man in this story, that often people's spirits are more powerful than their bodies. They will find that it is admirable to have views on life which do not permit unhappiness that can be avoided by keen enjoyment of life. Pupils gain an insight also into the fact that simple pleasures can be deeply rewarding, and that for most people simple pleasures are the only kind of pleasures available.

The essay "A Double Harvest" contributes to an understanding of the fact that frequently people do not appreciate their possessions. It helps them understand the fact that, as the Preparatory Note says, "Man cannot live for bread alone."

Other selections contribute to other opportunities for understanding. 

In teaching selections that contribute to this purpose, the teacher will find it desirable to point out from time to time how important it is to have an understanding of the world in which we live, and of the people who live in it with us. In this connection she may point out that in practically all professions and businesses, social understanding is absolutely necessary. Discussions along these lines are easy to begin and helpful to develop. 93

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, reading to and with the pupils the paragraphs preceding the section study exercises. Through this reading and discussion she will assist pupils to find helpful conclusions regarding the material they have studied in this section. The teacher will make sure that the pupils use the study exercises effectively. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make at this time the customary check on the achievement of the pupils in regard to their appreciation of the material read and the increase in their reading skills. If any are found deficient in any of these aspects of reading improvement, the teacher should take steps to correct these deficiencies. 

3. Use of book list. The list may be increased by the pupils and the teacher on the basis of the books that pupils know about, and the teacher's knowledge of the books that are available to the group through school and local libraries.

SECTION SIX-GLIMPSES OF NATURE 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that in the field of nature much has been written that can contribute to the pleasure and information of readers. The section consists of the following selections: 

Afternoon on a Hill (p. 362)

A Fight with a Whale (pp. 363-369) A Letter from Rocky Cliff (Speech) (pp. 370-371) 

Speech on the Weather (pp. 372-374) 

With Helmet and with Hose (pp. 375-382) 

Some African Experiences (pp. 382-386)

Another Letter from Rocky Cliff (Speech) (pp. 387-388) 

The Silver Fox (pp. 389-394) 

When the Stars Come Out (pp. 395-402)

Let's Read Poetry Aloud! (Speech) (pp. 403-404) 

I Sigh for the Land of the Cypress and Pine (p. 405) 

April Weather (p. 406)

Spring, The Travelling Man (pp. 406-407) 

A Vagabond Song (p. 407) 

Reynard the Fox (pp. 407-409) 

The Hummingbird (pp. 409-410)

The Song Sparrow (pp. 410-411) 

Woodman, Spare That Tree! (pp. 411-412)

The Sound of Trees (p. 412) 

May Flowers (p. 413)

Section Review (pp. 414-416) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Six the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. 95

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Afternoon on a Hill." In reading the introductory poem to and with the pupils the teacher will want to indicate that the poem is written by one noted for her enthusiasm about life; that the poem reflects that kind of enthusiasm for life and nature; that the poem combines reality and imagination; and that it suggests the possibilities of enjoyment that may be had in connection with nature. Then as in previous sections she will indicate to the pupils the value of this poem as a symbol of the section content, and suggest that the selections in this section are going to deal with various aspects of nature that they will find enjoyable and informative. She will want also to make the customary use of the interpretative pictures in the border. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will make the customary preview of the section with the pupils, indicating the variety and extent of the nature material that they are to study. She will point out the fact that this section is called "Glimpses of Nature," and that the title is meant to indicate that here they will see a small portion of the great subject of nature. She will stress the fact from time to time that after they have glimpsed these aspects of nature here they will probably want to follow this kind of reading in other books that they may find at home or in libraries.

In making the preview the teacher may want to point out that "A Fight with a Whale" tells an unusual story in which one of the exciting moments is illustrated on page 367; that "A Letter from Rocky Cliff" is a selection in their Speech Improvement Program that combines an amusing letter on nature with definite speech instruction; that "Speech on the Weather" is a short article by Mark Twain, who is noted for his humor; that the article "With Helmet and with Hose" is written by a famous and popular scientist about his experiences under the water; that "Some African Experiences" is an article that will take them far away with a famous explorer; that "Another Letter from Rocky Cliff" goes on with the experiences of the friend at camp; that "The Silver Fox" is an interesting animal story by a writer whose works they have probably read before.

And so the teacher will conduct the children briefly through the section, causing them to look forward with interest and enthusiasm to the material that they will read later. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Several of the selections in this section are correlated with other studies. The list of such relationships appears at the end of this second book division of the monograph. 96

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will direct the customary use of the Preparatory Notes for this section, supplementing them whenever it seems necessary for the adequate preparation of her particular group. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the customary use of the Study Exercises, making sure as she goes along that pupils are gaining from their reading a maximum increase in their appreciation and reading skills. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the pupils proceed through the section, the teacher will make sure that they are aware of the purposes and goals that are to be sought in this section. 

 The subject of nature is an easy one for children to find joy in. Real nature is something with which most children are pleasurably acquainted. Reading about nature is an activity which most of them have for a long time pursued. It is necessary then only that the teacher make the most of this congenial subject in establishing firmly the thought that reading enjoyment may come from it. 

One of the approaches to be made in the indications of genuine enjoyment is through variety. Most children, it is true, know something about some phase of nature, but no child will know all the aspects of nature that are presented in this section. For example, it is unlikely that any of the children have had any real experience concerning whales. 

Therefore the first story in the section will contain material new and interesting for everybody. Pupils will get from it the joy of excitement, and satisfaction in the triumph of courageous men. The speech letter, however, contains familiar facts, and Mark Twain's "Speech on the Weather" concerns a subject with which everyone is well acquainted. 

On the other hand, the article by Beebe, "With Helmet and with Hose," takes readers into unknown realms and makes evident a possible variety in this field of nature. Also it is unlikely that any pupils have a first-hand acquaintance with Africa, and this journey to a distant continent will impress them with the variety of nature. Many pupils have seen some of the animal traits and activities presented in Seton's story, "The Silver Fox." All of them have seen the stars. Yet each of these stories, because of its point of view and special approach to the subjects, will bring something new to most of the pupils, and will contribute to the knowledge that the study of nature is, partly through its variety, genuinely enjoyable. The nature poems will, through their word pictures, bring fresh delights and fresh information regarding nature. 97

In this connection the importance of the ideas in the speech selection, "Let's Read Poetry Aloud!" should be stressed. This article, coming as it does before the nature poems, prepares the way for heightened enjoyment and appreciation of these poems and others that may be read later, in school or out, through oral interpretation. Using this material at this time and in this connection, the teacher can vividly, effectively, and lastingly impress upon the pupils the importance of adding to their skills techniques that permit them to make the most of the possibilities of the oral interpretation of literature. Following the introductory material on page 403, the teacher will discuss with the children in considerable detail the answers to the question, "Can you tell what mood?" Then by having the children carry out the instructions on page 404, the teacher can make it increasingly certain to pupils that their voices and the manner of their using them is vital in the correct oral interpretation of literature. 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. The material in every section of the book contributes to the achievement of this purpose, whether the purpose is literally mentioned or not. This section, however, is one of the best in which to make this purpose consistently sought because it is easy for pupils to see, since nature is all about them, that it is a great and worth-while subject. Therefore, an obvious connection can be made between the value of the subject, and the importance of learning about a subject, which leads to a conviction on the part of readers that in this kind of reading they are cultivating permanent worth-while reading interests. In extending and deepening the cultivation of these interests, the teacher will want to indicate that these are but glimpses of nature that the pupils are getting, and that dozens of other stories and poems dealing with nature are available in the books that may be had in their home, school, and community libraries. 

The teacher will want to stress the fact that in this section, with one or two exceptions, the material is to a large extent factual, even though it is presented fictionally. She will want to indicate on the basis of this observation that reading of this kind is not for recreatory pleasure alone, but for the purpose of finding information of the world as it may be gained through reading and literature. Statements and observations of this sort help build the confidence pupils have in the fact that literature has these many, perhaps unsuspected, values; that it is not a circumscribed subject to be studied and forgotten, but a great field of varied information and recreation. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in the experiences of others. This purpose, like most of the others, runs through the material of most of the sections, but it, too, from time to time needs 98

special emphasis. It should be remembered that the value of this purpose lies in its power to broaden pupil experience—that it makes it possible for pupils to break through the limits of their actual life and acquire the knowledge and experience that others have put into writing. It is this broadening that makes vicarious experience valuable. 

In this section children may gain particularly interesting and helpful experiences in the story "A Fight with a Whale," in the articles "With Helmet and with Hose" and "Some African Experiences," and in the story "The Silver Fox." In connection with the whale story, they may project themselves so as to take part in heroic action, share in difficult work well done, and participate generally in the excitement of a new experience. In the article by Beebe they may descend with him under the surface of the ocean, and explore with him this strange new world of underwater life. To many children who are interested in the possibilities of explorations, this experience can be made real. 

Thousands of readers have gone vicariously to Africa with Osa and Martin Johnson and been richer for the experience. Most children will find that easily possible in this selection. Ernest Thompson Seton has for many years been carrying children away with him to strange far places where animals are the chief actors. 

Whenever it is possible for readers to identify themselves with the activities of characters read about, they gain from their reading not only vicarious experience, but also a deeper interest in reading, and a greater respect for the possibility of what reading may do for them. 

Therefore it is well always for the teacher to keep in mind the need to encourage pupils to see themselves in the actions and experiences of those read about, in whatever reading they do in this and other suitable books.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will direct the customary use of the section review, being careful to see that pupils understand what the section purposes have been, and that they have with a fair degree of success achieved these purposes. The exercises will be done for the purpose of making the child feel assured that he has adequately remembered and sufficiently understood the selections he has read in this section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make her customary check at this time to make sure that pupils are not accumulating deficiencies in skills and appreciations that will handicap them in their future reading. If any pupils are found deficient, the teacher will want to help them, through discussion and exercises, to overcome their reading weakness. 99

3. Use of book list. The customary use will be made of the book list, with the teacher and pupils adding other titles and descriptive comments of books that are available. From time to time in this connection the teacher will want to stress the point that she has previously brought out-that the reading they do in their literature text prepares them and encourages them to read other books that carry their special reading interests farther.

2; page 382, exercise 5; page 386, exercise 2; page 413, exercise 2; page 

414, exercise 1.

1; page 382, exercises 1, 2, 3, 4; page 386, exercises 1, 4, 5; page 394, exercises 1, 4, 5; page 401, exercises 1, 2; page 402, exercises 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 9, 10, 11; page 414, exercise 5.

SECTION SEVEN-THE WONDERS OF SCIENCE AND INVENTION 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading of the wonders of science and invention that contribute to the spirit and accomplishment of our age will help pupils interpret the world through literature. The section consists of the following selections: 

The Promise of Science (p. 418) 

With the Ice Patrol (pp. 419-425)

The Man Who Conquered Hunger (pp. 425-430) 

100

Preparing a Radio Talk (Speech) (pp. 431-432) 

The Secret of the Machines (pp. 433-434) 

A Great Museum (pp. 434-440) 

Outrivaling Aladdin's Lamp (pp. 440-444)

The Romance of the Wheel (pp. 445-447) 

The Power Plant (pp. 448-449) 

The Battle of the Wires (pp. 449-452) 

Number, Please? (Speech) (pp. 453-454)

The Telephone Directory (pp. 455-456) 

Under a Telephone Pole (p. 456) 

What the Bees Told Henri Fabre (pp. 457-461) 

Air Stewardess (pp. 462-465)

Section Review (pp. 466-468) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Seven the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To cultivate worth-while reading and speaking interests. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

1. Use of the introductory selection "The Promise of Science."

The teacher will want to point out to the pupils in reading this introductory selection with them and to them that it is written by a famous scientist who is noted also for his excellent writing. She will make a point of the suitability of having this section introduced by a man of Millikan's particular interests, rather than by a poet. 

She will want to bring out also the fact that these sentences contain a great thought which they will enjoy discussing thoroughly before going on to the section selections. The border pictures will, as usual, contribute effectively to the understanding of the spirit and nature of the selections to be found in this section. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher in making a preview of this section with the pupils will want to keep before them awareness of the fact that this section differs somewhat from some of their previous sections, and that the big contribution it is going to make to their general knowledge is that reading about science helps them understand their world, and helps prove the value of literature. She will find it easy to point out also that such reading can be, and here is, as interesting as the reading they have done in connection with other subjects. She will prove this aspect of the study with such selections as "With the Ice Patrol," "The Man Who Conquered Hunger," and "The Battle of the Wires," in which information is accompanied by strong narrative interest. 101

In presenting the actual selections in this brief preview, the teacher may want to indicate that the men in the ice patrol story risked their lives many times in carrying out their duties, and that the illustration on page 423 will give pupils some idea of the dangers of the job that these men did; that there are many hardships in the development of anything that is worth while, and that the story "The Man Who Conquered Hunger" will show the hardships that McCormick had to go through before finding success; that the selection “Preparing a Radio Talk” will give them interesting and helpful information regarding an activity that is becoming more and more important to everybody; that the poem “The 

Secret of the Machines" will show them how it is possible for a clever poet to make poetry out of such things as machines; that the selection "A Great Museum" will show them how many animals and other strange items of interest can be preserved so that people may see them and appreciate them in our own country; that the selection "Outrivaling 

Aladdin's Lamp" will help them see the great inventor Edison at work; that the selection "The Romance of the Wheel" will give them an interesting combination of history and geography and science. And so she will proceed through the section with the children, keeping them aware of the spirit and content of the section and the manner in which the various selections contribute to the section meaning. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section practically all of the selections have a high correlation with the subject of general science. 

The list of selections with such relationships appears at the end of this second book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes and the autobiographical letters in this section, making sure before assigning each selection for reading that the preparation has been sufficient for her particular group. For average classes the preparatory material that the pupils will read in the text is sufficient. The teacher will find it particularly helpful in this section to encourage discussions touching upon facts related in the introductory letters. For example, in the letter by Charles Peden on page 419, an interesting discussion may be had concerning the people mentioned by Peden as the subjects of the "talkies" that he made. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. In directing the use of the Study Exer cises the teacher will make sure that her pupils are gaining the kind and amount of information that they should absorb through proper reading of the selections, and proper use of the exercises. References to exercises in this section appear at the end of the section presentation in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the class proceeds through the study of 102

the selections in the section, the teacher will make it easy for them to remain aware always of the section purposes and the main ideas of the section and the manner in which the selections that they are reading contribute to the purposes and ideas. This type of awareness will in itself contribute to a general understanding of the section after the section has been completed.

 The obvious kind of joy that pupils will find in reading this section is in the narrative interest of most of the selections. But as they read and realize this type of enjoyment, they will be conscious also of the opportunity for deeper enjoyment through the fact that all these selections, though they have narrative excitement, also offer interesting and helpful information. The teacher will remember, and the pupils will be quick to recall, that this latter type of enjoyment is what is meant by genuine enjoyment—that it is the kind of enjoyment they are expected to find in their reading. 

In the story "With the Ice Patrol," for instance, pupils find enjoyment not only in the reading of action in dangerous places, but also in the splendid characters of the men who take part in these actions. In the selection "The Man Who Conquered Hunger" the pupils may find enjoyment in the achievement of one who refused to be defeated. This kind of genuine enjoyment can be found in most of the selections in this section.

The teacher is further reminded that this constant first purpose of creating genuine joy in reading is never to be considered merely an end in itself, nor as an exclusive purpose. It forms the basis for the true achievement of all other purposes, and is considered a foundation without which other purposes cannot be achieved. 

2. To cultivate worth-while reading and speaking interests. a. Reading. For any pupils who may feel that reading in literature cannot bring to them the clear-cut kinds of values that are worth while, this section is particularly helpful. The reading interests that will be encouraged here lead obviously to an understanding and appreciation of matters that are worth while in everyday living and thinking. This point can be easily stressed by the teacher, and easily absorbed by the pupils. b. Speaking. The speech material in this section makes a definite contribution to the cultivation of worth-while reading and speaking interests. These selections provide valuable knowledge in the use of two of the commonest and most important mechanical devices of our time, 103

the radio and the telephone. Acquiring a knowledge of such oral skills helps the pupil find ways whereby he can make better use of things with which he has been familiar. Children are quick to see the value of really knowing something about the requirements of radio broadcasting. 

The material in this first speech selection in the section gives him such information in a manner which is casy to understand. The second speech selection gives valuable information concerning the telephone. It is through the use of such obviously valuable information that the pupils can become aware of the possibilities of acquiring worth-while reading and speaking interests. Once they are aware of these possibilities most of them will be quick to add to their abilities along these lines and will find pleasure in making themselves more competent in the finding and appreciating of worth-while interests. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. In helping pupils understand how this third purpose may be achieved, the teacher may remind them of the fact that our age is chiefly noted for its progress along the lines of science, mechanics, and invention. She may, in developing the achievement of this purpose through the use of material in this section, spend some time discussing the fact, and the evidences of the fact, that our age is a scientific one. Discussions may be had concerning such subjects as coastal defenses and patrols, the importance of weather and the means of foretelling it, the importance of farm machinery, the great extent in use and power of the radio, the improved methods that exist for preserving scientific specimens, the tremendous importance of electricity, the constant need and use of telegraphy and trans-ocean cables, the value of the telephone, the great value of scientific thinking and investigating as brought out in the story about Henri Fabre, and finally in this section, the importance of the airplane, which is perhaps the best symbol of the scientific and mechanical development in our age.

Discussions on these subjects, held either independently of the actual material in the section, or preferably as bases for later discussion of the particular selection, will be stimulating and helpful to pupils. During and after such discussions the teacher will point out the connection between such studies and the purpose of promoting social understanding and developing character. She will indicate that social understanding means an understanding of the way people live and think, and that to have such understanding in this age it is necessary to know and appreciate the influence that science and invention have in our age. 

In connection with the development of character, as a partial preparation the teacher may point out the persistence, skill, and practical heroism of many of the people whose accomplishments are written about 104

in this section. Through these means the teacher will be able to indicate to the children several of the qualities that they or anybody must have in order to develop and maintain good character. 

Thus throughout the section, in developing this purpose, the teacher will stress the idea that an understanding of our current world requires an appreciation of some of the contributions that science and invention have made.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, making sure that pupils understand and appreciate the summary material just above the section study exercises, and through the use of the exercises themselves that the pupils are able to recall and assimilate information that they have gained in the reading of the section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making this check on the basis of observations she has made through the study of the section and the summary check afforded by the section study exercises, the teacher will make certain that none of her pupils are accumulating deficiencies in their reading skills and their general appreciation abilities. 

If she finds any pupils who are below average in these reading abilities she will need, through added review and special additional work, to bring them up to what is for them an adequate level. 

3. Use of book list. To this list may be added by the pupils and the teacher such additional books concerned with science and invention as may be available in a particular school or community library. The teacher will find that many of her pupils will be especially interested in this kind of reading and she should make, in connection with this section, a special effort to find and describe for the pupils all the good books on the subject available to them.

SECTION EIGHT-THE CHARM OF AMERICA 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading in this field provides an understanding of one's country that leads to increased appreciation of its beauty, variety, and strength. The section consists of the following selections: 

Going on a Journey (p. 470) 

America the Beautiful (p. 471)

The Desert (pp. 472-475) 

My Prairies (p. 476) 

Hills (p. 477) 

Notes on Yellowstone (pp. 478-479) 

New York (p. 481)

Preparing a Travel Talk (Speech) (pp. 482-483) 

"He"-The City (pp. 484-486)

The Carolinas and the Low Country (pp. 486-490) 

A Prayer for a Little Home (p. 490) 

Dusk in the Low Country (p. 491) 

A Swing through Texas (pp. 492-495)

An Immigrant Boy Arrives in New York (pp. 495-498) 

The Song of the Chattahoochee (Speech) (pp. 499-500)

Section Review (pp. 501-502) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Eight the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 106

1. Use of the introductory selection-"Going on a Journey." In reading this selection to and with the pupils the teacher will want to encourage discussion of the complete meaning of each of the three sentences. She will want to discuss with the pupils the aspects of a journey that Hazlitt emphasizes, and the interest in the thought and manner of expression that can be found in the third sentence. She may want to point out that Hazlitt was a fine essayist of the early nineteenth century, and that he found particular delight in making observations on life, literature, and the activities of people. 

The pictures in the border are particularly useful in this section in helping the pupils see the meaning and extent of the section content. 

They will find, for example, the most obvious contrasts between the middle picture at the top with the farm scene, and the middle picture at the bottom with the skyscrapers of a great city. The other small pictures in the border will be equally helpful in encouraging discussion of the section. They depict scenes that will be described or suggested in the content of the section. 

2. A preview of the section. In making the preview with and for the pupils the teacher will continue the thoughts brought out in discussions of the introductory selection and the border pictures. She will keep the pupils aware from time to time that all selections in the section contribute to the general spirit and information of the section, and indicate the contribution that the selections make to this section meaning. 

In beginning her preview with the poem “America the Beautiful” she will point out that the inspiration for this poem came to a teacherpoet as she stood on a high mountain and was thrilled by the beauty she could see from this peak; that the article "The Desert" will bring some new ideas about this land ordinarily considered worthless; that the two poems "My Prairies" and "Hills" will show how people are attracted variously by differing kinds of landscapes, both of which are common in our country; that "Notes on Yellowstone" will take them briefly to one of our most famous national parks, which some of them may have seen; that the picture on page 480 contributes to the amazing richness and variety of our country; that the poem "New York" provides a word picture of our largest city; that “Preparing a Travel Talk" is a speech selection in which practical use will be made of the kind of material they have been reading in this section; that the article "He-The City" is an unusual account of the city of Chicago; that in the selection "The 

Carolinas and the Low Country" they will find much that will interest them about the South; that "A Prayer for a Little Home" will bring 107

them back briefly from their travels; that the poem "Dusk in the Low 

Country" takes them again into the South; that "A Swing through

Texas" gives them 'a glimpse of that mammoth state; that “An Immigrant Boy Arrives in New York" shows them, through the eyes of a newcomer, the wonders of the city; and that the poem "The Song of the 

Chattahoochee" is another speech contribution which leads to their increased enjoyment of the choral reading of poetry and the oral interpretation of literature.

Having made this brief preview for the purpose of suggesting with enthusiasm what the pupils will want to look forward to reading, the teacher will lead them back again to the beginning of the section to take up the more thorough presentation of the selections. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section nearly every poem and article has a high correlation with the subject of geography. A listing of the selections that have such relationships appears at the end of this second book division in the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, adding to them such supplementary information as may be desirable for her particular group. In classes which are familiar with the subjects described in any particular selection, pupils will enjoy discussion concerning those subjects. In many of the 

Notes in this section there are interesting bits of information supplied by the authors especially for this book. Hamlin Garland, for example, shows in his comments his love of the prairies he writes about. Arthur 

Guiterman, though he does not mention it in his letter, spends much of his time in Vermont, where his love of hills may be indulged. Thus also do the other authors who have written autobiographical notes assist in showing their qualifications for being interested and competent writers of the material they have written. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the customary use of the Study Exercises in helping pupils to appreciate and understand these selections. The teacher will remember that the exercises are to be used for the combined purposes of increasing specific reading skills and increasing reading appreciation. References to the exercises in this section are made at the end of the section presentation in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher should keep the children aware of the reading goals of this section, the general meaning of the section, and the contributions that each selection makes to this section meaning. 

Through this awareness it is expected that pupils will gain not only a better perspective and understanding of the content read, but that they will at the same time be forming an ability to understand that will apply to all their reading. 108

Pupils may be helped to find in reading this section that joy in reading can be extremely various. 

The point that may be stressed here is that pupils find delight in reading about scenes that are familiar to them, and that they may find equal delight in reading about scenes that are far away from their experience. 

This ability to enjoy both the familiar and the unfamiliar is a requirement for good readers. It should be pointed out to the pupils that if they read only about things with which they are already familiar they would add very little to their information. It may be pointed out also that reading about scenes with which they are somewhat familiar helps them to realize that other people have developed ideas, and formed word pictures, about these familiar scenes that bring a newness to them, that enhance known beauty or add depth to an understanding partially held. 

The speech selection "Preparing a Travel Talk” adds pertinently to the possibility of enjoying this type of material. One of the interesting facts of travel lies in the telling about it, at least to the one who tells. 

In order to make it possible for this kind of enjoyment to be shared more by those who listen, speech work in this connection is extremely valuable. 

For that reason an outline was provided within this speech material to be used as a model for the organized preparation of competent talks about travel. It will be easy for pupils to see that if one has anything to say, he should know how to say it effectively, whether his purpose be to entertain, to inform, to persuade, or to inspire. 

In connection with the other speech selection in this section, "The Song of the Chattahoochee,” the teacher will make the approach through the value of the oral interpretation of literature. Pupils will gain infinitely more in a thorough understanding and appreciation of poetry through this type of interpretation than they could through silent and unshared reading. By this time in their school year pupils have discovered this fact, and further emphasis upon it is merely for the purpose of maintaining the confidence they have in this preferable method of interpreting literature which lends itself to the method. 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. The manner in which the content of this section contributes to the achievement of this purpose is similar to that in the last section, though it is more specific. The material of the last section helped promote social understanding through appreciation of one of the great developments of our age; the material of this section can promote social understanding through increased appreciation of the make up of our own country. 

Such increased appreciation leads to a development of character through 109

providing readers with a basis of greater toleration of people and places with whom and with which they had not been previously familiar. Just as real travel is broadening, in that it brings to travelers the truth that in other places people are still people and that their lives are similar to the lives of those who travel, so the arm-chair traveler may have, though in somewhat lessened degree, this same kind of broadened and deepened understanding. 

In this connection the poem "America the Beautiful" is of value. 

Here, through the development of pictorial appeal, of historical subjects, of heroic intimations of the past, and of hopeful looking into the future, the various delights and values of our country are combined. Using this combination as a starting point, pupils may helpfully be introduced in the following selections to the specific aspects of our country that contribute to the general picture suggested in this poem. When lovers of hills appreciate the love others have for the prairies, they absorb knowledge that leads to their social understanding. Thus it is too when lovers of the prairies appreciate the love others have for hills. Those who have never lived in or even seen great cities may see in the poem about New York the desire for the same ideals that are sought in their own environment. From the selection concerning another great city-"He—The 

City" others not familiar with cities can find the same type of understanding, can glimpse the similarity of values that exist everywhere in cities, in country towns, and on farms. This knowledge, which may easily be developed throughout this entire section, leads similarly to the fact that all these varied aspects of our country, both in regard to people and places, combine to make the greatness and beauty and the strength of this one country of which we are all a part. In realizing this basic social truth readers find it easy to understand other people, to develop in their own characters helpful tolerance and appreciation of some of the basic truths of life.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review for this section, discussing with the pupils the two short paragraphs above the section study exercises in order to encourage them to look back over what they have read to establish the memory of the general picture that their reading has built for them. The exercises themselves will help pupils test their understanding and review the facts that should be remembered. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. This check will be made by the teacher to make sure that pupils have not accumulated reading 110

difficulties that will lead to inadequacies in further reading experiences. 

If any pupils are deficient at this time the teacher, through special use of the exercises in this section or through further exercises of her own devising, will want to bring them up to the level they are capable of achieving. 

3. Use of book list. In adding to the list of books that may be read as further contributions to the theme of this section, the teacher may find it possible to acquire special local material, in pamphlet or other brief forms, that will encourage pupils to have a livelier interest in the beauties and significant values of their own locale. The teacher should also encourage reading far afield from the environment of her pupils. Books and material of this nature are usually numerous, and the list of what is available to particular groups should be made as extensive as possible so that everyone may find something which to him can bring joy and information.

Exercises for training in reading skills-Section Eight-Book Two

3. Selection and evaluation of pertinent material: page 479, exercises 2, 3; page 490, exercise 4; page 495, exercise 3; page 498, exercise
