SECTION NINE-A MIDSUMMER NIGHT'S DREAM 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that reading a Shakespearean play is an enjoyable and enriching experience. The section consists of the play, “A Midsummer Night's Dream."

Purpose. In presenting Section Nine the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purpose: 

To create genuine joy in reading.

It is probable that this is the first play by Shakespeare that the pupils have read in school. It is therefore essential that every effort be made to provide in this reading experience for the enjoyment and unlabored appreciation of the pupils. Bearing in mind that it is an unfortunate truth that children are frequently turned against Shakespeare, the teacher must do everything necessary in preparing for the reading, and in conducting the reading, to make the experience constantly clear and enjoyable. The procedures are set up in this monograph in collaboration with the text material to make this highly valuable achievement possible.

The teacher should be constantly aware that this play may be thought of as the summation of all that has been learned and aspired to in the pupils' training to find the values in the oral interpretation of literature. 

All the discussions concerning this play should center on the assumption that the play will be read orally for its greatest enjoyment. Probably the teacher will want to make preliminary presentations, and suggestions are made below for the carrying out of such procedures. However, it should be repeated that the entire final emphasis concerning the presentation and development of the play should be upon the oral aspects. It is perhaps enough to say that Shakespeare wrote his play for acting, and that reading aloud in class is the closest practicable approach to presenting what Shakespeare intended. 

1. Use of the introductory selection-"Two Worlds." The brief paragraph by Leigh Hunt on page 504 serves well to bring the child into contact with a highly imaginative selection. The teacher will, in discussing this paragraph with the pupils, point out that they have read many selections that deal with the world that can be measured as Leigh Hunt indicates; that they have read several selections that are fanciful, but that here for the first time they are going to read a long selection that requires, as Hunt expresses it, that "We feel with our hearts and our imaginations.” 112

The teacher will want to point out to the pupils the significance of the small pictures in the border, saying of the top picture, for example, that it shows a man named Bottom, who in the play put a donkey's head over his head, and that those little fairies flying about him are fairies that appear in the play; that on the right side an impish little figure called 

Puck is chattering to Oberon, the king of the fairies; that on the left side two lovers are talking; that on each side of the bottom panel men sleeping in the woods are seen; and that in the center of the bottom panel several workmen of Athens are practicing a play which they give within the play that the pupils are going to read. She will point out to the pupils that most of these illustrations indicate humorous incidents, and that the play has in it much that is very funny. 

2. Use of the preparatory material. The material written as preparation for reading the play is adequate for average groups. The teacher should read it carefully to and with the pupils, expanding any ideas that she may think require more explanation for her particular group, and encouraging discussions of any ideas that the pupils as they go along would like to develop further. It would be helpful for the teacher to have a small model of an Elizabethan theater made, showing the main points that are discussed in the preparatory material. It is likely that several of the pupils in the class would like to make this theater as a project. If so, they should be encouraged to do it and submit it for use in class. 

The suggestions under the heading "Reading the Play" on page 506 should be most carefully read, as should the suggestions on the following two pages. The teacher should frequently indicate to the pupils that it is necessary for them to understand all this preparatory material in order to get the most out of the play, and that if they do understand it they will have no trouble at all in appreciating the play. The teacher will note that all these instructions are addressed directly to the pupils, and that she may very well expand many or all of them and give them to the pupils independently in her own words, and with her own additions. 

She will note also that the pupil is told that the teacher, or someone in the class, should read the story of "A Midsummer Night's Dream" from one of the books that tell the story of the play. It would be desirable in most groups for the teacher herself to do this, choosing the story from one of the two books mentioned here, or from any other book with which she may be familiar. If the teacher is adept at retelling stories, she could retell it more effectively for her particular group than can those who have retold the play in writing.

The purpose of this preliminary retelling of the play is to make clear to pupils what the general plots are, and to describe the types of 113

characters that take part in them. When these two aspects of the play are clear, children are not likely to have trouble in reading the play itself. After the story of the play has been told, either by the teacher or through reading, the suggestions on pages 507 and 508 should be carried out in some detail. When the teacher is reasonably sure that the pupils understand the main outlines of the play, and the main differentations between the character groups, the actual reading of the play may proceed. 

3. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises are given for the play as a whole on pages 559, 560, and 561. The exercises were postponed in this manner so that pupils in reading their first play by Shakespeare could, if their ability permitted, proceed without interruption. 

It is possible, however, that in some groups the teacher will want to pause at fairly frequent intervals to make sure that her pupils are following the play with sufficient understanding. A few brief comments by the teacher at the end of each scene may be helpful, and should be made if she feels that some of the pupils are losing their interest or their appreciation. At the end of each act it will be well for the teacher and the pupils briefly to review the main action. When the Study Exercises themselves are reached, they should be used in the customary manner to help pupils check on their understanding of what they have read and the accuracy with which they have read. Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

4. Awareness of goals. In this connection it will be necessary only that the teacher by her persistent enthusiasm and desire to make things clear and interesting keep pupils aware of the fact that their only purpose in reading this play is to find enjoyment in it. 

To create genuine joy in reading. It should not be expected that pupils gain anything from this play except genuine enjoyment. This can be made and should be made a convincingly valuable purpose. When attempts are made at the first teaching of Shakespeare to point out all the magic of his genius as a writer, to stress every evidence of his understanding of humorous action, thought and phraseology, the pupils may be impressed by his ability but will almost certainly become confused by the apparent complexity. 

If on the other hand, as it is suggested here, the one purpose be kept clear, and all techniques remain directed to the achievement of this one purpose, the pupils will understand and appreciate this play. And more 114

important, the thought will become fixed in their minds that Shakespeare, far from being the frightening task-master that many pupils fear, can be light, charming, funny, and enjoyable. Instilling children with this feeling is the biggest contribution a teacher can make at this level in the teaching of Shakespeare. 

The teacher, thoroughly familiar with the play herself, will realize that there are some places in it which do not offer as great opportunities for enjoyment as do other parts. Through these parts, dealing largely with the Duke and the people of his court, she will conduct the pupils quickly and without insistence that they do more than get the general trend of events and understand the background of the local royalty. 

The children must, of course, be familiar with the status of the various younger characters who are in love with each other, but it is not necessary that they study, analyze, or fully comprehend this secondary plot. 

Summing up this aspect of the procedure it may be said that Scene 1 in its entirety may be read without interruption, as a background for the parts of the play which are based upon it. At the end of the scene the teacher may briefly sum up what has occurred. She should not attempt to emphasize to the pupils that any of this material is especially interesting, or that it contains any special merit. She should point out merely that it is of value as background. 

However, in teaching Scene 2, which concludes the first act, the teacher will want to make every preparation for the pupils' full appreciation of the actions and speeches of Bottom and his associates. Throughout the play the teacher should encourage the pupils to read and enact this material in the broadest possible manner, making of it the kind of hilarious humor that Shakespeare had in mind and that modern directors emphasize in this play. Most children will have no difficulty in understanding this kind of humor and finding much fun in it, if they have not previously been frightened by Shakespeare's reputation. Before having the children read this scene aloud, the teacher should tell them that they will find several expressions in it that do not make sense, because Bottom is an ignorant man. But she should also point out that Bottom is a very enthusiastic man, and that all these people, though they have no ability as actors, are very intent upon giving their play for the duke. 

In Act II the fairies enter, and the pupils should be prepared for this third plot and this wholly different treatment of imaginative material. 

They should be told that Shakespeare did not expect anybody to believe in his fairies, but that they are created to supply fun and imaginative joy to an entertaining play.

It may be desirable for the teacher to review with the pupils the aspects of the lovers' plot that enters into this second act. The pupils 115

should understand, for example, the effects of the magic juice on the people on whose eyelids it is placed. 

In the first scene of Act III Bottom and his friends again appear and practice their play. Later in the scene, after the others have left, Bottom wearing the ass's head is found by Titania as she awakens under the influence of the magic juice. The first part of this scene should be read with the same enjoyment of broad humor that was found in the first appearance of Bottom and his friends. The second part, however, including Titania and her assisting fairies, should be read with a feeling of lighter humor, to bring out the charm of Titania's words and the lightness of the fairy scenes. The second scene in the act, in which the parts are played by Puck, Oberon, and the lovers, should be read quickly and with emphasis only upon basic understanding of the action and the results.

And thus through the rest of the play the teacher will vary her procedure according to the material. Those parts which cannot be made especially interesting to pupils should not be dwelt upon lengthily. Those more frequent parts which can be made, through their humor and charm, of interest to pupils should be emphasized for these qualities, and the pupils should be constantly encouraged to read them with enthusiasm, in a spirit of fun.

Summary Activities 

1. Check on section goals and purposes. The outstanding purpose of this section was to help pupils find enjoyment in reading a Shakespearean play. If that purpose has been achieved the presentation and pupil reading have been successful. If it has not been achieved, there is probably no way in which now, at the end of the play, the teacher may make many helpful checks or improvements. However, the use of the exercises on pages 559, 560, and 561 will be very helpful in clearing up for the pupils some points on which they may not have been quite certain, thus adding to the other kind of enjoyment they have found in the play the enjoyment of final understanding. These exercises should be conducted, not with the thought of testing the pupils to find what they do not know, but for the purpose of permitting them to share with others in the class expressions of what they do know about this play. 

Arranging the incidents within each act should be an activity carefully directed but kept interesting. After the pupils have written their arrangements on their own paper, the teacher will read the correct order, clarify the sequence for any who have made imperfect arrangements, and explain their errors. The written work in connection with these 116

exercises permits a choice from several suggestions. Pupils should be encouraged to take the suggestion that really appeals to them, or to choose other ideas that they may prefer. Writing about these points in the play should be enjoyable, and not laborious. The same attitude should be taken by the teacher and the pupils regarding the group activity suggested in exercise 12. 

2. Use of book list. Reading books that give further information of 

Shakespeare's time and works will be enjoyable to some of the pupils, and the teacher should help such pupils find such books if any are available. No attempt should be made, however, to indicate that for all pupils all these books would be interesting. Pupils should be left with the feeling that there is other material connected with practically any type of reading that they may do, and that much of that material is available for them whenever they want it. It should not, however, be forced upon them.

2. Practice in locating sources of information: page 559, exercises

3. Selection and evaluation of pertinent material: page 559, exercises 2, 6, 7.

STRANDS OF APPRECIATION 

Attitudes of thought and feeling resulting in habits of conduct are the outgrowth of appreciation. Appreciation, itself, is based upon awareness and understanding.

To call the pupil's attention to opportunities for appreciation, and to lend cumulative emphasis to desirable attitudes and habits of conduct, the selections in Your World in Prose and Verse have been assembled into strands of appreciation. For the convenience of the teacher the table of contents thus arranged appears below. (It should be noted that in the book itself the table of contents is arranged in centers of interest.)

EXPANDING LITERARY INTERESTS Introduction 

Before proceeding with the teaching of this third book in the Cultural Growth Series, the teacher will want to review the introductory material found on pages 3 to 7 of this monograph. Here will be found the statements of the basic philosophy of this course in reading and literature, and the suggested procedures for the most effective use of the program. 

The material in the following paragraphs supplements the information contained in the part of the general introduction called Reading for Appreciation. As examples of the cumulative growth toward literary culture throughout this series the following titles of selections contributing to the areas of knowledge mentioned in the general introduction are 

Awareness of the Physical World. "To the Uttermost Ends of the 

Earth," "Southward Bound," "The Christ of the Andes," "Jungle Byways in India," "Goethals: The Prophet Engineer," "The Spirit of the 

Night Air Mail," "A Guide in Genoa," "The Tea Trader," "A Modern 

Pied Piper," "Big Brother," "Work: A Song of Triumph," "On the 

Track of Moby Dick," "All for the Sake of a News Beat," "Sheriff 

Roosevelt and the Thieves," "The Ice-Cart," "Wild Brother as an Engineer," "A Wonderer under the Sea," "Split Cherry Tree." 

Social Awareness. "A Day's Pleasure," "The Outsider," "The Last 

Lesson," "The Family Chronicle," "The Citizen," "Falcon Feathers," "Your Gifts," and "A Christmas Carol."

Awareness of the Complexities of Human Nature. "Paul Jespersen's 

Masquerade," "Mary," "Tuft of Flowers," "Brotherhood," "A Mother in Mannville," "From Renascence," "Nancy Hanks," "Big Brother," "Ingo," "The Greater Heroes," "Christmas at Sea," "The Hill Born." 

Awareness of Moral Values. "The Riverman," "Jean Valjean and the Bishop," "The Father," "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner," "Quality," and "The Gold Brick." 

Awareness of Aesthetic Values. "Clearing at Dawn," "The Coromandel Fishers," "The Song of Solomon," and "Song of the Rain Chant."

The teacher will note that the four areas of knowledge developed in the second book in the series are developed more extensively here, and that a new area of knowledge has been added. These facts make evident the opportunities pupils have through this series to progress efficiently toward the ultimate goal of literary appreciation and cultural growth. 127

EXPANDING LITERARY INTERESTS SECTION ONE-HOME LIFE AROUND THE WORLD 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading of home life in their own country and in distant places pupils will gain an understanding and greater appreciation of this aspect of the world. The section consists of the following selections: 

A Choice Gift (p. 20) 

A Day's Pleasure (pp. 21-28)

Character Voices (Speech) (pp. 29-30) 

Your Gifts (p. 31)

The Family Chronicle (pp. 32-39) 

Paul Jespersen's Masquerade (pp. 40-46) 

The Old and the New (pp. 47-52) 

Cradle Song (p. 53) 

Clearing at Dawn (p. 53) 

A Mother in Mannville (pp. 54-60) 

Nancy Hanks (p. 60)

Abe Lincoln and Storytelling (Speech) (pp. 61-63) 

Ingo (pp. 64-68) 

Christmas at Sea (pp. 68-69) 

A Christmas Carol (pp. 70-101). 

My Heart's in the Highlands (p. 102) 

The Hillborn (p. 103) 

Duna (p. 103)

Home-Thoughts, from Abroad (p. 104) 

In Service (pp. 104-105)

Section Review (pp. 106-108) 

Purposes. In presenting Section One the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

It will be seen throughout the portion of the monograph to be used with the book Expanding Literary Interests that the first purpose of every 128

section is to create genuine joy in reading. This purpose is consistently first because without genuine joy no lasting reading benefits can accrue. 

In connection with this fact it should be pointed out that one of the bases for the selection of material for this book was pupil-interest, and this basis was chosen with the same thought-that without real interest no successful teaching in reading and literature can be accomplished. 

Two other purposes that are listed as objectives on page 5 of the Teacher Preface to increase efficiency in both oral and silent reading, and to make a definite and real correlation between good reading and good speaking-appear consistently throughout this monograph by implication, but they do not appear openly. 

In the material that follows the teacher will find assistance in gaining from the study of the contents of this section the chief values that the selections offer. 

1. Use of the introductory selection-"A Choice Gift." The teacher will know, from having made a previous study of the organization and content of this book, that it is divided into sections, each of which has a definite basic theme. The teacher will find it efficient and natural in teaching each section, to use its theme as the core of learning for the pupils. In this connection she will, for each section, find the introductory selection and the small pictures in the border around that selection of pertinent value.

The teacher will want to read the paragraph, “A Choice Gift," with and to the pupils for the purpose of bringing out the thought Irving expresses in the last sentence. The teacher will then be able, having explained to the pupils the purpose of this introductory selection, to suggest that apparently in this section they are going to find stories and poems that tell about the idea of home in our country and in foreign lands. Then using the small pictures, she will want to point out the variety of people and places indicated, and to suggest that each of these pictures indicates the action and characters and locale of selections that they will read later. 

2. A preview of the section. It is suggested that the teacher conduct her pupils on a brief but comprehensive tour of the selections in this section for the purpose of pointing out with enthusiasm the type and variety of selections that the pupils will read later with interest. 

In conducting this preview she may point out, for example, that “A Day's Pleasure" will show them some interesting evidences of home life on the farm and in the small town in our own country not so many years ago; that "Character Voices" is a selection in the Speech Improvement

Program that is provided in this book, and that they will find in it both information and interest; that the poem "Your Gifts" tells of a man's 129

loving memory of his mother, who is a symbol of home; that “The 

Family Chronicle" is a story of people in a foreign country who show their courage in the face of great trouble; that “Paul Jespersen's Masquerade" is an amusing story of a young man's activities in a foreign country; that the poems "Cradle Song" and "Clearing at Dawn" are little word pictures that will take them far away; that the story “A 

Mother in Mannville" brings them back to their own country and tells them about a remarkably fine young boy; that the selection "Abraham 

Lincoln and Storytelling" is another selection in their Speech Improvement Program and that here they will learn not only why Abraham Lincoln was such a fine storyteller, but how they may add to their own ability as storytellers. And so the teacher will proceed through the entire section, making some interesting and effective comment on each of the selections.

In stimulating this interest through a brief survey, the teacher will find the illustrations of particular value. Through seeing the artist's portrayal of a striking event in a selection the pupils will gain an immediate insight into the meaning of what they are to read, Later when they read the selections thoroughly, they will find a new type of interesting information on the back of each of the colored illustrations. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. The teacher will find in this section many selections which may helpfully be correlated with other school subjects whenever she feels that such correlation is of value. A list of selections showing such relationships appears at the end of the third book division of this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will realize, having become familiar with the entire book, that each selection, or each group of short selections, is preceded by a Preparatory Note. This note is to serve the purpose which its name implies. In most instances and for most groups, these notes are adequate as background for understanding and appreciation. However, from time to time, and with certain groups, the teacher may find it necessary to add background, and supply in advance the meanings of certain concepts with which some of her pupils may need help. The autobiographical letters will frequently be found helpful in giving additional background material. Footnotes have been supplied to explain the meanings of the words with which pupils may need assistance. Pupils are expected to use these Notes not only for immediate meaning but as assistance in vocabulary training. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises in this book are planned to help the pupils achieve complete understanding of what they read, to add to their enjoyment through understanding, and to contribute to the maintenance and increase of their reading skills. The optional 130

studies are for those pupils who can answer happily the challenge for further achievement. The Study Exercises should never be used merely to keep the pupils occupied, nor should they be administered so exactingly that pupils are forced to look upon their work in literature as a minute and laborious task. They should be consistently limited to the achievement of the purposes mentioned above. A list of the exercises contributing to specific reading skills appears at the end of this section presentation in the monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the teacher conducts the pupils through the material of this and every section, she should herself remain constantly aware of the goals and purposes that are to be achieved in the section, and she should keep the pupils equally aware of what they are expected to achieve. This is recommended with the thought that learning can be better directed and more lastingly received if the learners know always exactly what they are supposed to be doing. 

 The teacher should understand and the pupils should be helped to appreciate the fact that joy is not an empty surface pleasure, but that genuine joy, the kind that is sought here through reading, has real depth and value. To be sure, the more superficial aspects of joy, in so far as they give children an impression of having surface pleasures as they read, is important in an introductory way. But over and above this kind of inviting, first-glance joy, the other type of deeper joy must be developed. Suggestions for achieving that type of joy will be given in each section in connection with a discussion of this first purpose. 

The teacher may indicate that the thought expressed in "A Choice Gift" is a joyful one, centering as it does around the aspect of home, toward which all people have a joyous attitude; that in the story “A 

Day's Pleasure" they may find the genuine joy of seeing through fiction the pleasant, helpful and unasked for acts of a woman who in her kindness brings deep joy to one less favored; that in the selection "Character 

Voices" they will find information that leads to greater pleasure in speech, and therefore greater joy in doing better what they may not have done well before; that the poem "Your Gifts" shows the possibilities in speaking with joy and respect and love of one's mother; that the story "The Family Chronicle" gives them a genuine pleasure and joy in the splendid characters of the people of this story and that it is a joy to know that such brave people really live; that in the story "Paul Jespersen's 

Masquerade" they find not only the surface joy of reading an amusing story but the deeper, more genuine joy, of realizing that they have found 131

added proof that people's personalities are usually interesting and rather similar all over the world.

Thus in practically every selection in the section, the teacher will find it possible to point out, and the pupils will find it easy to agree, that joy can be and is of great variety, and that when it is genuine it brings readers a deeper appreciation of what they read. 

2. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. In this series of literature books it is assumed that no one has learned as much about reading successfully as he should know, and that therefore it is necessary from time to time to provide work toward the achievement of skills and abilities that perhaps should have been developed earlier, but have not been. If, however, the teacher is certain that in particular groups it is no longer necessary to develop fundamental habits of reading and speaking, she will find that such habits are in constant need of maintenance. Therefore it is well that everyone work toward the achievement of this second purpose, both when it is mentioned, as here, overtly, and also when it is present, as it always is, by implication. a. Reading. One of the important approaches to the establishment of desirable attitudes is through helping pupils find joy in what they read. That aspect has already been adequately developed. Another approach is through the encouragement of pupils to find that what they read has real value for them, and that these values have great variety. 

The pupils should be helped to appreciate the fact that much fiction contributes to an understanding of the basic facts of life as effectively, or more so, than does much that is presented as fact. This is a concept which too many pupils are prone to disregard, or fail to accept fully. 

Pupils should be helped to look upon fiction not as entertainment which helps them get away from reality, but as instruction in story form which helps them to understand people and events. The teacher must first be entirely aware of this fact herself and then be able, through the use of examples, to point it out to the pupils. 

In this connection she would do well to think over a list of books that she and at least some of the pupils have read recently, and from this mutual reading draw out, through discussion, some of the truths of life that have been presented and developed in such books. Then making application to certain stories in this section, the teacher will be able to bring to all the pupils the truth of what she is pointing out. 

Such stories as "A Day's Pleasure," "A Family Chronicle," "A Mother in 

Mannville," and "A Christmas Carol" all contain helpful, pertinent, and interesting contributions to an understanding of life, in addition to their narrative interest and fictional characterization. 132

In general the establishment of good attitudes is not a difficult task. 

Only with pupils who have found reading an unhappy experience will it be necessary to spend much time in the encouragement and development of better attitudes. Most pupils are anxious to find pleasure and value in their reading.

However, it is possible to have excellent attitudes toward reading and yet to have poor habits of reading. Many pupils are excessively distracted as they read. Habits of poor reading observed in the classroom should be checked and changed as they are noticed. Pupils may be subject to such distractions as gazing out the window, looking at each other, looking at the clock, and so on. The teacher will be able to discourage any who indulge these distractions excessively by judicious moving of such pupils into positions that will make it more difficult for them to do the things that have injured their abilities to concentrate. In checking such habits and in carrying out procedures to help break the habits, the teacher should approach the pupils on the basis of helping them do their work better through the elimination of some of their personal activities. When this whole matter of improved habits is tied up with the basic thought of finding pleasure and value in reading, pupils will be, in most instances, extremely willing to do what is necessary to correct habits that have kept them from doing their best work. b. Speaking. In addition to the reading information given above that applies equally to speaking, one aspect of speech usage should be mentioned in connection with good attitudes. That is the use of speech in the oral interpretation of literature. This is the unique contribution that speech makes to a deeper appreciation, and a greater understanding, of all literature that can in any effective way be interpreted orally. This type of interpretation should not be limited in this book to the selections which are specific parts of the Speech Improvement Program. All selections that are parts of this program do contribute to added skills in oral interpretation, but the teacher will find it easily possible on the basis of these specific selections to use oral interpretations of many of the stories, and practically all of the poems, in every section. This does not mean that all reading will be done orally. It does mean that whenever added values are sought, added appreciations are needed, an oral rereading of certain selections or parts of selections will be extremely helpful. 

In connection with the story "A Day's Pleasure," for example, and the speech selection based upon it, "Character Voices," the teacher may assign certain pupils to read aloud some of the conversations between the two women and between other characters as they speak in the story. 

Through the necessity of putting themselves in the places of these people, and reading as they think the others would have talked, the pupils will 133

find it far easier to realize how these people in the story must have felt and acted. This activity will add to the depth of appreciation felt not only by those who participate (though it will be felt most by them), but also by those others in the class who hear such pupil interpretation. 

In regard to habits of speaking, the more specific aspects of the subject may be stressed. These aspects have to do with actual letter and letter combinations of sounds in which some pupils, even at this level, may have marked deficiencies. To such pupils the teacher will want to give some extra and specialized assistance in improved speaking habits. 

This she will be able to do through reviewing for her own information all the speech instruction given on pages 7, 8, 9, 10 and 11 of the Teacher Preface. This information, including the words and sounds in the Phonetic Test Chart, should be sufficient to cover all situations concerning normal pupils in the classroom. Through finding out what sounds certain pupils cannot make successfully, and through providing practice in making such sounds correctly, the teacher will be able to bring all pupils whose speaking equipment is normal to a fair degree of oral efficiency. After everyone in the class is aware of the need to make his word sounds properly, and of the desirability of speaking generally in clear and effective ways, the teacher will want to eliminate such habits of speech laziness as cutting off letters at the ends of words, of leaving out syllables wherever they may occur, of speaking with the mouth inadequately opened, and other aspects of poor speech that are observable in the average classroom.

In developing systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking, the teacher should realize constantly that the best way to eliminate bad habits is to substitute good ones, and to require practice of the good ones. 

3. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

Through a discussion of the story "A Day's Pleasure" the teacher will find it easy to help the pupils see that they have gained just as clear an insight into certain human situations as they would have if Mr. Garland had set out to tell them in one, two, three order something about the hardships of people and the importance of kindness. 

Entering this discussion also will be the contribution that is made here to the development of character. When people read about the kindness of others and the immense difference such kindness makes in the lives of those who give it and receive it, they are likely to absorb this truth into their own consciousness, and add greater kindness to their own being.

This idea should not be taught as a magical contribution, something that leads through two or three days' reading to a splendid new character, 134

but should be taught as one link in a cumulative chain, which when it is finished can be a thing of strength and beauty.

The same application, the same search, and the same conclusions may come from study and discussion of such stories as "The Family 

Chronicle," "A Mother in Mannville," "Ingo," and "A Christmas Carol."

Also such reading, as it carries the reader across oceans and into strange lands, brings to him the understanding that people are in normal times rather similar throughout the world, and that though they may be, and have been from time to time, enemies, they all possess the possibilities of becoming real friends. To promote such social understanding among pupils through literature is to make them aware of one of the really vital and tremendous powers that literature has. That, it should be kept in mind, is one of the major purposes of this entire book-to take pupils around the world and help them see further than the line of their own horizon.

The teacher will note that there is in this section a group of poems following the last prose selection. This grouping occurs in practically every one of the sections in this book. It will be found that in each section these poems contribute definitely, with poetic truth as well as poetic beauty, to the meaning, the theme, of the section. In this first grouping, for example, the home poems make their individual and group contributions to the theme title, “Home Life around the World," and help pupils widen their horizon just as effectively, and along the same lines, as all the other poems and all the prose selections in the section. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will find preceding the study exercises in each section review a paragraph or two of material that looks back over the section, ties up the various thoughts within the section that contribute to its central theme, and in general invites the reader to form certain remembered conclusions regarding the reading he has done in each section. These paragraphs should always be read to and with the pupils to make sure that they understand fully all that the material has to offer. The study exercises in connection with the section work should be used not only as exacting check-ups on remembered facts, but also as activities providing an opportunity for each pupil to express his own reactions and recollections of what he has read. The teacher will note that in the section review there are optional studies, just as there have been throughout the section. These studies should be carried out by those who are capable of making them without excessive burdening. 135

2. Check on section goals and purposes. At the end of each section the teacher will find it desirable to look back upon the accomplishments of her pupils and make certain that they are not falling behind in the various aspects of improvement that they are expected to achieve through reading. The teacher should check the general aspects of reading, such as attitudes and appreciative powers, as well as the more specific aspects dealing with improvement of speech and reading skills. 

She should realize that a check on the general aspects cannot be very definite, but that any gross deficiencies will show up and can be eliminated to some extent. In connection with finding specific deficiencies, if any, the teacher may find it necessary to go back with pupils who show them to some of the exercises that will give them further practice in improving the skills in which they are weak; or it may in a few instances be necessary for the teacher to supply supplementary material of her own, and to work individually with some of the pupils to bring them up to their proper level. In average groups the teacher's goal should be to have each member of the class performing at what for him is a proper level. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will find at the end of each section in this book, a list of books containing material that is basically similar to the theme of the section which it follows. Each of these books is described briefly. In using this list the teacher will want to go over it herself to find out which books are available in various local and school libraries. With the pupils the teacher should add to the list books that she knows, or the pupils know, are available locally. It should be stressed in connection with the book list that one of the chief purposes of an anthology is to encourage readers not only to master what they read in the text, but to seek other books that will add to the pleasures and values of their reading.

SECTION TWO-THE WORLD AT SCHOOL 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that pupils may see and understand youth at school in other lands and other times, and thus deepen their knowledge of people and the world. The section consists of the following selections: 

Thoughts of Youth (p. 110) 

At School on the Middle Border (pp. 111-115) 

A Book (p. 115)

In the Speech Laboratory (Speech) (pp. 116-117) 

The Outsider (pp. 118-123) 

School (p. 124) 

Split Cherry Tree (pp. 125-132)

The Last Lesson (pp. 133-138) The Spires of Oxford (p. 138) The Oxford Recitations (Speech) (pp. 139-140) 

Mary (pp. 141-145) 

One Uses the Handkerchief (pp. 145-156) 

Japanese Students (pp. 157-161)

A Modern Pied Piper (pp. 161-165) The Greater Heroes (p. 166) 

137

The Tuft of Flowers (pp. 167-168) From Renascence (p. 168) In an Old Library (p. 169) Section Review (pp. 170-172) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Two the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in other people's experiences. 

 1. Use of the introductory selection-"Thoughts of Youth." In reading this poem to and with the pupils the teacher will point out that it was chosen to suggest the general subject of the section and that it does so through referring to schoolboys (a reference which includes schoolgirls also) and their thoughts.

Supplementing the discussion of this introductory poem, the teacher will want to mention the small pictures in the border, and to remind the pupils that each of these pictures is connected in some way with the selections they will read in this section. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will make a preview of the section with the pupils along the lines indicated in the procedure for section one. It should be remembered that the main purpose of this preview is to engage the pupils' interest in what they will read later and to direct their thinking so that they can see in what way each of these selections makes its contribution to the section theme. 

The teacher may point out, for example, that the story "At School on the Middle Border" is by the same author who wrote "A Day's Pleasure," but that this story tells something about his own schoolboy days; that the poem "A Book" is a delicate little thing that contains a fanciful thought they will like; that the selection "In the Speech Laboratory" will give them a glimpse of some of the interesting ways in which speech improvement may be carried on; that the story "The 

Outsider" gives a very appealing picture of a little girl who felt herself strange and out of place in her school; that the poem "School" gives a sympathetic picture of something that we all do from time to time; that the story "Split Cherry Tree" contains some very interesting action. 

Thus the teacher will proceed through the section, pointing out to the pupils the kind of material they are going to be reading, and helping them to look forward to it with pleasure. 138

3. Use of helpful correlation. The teacher will find several selections in this section that have a close correlation with certain other school subjects. A list of these relationships appears at the end of the third book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher should continue to bear in mind that the notes written as preparation for an understanding of these selections will be found adequate for average groups. However, knowing her particular group, she will know whether it is necessary to supply additional preparation, or to explain in advance certain concepts that she thinks her pupils may not understand sufficiently well. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The Study Exercises, like the Prepara tory Notes, are designed to meet the needs of average groups. They should be considered as an agency for adding to the reading skills, and as a check on specific abilities. Their general use, however, should be to help pupils come to a complete understanding, and reach satisfactory conclusions, regarding specific selections. The optional studies, which follow all selections for which they are suitable, should be done only by those who can do them happily and without excessive burdening. 

Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher, as the class proceeds, should from time to time call her pupils' attention to the fact that each selection is making its contribution to the general subject of the section. She will call their attention also to the fact that they are working toward the achievement of certain purposes in this section, and that as they go along they are expected to acquire reading information and skills which will help them achieve these purposes. 

 Helping the pupils to find genuine joy in their reading, the teacher will continue to use such approaches as were suggested in the first section. She will want to bring out the fact that some pupils may want to change their ideas of the importance of joy in reading—that they might be amazed to find out how big a thing genuine joy can be. Then she will want to indicate that joy is not just an empty pleasure, but can come as a result of successful thinking and successful doing. She may want to suggest that much of the joy that they find in reading lies in the sympathy they feel for deserving characters who are not very happy or not very successful, and in the pleasure they feel when such people find happiness and success. This is a real joy that pupils can easily understand. 139

In discussing such a story as "Split Cherry Tree" the teacher may bring out the thought that in it one finds joy in the interesting dialogue, in the fact that it is so easy to understand the boy's feelings, in the exciting fear that the father may carry out some violent action, but most of all in the knowledge that joy lies in the satisfaction of everybody's getting together on a basis of happy understanding. Through discussing such aspects of this story and other stories, the teacher will find it possible to impress pupils with the true worth of genuine joy, and create in them the desire and ability to find genuine joy in reading. 

In helping the pupils find genuine joy in their speech work, the teacher will find it desirable to bring out the fact that almost invariably their speech selections combine information with opportunities to participate in an interesting manner in new and obviously helpful activities. 

For example, in the first speech selection in this section, "In the Speech Laboratory," pupils are taken "behind the scenes" to observe activities and methods of speech teaching that they have probably never had any contact with before. From reading about these speech experiences of others they can gain a new and interesting insight into the possibilities of speech training, and a new appreciation of the importance of a subject which is worthy of such thorough and scientific methods. Here they will gain the satisfaction and genuine joy one finds in information. 

In the second speech selection in this section, "The Oxford Recitations," pupils will find the combination of information as it is given in the Preparatory Note, and the pleasures of participation as they may be had in speaking the poem chorally according to the directions. Then after their participation, they will find at the end of the poem questions and statements which will make clear to them the ways and methods whereby they may improve their performances and add to their information regarding choral speaking. 

The other aspect of enjoyment to be gained through speech selections, and through all selections that may be read orally, is one which comes through the oral interpretation of literature. As has been pointed out before, the teacher should take every opportunity for making use of this approach to literature. In many of the stories in this section it is possible to help pupils deepen their appreciation of characters and action by encouraging them to read aloud such bits of dialogue as will permit them to project themselves into the characters who are speaking. 

Throughout this book, and in the teaching of all reading, this contribution that oral interpretation of literature makes should be appreciated by teacher and pupils alike. 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. In accomplishing this purpose the pupils must first become convinced that 140

reading has for them definite values which they may achieve through learning how to read well. In helping pupils become convinced of this basic and impelling truth, the teacher will want to discuss with them certain benefits through knowledge and increased understanding that they have gained through reading. In this connection she may want to go back with them over some of their achievements in the previous section of this book, or in some other book or books that they have read or read parts of recently. Then in developing this purpose in this section the teacher will indicate, whenever the material makes it reasonable, that here, too, they are reading selections that make their work worth while. For example, in the selection "At School on the Middle Border," they learned something about schools in our own country in the last quarter of the last century. This is historical information, as well as personal information of the author. Thus in this one story they are reading a combination of autobiography and history. Many other stories in this section contain equal values. 

The grouped poems in this section should be taught with the thought that schools are places where boys and girls are helped in their thinking, and encouraged to think. Teaching the poems in connection with the purpose of cultivating worth-while reading interests will mean stressing the fact that poems, in addition to their frequent beauty, are usually messages of basic or delightful truth; that each of these poems, though each is short, contains a thought or a few thoughts, that everyone can understand, and that everyone will appreciate. There is nothing trivial here, nothing difficult. In experiencing such poetry pupils will find it easy to free themselves from the suspicion that some of them have that poetry is not only rather meaningless, but definitely not worth while. 

The teacher, through helping the pupils see more in poetry than the jingle of verse, will gradually make it possible for all pupils to include poetry among their worth-while reading interests. 

3. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in other people's experiences. Among the most available values of reading and literature is the opportunity provided for readers to share the experiences that others have in stories and poems. Pupils find this sharing an easy and profitable process. It will be necessary only for the teacher to point out that such sharing has great value in bringing them information and enhanced pleasure through their reading, and to indicate a few examples that show how easy it is for readers to identify themselves with the thoughts and actions of people read about. "The Outsider," "Split 

Cherry Tree,” “The Last Lesson,” and “Mary” are well suited to the carrying out of this purpose. 

Through pointing out the possible ways in which one may increase 141

his ability to take part in the experiences of others, the teacher will add to the pupil's opportunities to widen his point of view and to deepen his understanding regarding the thoughts and activities of others. At the same time that he increases this ability he increases his respect for literature, which through its broad appeal brings him so much in which he can find both pleasure and information. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will read the two paragraphs with and to the pupils and help them review the stories and poems that they have read in this section. The purpose of this review will be to help the pupils find satisfying conclusions regarding the general worth of the section and the specific contributions of the selections. In using the section study exercises the teacher will be careful to see that pupils gain from them such increase in reading skills as it is possible to gain, and such help in summarizing their reading as will provide them with a clear memory of what they have read. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making this check the teacher should bear in mind that two general aspects of reading should be considered: (1) the attitudes and appreciations and (2) the reading skills and abilities. There can be no objective certainty regarding the increase in right attitudes and appreciations, but the teacher will know, if she knows her pupils, whether progress is being gained in that direction. If it is not, she will have to take suitable means to improve the situation. These means should be directed toward an attempt to help pupils to enjoy reading, and to realize that if they enjoy reading all these other benefits will come to them. If some pupils are found deficient in reading skills the teacher should, through further use of the exercise material in the section or through supplementary exercises of her own devising, bring such pupils up to levels that they are capable of achieving. One of the main purposes of this check is to make sure that no pupils be required to proceed in the book with the handicap of accumulated reading deficiencies. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher, having studied the book list previously, will know which books are available to pupils in the local libraries. 

With the pupils she will want to add to the list such books as she and they know are available. Pupils who have read such books may be asked to write brief descriptions of them for the benefit of other pupils. 

The teacher will want to point out at this time that one of the purposes of a reading and literature book is to encourage pupils to read elsewhere along the lines that interest them. 142

SECTION THREE-HOBBIES FOR LEISURE MOMENTS 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading pupils may find information that guides them to an interest and skill in occupations for their leisure time. The section consists of the following selections: 

Speeding Hours (p. 174) 

Marionettes (pp. 175-178)

A Library of One's Own (pp. 178-181) 

A Punch and Judy Show (Speech) (pp. 182-183) 

My Doll's Theater (pp. 184-186) 143

Wild Brother as an Engineer (pp. 187-192) 

Old Shoes (p. 192) 

Pipes of Pan from Bottles (pp. 193-196) 

Treasure Trove (pp. 197-198)

Section Review (pp. 199-200) 

Purpose. In presenting Section Three the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purpose: 

To create genuine joy in reading.

1. Use of the introductory selection—"Speeding Hours." The teacher will consider with the pupils the short sentence that introduces this section, and discuss with them the significance of the title, "Speeding Hours." By mentioning some of the ways in which pleasure and action have combined in their experience, pupils will almost certainly lead in a natural way to a discussion of some of the hobbies that they have or have had. With the discussion so directed, the teacher will use the small pictures to help guide the thinking further along the lines of hobbies and hobby interests. 

2. A preview of the section. In making the customary preview of the section, the teacher will as usual touch briefly upon the content and nature of each selection, making comments with enthusiasm that will inspire pupil-interest in reading later these briefly mentioned selections. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section several of the selections have a high correlation with the subject of manual arts and, secondarily, with music. A list of such relationships appears at the end of the third . book division of this monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, supplementing the information given if for her particular group such additional material may for any reason be necessary. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will make the customary use of the Study Exercises, making sure that they serve the dual purpose of deepening appreciations and adding to proficiency in reading skills. 

Specific references to exercises that contribute to reading skills appear at the end of each section in this monograph. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher will remember the importance of keeping pupils aware of the section theme, and the manner in which each selection contributes to this section theme or meaning. This is for the purpose of making sure that reading is not haphazard and isolated. 144

The teacher will find it easy to develop the means whereby this purpose may be achieved. She may, for example, indicate as a general truth that reading about hobbies brings joy in at least two ways, one of which is through increasing the appreciation of everyone for all hobbies read about. Thus, when one sees a marionette or puppet show, even though he is not a puppeteer himself or a maker of “little people,” he has a deeper appreciation of the work that went into the accomplishments of those who are putting on the show. This same thought may be applied to the other selections. 

The other aspect of value, and of finding joy in this material, comes more directly, through pupil participation in the hobbies suggested here. 

There is probably not enough information in any one of these selections to provide the pupils with complete guides and rules for carrying out the hobbies mentioned. However, the teacher may point out that throughout this book one of the major purposes is to indicate that after reading something that interests them, pupils may easily find more interesting material on the same lines. 

For example, anyone who wants to know more about marionettes may find the book mentioned in the Preparatory Note, Hobbies for

Everybody, or other books from the book list at the end of the section. 

If anyone is impelled to start a library of his own he may, on the impetus of the ideas found in the library selection, easily proceed to set about building up his own. There is much material regarding Punch and Judy plays, and on the basis of this funny presentation, pupils may build up their own programs and give their own performances. And so throughout the section, using each selection as impetus for further information, pupils may find hobbies that appeal to them and in which they may take further pleasure.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, reading with the pupils the paragraphs that come above the section study exercises, helping them to look back upon the content of the section and come to helpful conclusions about it. Doing the section study exercises should be part of the attempt to round out the section with meaning, and to carry from it a keen memory of the pleasures to be found in this kind of reading. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make the customary check at this time for the purpose of making sure that none 145

of her pupils are accumulating reading deficiencies which will cause them trouble in their further reading. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher, with the pupils, will add other titles to this list of books which they know are available in school or community libraries. In this connection the teacher may want to remind the pupils once more that the reading they are now doing is not complete in itself, but is to be thought of as opening up whole new fields of reading interests.

SECTION FOUR-THE WORLD AT WORK 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through a comprehensive picture of the world at work pupils will increase their understanding of an important aspect of world activity. The section consists of the following selections: 

Work Done Squarely (p. 202) 

Pioneers! O Pioneers! (pp. 203-204) 

The Riverman (pp. 205-213) 

Shanty Boy (pp.214-223) 

The Coromandel Fishers (p. 224) 

Mary-'Gusta Keeps a Secret (pp. 225-231) 

As You Speak, So You Are (Speech) (pp. 232-234) 

Quality (pp. 235-241) 

Caliban in the Coal Mines (p. 242) 

Brin (pp. 243-249)

My Financial Career (pp. 250-252) The Height of the Ridiculous (p. 252) Laughter on the Air (Speech) (pp. 253-255) 

"In the Beginning All Things Are Difficult" (pp. 256-266)

Goethals: The Prophet Engineer (pp. 266-267) 

Big Brother (pp. 268-273) 

Heard in Hull House (Speech) (pp. 274-275) 

Sheriff Roosevelt and the Thieves (pp. 276-280)

Work: A Song of Triumph (pp. 281-282) 

Pushcart Row (pp. 282-284) 

Fish Crier (p. 284)

The Flower Factory (pp. 284-285) A Consecration (p. 285) Section Review (pp. 287-288) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Four the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

 1. Use of the introductory poem "Work Done Squarely." The teacher in reading the introductory poem to and with the pupils will bring out the various thoughts contained in the poem, with emphasis on the two basic thoughts concerning devotion to duty and the need to do work honestly and without waste. She will want to discuss with the pupils several of the phrases in this poem to make certain that vocabulary difficulties are not keeping them from a full understanding of the mean- 147

ing. She will want to indicate on the basis of this poem and of the pictures which surround it, that the stories and poems in this section are going to be concerned with work. 

2. A preview of the section. In making the preview with and for the pupils the teacher will want to keep in mind the desirability of speaking with such pertinency and enthusiasm that the pupils will want to read later the selections she is briefly mentioning, and to keep in mind also the need of reminding the pupils from time to time of the basic meaning or theme of the section and the manner in which the selections contribute to this section meaning. 

She may indicate, for example, that the poem "Pioneers, O Pioneers" refers forcefully to those who did some of the first work in this country; that the story "The Riverman" not only shows men at work but has a dramatic and exciting plot; that the story "Shanty Boy" tells about one of the most interesting figures in American imaginative literature; that "Mary-'Gusta Keeps a Secret" takes place, as they can see by the illustration, in a grocery store and does more than show the workings of such a store; that the selection "As You Speak, So You Are" is a part of the Speech Improvement Program, and contains much that is helpful in increasing speaking abilities; that "Quality," a great story by a famous author, shows the difference between working methods of a few years ago and the methods now; that the poem "Caliban in the Coal Mines" is a plea by a poet for fairness in work. 

And so the teacher may proceed through the section, making brief comments that will increase pupils' desire to read the selections in this section. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Several of the selections in this section have high correlation with other school subjects. A list containing such relationships appears at the end of this third book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, supplementing them, if it is ever necessary for particular groups, with additional material that she may know her pupils need. There are several autobiographical letters in this section which add interest and information to the preparation for pupil reading. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the use of the 

Study Exercises in the customary manner, remembering their dual purpose of helping pupils acquire and maintain reading skills and abilities in appreciation and understanding. A list of exercises contributing to the skills taught in this section appears at the end of the section. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the reading proceeds the teacher will from time to time make certain that the pupils are aware of the partic- 148

ular goals and purposes of this section. The value of this awareness lies in the fact that it gives direction to pupil reading, and eliminates the possibility of reading done in isolation without appreciation of its contribution to the whole section meaning. 

 The teacher will remember the need of reminding pupils that joy is not merely a surface pleasure, but that it can be, when it is genuine, a deep and lasting value. She will remind them also that that joy-genuine joy—is the one with which they are concerned throughout this reading, although the lesser joys, the surface joys, are always automatically present. 

In this section the main joy is that which one finds in the accomplishment of good work. This concept appears time after time throughout the section and should be stressed in this connection for the transferred joy it gives to those who read about it. As an enrichment of reading, the teacher may well point out from time to time also the joy which those who do work have in its doing, and the joy that the pupils have, or may have, in the doing of their own work in a way that leads to the joys of satisfaction.

The point may also be made that practically all the work done in this section involves hardships for the workers. This thought is also one which the teacher may use in personal application to the experiences of her pupils, in helping them appreciate the thought that good things are not easily won either in work or in play. This will, of course, be an old idea to the pupils, and it should not be presented as a moral preachment. It should rather be mentioned as one more indication, among the many they have had, that the values and offerings of reading and literature are practically limitless-that one who reads well finds through his reading the truth and information of the world. Most of the selections in the section lend themselves to the carrying out of this purpose. 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. The material of this section lends itself well to the carrying out of this second purpose. Pupils need to know the importance of work, and the fact that everywhere throughout the world people are working for the same reasons that impel the working of those whom they know locally. This is an aspect of social understanding that pupils are prone to overlook or neglect because to most of them work has been something in the distant future with which they feel at this time no relevent concern. For this reason it is always difficult for children to appreciate the importance that work has to workers, as a means of contributing to their integrity 149

of personality, as well as the more immediate means of contributing to their day-to-day existence. Therefore this thought should be stressedthat people throughout the world do work, and that they have to work. 

Through a heightened appreciation of this fact, and through an increased knowledge of it, pupils will add to their social understanding and to the development of their characters. 

In such a story as "Mary-'Gusta Keeps a Secret," for example, the fact may be brought out that Mary-'Gusta was not working entirely because she liked to, but at least in part because it was her duty, because it was necessary for her to contribute in this way to an enterprise which helped bring people a living.

In the story "Quality” various thoughts are possible. Many readers may think, and perhaps rightly, that the tragedy of these workers lay in their inability to keep up with modern methods. Those who think this are aware of other aspects of the world's work, and so far as this section is concerned they can make their contribution through this interpretation. However, the more important truth regarding these men is that the only thing they thought of was making the finest product possible regardless of machinery and other changes. This is a quality of man that lies at the base of all the finest achievements in work. Through understanding this, pupils gain in their perception of social truths. 

The story "Brin" helps pupils realize two vital aspects of one general subject—that there are people in the world who need assistance, and that there are other people in the world willing and able to give it. This fact, based upon the evidence as it appears in the story "Brin," may be used as the basis for a helpful discussion of the general subject of helping those less fortunate than ourselves. Such discussion, engaged in by interested pupils, can go a long way toward promoting social understanding and developing character. 

And thus it is possible to go through the entire section, helping pupils see the essential facts of every selection as they apply to situations which contribute to the achievement of this purpose.

The contribution made by the speech selections in this section are also valuable in this connection. Throughout the book and throughout the series Program for Speech Improvement the speech material is presented for its practical and observable values. In this section those values are obvious. The first selection, "As You Speak, So You Are," based upon the material of the story which precedes it, contributes to an understanding of a situation by stressing the variety of speech present in that story. 

On the thesis that as one is so he speaks, the characters from the story appear in this article only through what they say, and it is proved that through what they say and how they say it their entire characters are 150

portrayed. This is not only a definite speech lesson in that it makes pupils aware that they must be careful of their speech; it also contributes to the achievement of the section purposes and the theme of this section in that if we understand people's speech, and if people speak as they are, then we are helped to understand people and therefore social situations in which people take part. This speech selection should be studied from all these angles.

The selection "Laughter on the Air" takes its start from the Leacock story but, leaving that, develops its own ideas. These ideas are concerned with the manner in which humor may be used to contribute to the general pleasure of listeners. The pupil may be reminded that thousands of people are now engaged in the various aspects of the gigantic industry of radio, and that everything they learn concerning this subject is valuable information concerning the work of the world and the theme of this section. The selection also, in its attempt to contribute its share to the Speech Improvement Program, makes an appeal for the appreciation of genuine humor. The teacher should stress, and the pupils should appreciate, these various aspects of speech as developed in this selection. 

The third speech selection, "Heard in Hull House," follows the story about Jane Addams of Hull House and contributes in its own way to the thought of that story and the theme of the section. The teacher should point out that in getting and holding good jobs it is essential to have adequate speech abilities. Discussions in connection with this selection, regarding jobs in which good speech is especially necessary, will be helpful in establishing the thought of this article in pupils' mind. It should be pointed out to them that foreigners living in our American communities have justification for misusing our language, but that most of us who are guilty of the same misuse do not have any justification at all. 

Thus it may be seen that all these speech selections play their part not only in the development of speech improvement, but also in the development of the theme meaning. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, reading its introductory parts to and with the pupils, helping them form useful conclusions regarding the section they have just read. In using the section study exercises pupils should attempt to fix in their minds the helpful memory of the general thoughts that stand out in the selections in this section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher should at this time, through observation and testing, make sure that the pupils are 151

achieving levels suitable to their abilities. If she finds some pupils who are not doing as well as they should, she will make every effort to raise their skills to proper levels and not permit them to accumulate reading difficulties which will cause them trouble later. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will make the customary use of the book list, presenting it to the pupils with the encouragement that they read such books as are available and that they find, with her help, other suitable books that are available but may not be on this list.
