SECTION FIVE-THE LURE OF DISTANT PLACES 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading of faraway places pupils will gain a more accurate perspective of the extent and variety of the world. The section consists of the following selections: 

Adventure (p. 290) 

Southward Bound (pp. 291-298) 

A Close Call (pp. 299-307) 

It Happened in Rome (Speech) (pp. 308-310) 

A Guide in Genoa (pp. 311-314)

Robinson Crusoe's Story (p. 315) 

A Wonderer under the Sea (pp. 316-321) 

Going Up to London (pp. 322-323)

A Race for the Swift (pp. 324-330) 

Sounds in a Chinese Street (p. 331) 

Strange Scene in China (pp. 332-337)

Why Go Places? (p. 337) 

Autumn (p. 338) 

Jungle Byways in India (pp. 339-345)

Voices from the Holy Land (Speech) (pp. 346-349) 

Troy Unearthed (pp. 350-354) 

Climbing Mount Olympus (pp. 354-364)

Travel (p. 365) 

The Sea Gypsy (p. 365) 

The Tea Trader (p. 366)

The Ice-Cart (pp. 366-367) Section Review (pp. 368-370) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Five the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in other people's experiences.

1. Use of the introductory poem-"Adventure." The teacher will read the poem "Adventure" to and with the pupils, bringing out the con- 153

nection between its meaning and the section theme as suggested by the section title, "The Lure of Distant Places." In addition to such use of the poem itself, the teacher will point out that the pictures in the border show various phases of adventure and distant places. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will direct the pupils through a preview of this section for the purpose of indicating to them with enthusiasm the selections which they will read later with interest. 

This process, though it should be brief, should be extensive, and refer with one or two pertinent remarks to each selection in the section. In addition to indicating the nature of the selection, the teacher will also indicate that each selection contributes to the section theme. She will not, during this process, take time to do more than say that certain things are true. All proof of such assertions will come in later aspects of the procedure. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section are several selections which have a high correlation with other school subjects. A list of such relationships appears at the end of this third book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, supplementing them at any time when it appears that pupils in her particular group may need additional preparation. Pupils will not need this additional work often, but it is important that they have it when they do need it. The teacher will find it easy to supply such material on the basis of the Preparatory Notes themselves. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will make the customary use of the Study Exercises, bearing in mind that they are intended to serve as helps to pupils in their general understanding of what they have read, and in the increase and maintenance of their specific reading skills. 

They should always be used as positive helps, and never thought of as meaningless burdens. At the end of each section, exercises are listed in connection with the skills in which they give specific training. 

6. Awareness of goals. As the teacher proceeds through the section, she will remain herself aware of the goals and purposes of the section, and encourage this awareness on the part of the pupils. This is for the purpose of directing the learning toward definite aims, and eliminating the danger of studying in isolation material that should be connected with the basic section purposes. 

 Various kinds of genuine joy may be found in reading this section. The underlying joy of the entire 154

section is one that nearly all readers, either pupils or adults, find in going far away in their imagination to visit distant scenes and meet strange people. This joy involves not only the pleasures of arm-chair travel, but also the deeper pleasure of really understanding what previously they had had mere glimpses of. For example, the teacher may point out that nearly all pupils have from time to time thought how interesting it would be to go by dog sled into the little-known frozen extremities of our earth.

Yet no pupil has actually made this trip, and relatively few of them have even accompanied others through reading. Therefore for most pupils it will be a new and genuine joy to go with John O'Brien as he accompanies Byrd into the cold distance. Pupils have here not only the joy that is found in legitimate excitement, but the joy that is found in new and congenial information. This same type of joy may be found in the autobiographical story, "A Close Call," and in most of the other stories and poems in the section. 

The teacher will find it desirable to keep the pupils aware of the difference between mere surface joy, which is also present, and the genuine joy which they may find beneath the surface. 

2. To increase pupils' ability to take part vicariously in other people's experiences. It should be pointed out to pupils in connection with the achieving of this purpose that one of the great values of literature is its capacity for permitting countless readers to take part in the activities it portrays, and to know the people it pictures. Every author expects to take his readers with him. Every reader should enter his reading experiences with the hope and expectation that he is going to find it possible to go somewhere with somebody. Even in a personal story such as that which Amundsen tells, many readers will find it possible to be lost with him in the snows of Norway. This is a straightforward account, yet it has this appeal, this power to carry readers with it. The other selections in this section have similar appeal. 

Through taking part in the words, gestures, and attitudes in the speech selection, "It Happened in Rome," pupils will find it possible to transpose themselves into this amusing scene. In all dramatics, short or long, it is expected that participators shall submerge themselves into the personalities of those who are represented. It is quite possible in many selections for readers to make this transfer, even though the material is not written in play form. When characters speak in stories, readers should be able to see and hear themselves in similar roles speaking similarly. Training in speech which adds to this ability is of value. 

It is another form of contribution to the enhancement of literature through oral interpretation. 

The other speech selection in this section, "Voices from the Holy 155

Land," also contributes an opportunity for pupils to learn in what ways they may take more vital parts in what they read. The two selections here, both psalms, permit actual participation in the voicing of lines which by their oral interpretation become more meaningful than they had ever been before. In the explanation given here of choral speaking, pupils will find new facts about this subject, new information which leads them to greater skill in choral participation, and by those means to greater skills in appreciating and understanding literature. The informative paragraphs should be read with care, and supplemented by discussion directed by the teacher, for the purpose of giving the pupils a total picture of the values of this aspect of their Speech Improvement Program. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will direct the customary use of the section review, reading to and with the pupils the three paragraphs above the section study exercises, and helping them find suitable conclusions to the thoughts they have had regarding the material in this section and the section theme itself. Doing the section study exercises will add to the effectiveness of these conclusions, and make it possible for pupils to impress upon their memories the values of this reading that they should carry on through all their future reading. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making this customary check the teacher will make certain that all her pupils are adequately equipped with helpful attitudes toward their reading, and make sure also that they are not accumulating specific reading difficulties that will detract from their future reading success. If any pupils are deficient in their reading skills, or possess unsatisfactory attitudes, the teacher will spend sufficient time with them in further work to bring them to their own proper levels. 

3. Use of book list. There are many books related to the subject of this section. The teacher will go over the list here, indicating to the pupils which books are available to them, and add with the pupils other books that she and they may know are available in community and school libraries.

Exercises for training in reading skills-Section Five-Book Three

SECTION SIX-WORLD COMMUNICATION 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading pupils can gain a better understanding of the means, extent, and importance of communication throughout the world. The section consists of the following selections: 

A Word (p. 372) To the Uttermost Ends of the Earth (pp. 373-379) The Raven's First Flight (pp. 379-385) The Spirit of the Night Air Mail (pp. 385-390) 

Spreading the News (pp. 391-401)

Other Ways of Spreading the News (Speech) (pp. 402-406) Your Newspaper (pp. 407-411) 

Words That Laugh and Cry (pp. 412-413) 

All for the Sake of a News Beat (pp. 414-415) 

Grinding out the Newsreel (pp. 416-421) 

The Gold Brick (pp. 422-434) 

Section Review (pp. 434-436)

Purposes. In presenting Section Six the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. 

3. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking.

1. Use of the introductory poem-"A Word." In reading this brief poem to and with the pupils the teacher will point out the charm of its delicate brevity, as well as the significance it has as an introduction to this section. Discussions concerning the importance of the word as the core of communication will be helpful. Through the use of the small pictures in the border the teacher will find it easily possible to indicate that there are various ways of communicating, and that some of them seem to be rather exciting. 

2. A preview of the section. The purpose of the preview will be, as usual, to encourage an enthusiastic anticipation of material to be read in this section. The teacher will, by a careful choice of brief remarks, contribute to this enthusiasm and at the same time indicate to the pupils that each of these selections contributes to the basic theme or idea of the section.

She may, for example, point out that the article "To the Uttermost Ends of the Earth" tells about the famous scientist Marconi and his 158

invention of wireless; that "The Raven's First Flight" concerns the use of radio and other methods of communication; that "The Spirit of the 

Night Air Mail" will show them the contribution made by airplanes in communication; that the play "Spreading the News" is funny, but that it also shows one way of communicating; that "Other Ways of Spreading the News" is a selection in their Speech Improvement Program that gives them helpful and accurate information about radio newscasts; that the article "Your Newspaper" helps them understand more about this means of communication.

And so the teacher will proceed through the section, giving the pupils some idea of what they are to read, and helping them look forward with pleasure to reading it. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section several selections having a high correlation with general science appear. A list of such relationships appears at the end of this third book division in the monograph. 4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, supplementing them whenever it seems necessary for members of her particular group. It is quite possible that in this section preliminary discussions may be helpful in supplying additional information for certain pupils in the class whose interest in scientific and semi-scientific matters has not been sufficient to give them an adequate background for understanding this type of material. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the customary use of the exercises, remembering that they are expected to increase the pupils' general abilities and appreciation, and to improve their specific reading skills. At the end of each section, page references are made to the exercises that contribute to specific reading skills. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher will herself remain constantly aware of the section goals and purposes and the manner in which the selections contribute to the achievement of these goals, and she will also keep the pupils aware of the value of directing their reading toward definite accomplishments.

 Probably the most effective and obvious way of helping pupils see joy in this material is to point out the fact that we all enjoy things more when we understand them. This fact may be supplemented by pointing out that this is especially true when those things we want to understand are part, directly and indirectly, of our everyday lives, and that knowing about them contributes to the 159

pleasure we may have in feeling entirely familiar with the other things that are a part of our world.

Probably the best approach to this aspect of joy can be made through the radio, since it is the method of communication with which pupils are most interestedly acquainted. The selections that may be used in this connection in this section are "The Raven's First Flight," and the speech selection "Other Ways of Spreading the News." Each of these selections contributes in an interesting manner to one's knowledge of the radio. 

Pupils should be encouraged to think that since they use the radio so much they really should know something about it, so that they may appreciate its use more than they do. This same approach may be used in discussing newspapers, newsreels, and other agencies of communication. 

2. To cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. The achievement of this purpose gives pupils added confidence that what they read may have real value for them. One of the most obvious values for pupils can be indicated through the connection between what they read and possible methods of making a living. This connection can be illustrated through use of the speech selection, “Other Ways of Spreading the News." In this selection they will find the basic facts necessary to a thorough understanding of what people must know in order to make effective broadcasts of the news. All pupils know how valuable such broadcasts are, and all pupils realize how interested they and other members of their families are in broadcasts of the news. They are aware also of the vast number of people who are engaged in the work of radio, either in this connection or through other phases of this means of communication. Thus it is easy for them to realize that reading about the radio is worth while.

Using this thought as a basis for further development, it can be pointed out by the teacher and readily accepted by the pupils, that reading about other forms of communication makes other work of the world in this line equally understandable. 

When pupils have learned that their reading can bring them, and does bring them, such vivid practical information, they are helped to realize that through reading and literature it is possible to cultivate permanent worth-while reading interests. In a section which lends itself so well to the practical contributions that reading has, it is desirable that the teacher concentrate on this angle, saving other aspects of worthwhile reading interests for the types of material that will bring out such aspects more naturally. 

3. To develop systematically the fundamental attitudes and habits of reading and speaking. It is helpful to develop this purpose in con- 160

nection with the pupils' awareness that reading can be worth while on a practical basis. When pupils have such an understanding of the possibilities of reading, they are likely to be enthusiastic about establishing the attitudes and habits that will help them most effectively to find the values of reading. The teacher may, in this connection, point out that scientists and inventors like Marconi must be precise thinkers, that they must train their minds to study and perform their experiments with the most careful regard for detail. In this connection the teacher may point out that it is equally true that pupils, in reading about such subjects, must bring to their study careful habits of reading and thinking. a. Reading. In this connection the teacher will point out that those who best understand the scientific details in the article "To the Uttermost Ends of the Earth" received the greatest pleasures and benefits from the reading of that article. Knowing as she will that some pupils got too little from their reading, while others got practically all there was to be had from it, she will indicate this fact and suggest the reasons for this fact. This will be done with the hope that those whose benefits have been too few will be encouraged in the future to read with greater care so that their benefits may be more. In using such articles as this one to stress the general need of careful reading, the teacher can give valuable instruction concerning reading habits as they apply to all reading. 

In reading stories like "The Spirit of the Night Air Mail" pupils will immediately recognize that here they find a combination that they have often found before, of fact and narrative. This type of article has something for everyone. Those incapable of appreciating the service of planes as distributors of mail, can nevertheless be entirely engrossed in the narrative. In this connection the teacher should point out that much reading consists of such combinations, and that if they are to become wholly successful readers pupils must practice the reading habits, and must adopt the reading attitudes, that permit them to get the most out of such material. This point will be stressed equally in the selections "Your Newspaper," "All for the Sake of a News Beat," and "Grinding out the Newsreel." b. Speaking. Good attitudes toward speech make speech a pleasure. 

When good speech is a pleasure, good speaking habits are helpful in making it a pleasure more keenly felt. Good speech attitudes and habits can be encouraged through the oral reading of "The Raven's First Flight" and "Spreading the News." In the radio play the teacher may point out the necessity of appreciating the background upon which it is based. 

In this connection discussions of Poe, his personality and works, will be helpful in establishing the character that is presented in the radio skit. 

The attitudes and habits to be stressed here concern a desire and ability of 161

those who take parts representing other people to submerge their own personalities in favor of honest presentations of those whom they represent. The attitude toward such participation should be one of pleasure, and for nearly all pupils it will be. 

In the oral presentation of "Spreading the News," the teacher should encourage pupils to feel the spirit of fun that pervades the play, and to read their parts in ways that contribute to the amusing situations and the amusing lines. Here again the attitudes and habits of speech that should be encouraged are those which permit and require that pupils train themselves to slip into the character of others. Such attitudes and habits require special study of character so that pupil actors may speak as real interpreters of others.

Through the use of the speech selection, "Other Ways of Spreading the News," the teacher will have opportunity to establish attitudes and habits that will lead to precise efficiency through mastery of information, and through practice in performance. As has been pointed out before, pupils will have in connection with this selection an impetus that comes from their understanding of its practical worth. The teacher will need merely to indicate, in connection with achieving this third purpose, that if pupils are to find the true value of what they read, they must first have proper attitudes and habits of speech. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will direct the customary use of the section review, reading to and with the pupils the three paragraphs above the section study exercises for the purpose of establishing the pupils' thoughts concerning the material they have read in this section. In directing the exercises the teacher will bear in mind, and help the pupils remember, that the main purposes of the exercises are to summarize their abilities for appreciation and make obvious their progress in reading skills. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will make the customary check at this time on the progress of the pupils toward competence in abilities of appreciation and skills in reading. If it is found that some pupils are below the level they are capable of reaching, the teacher will want, through directing the further use of the exercises, or through additional exercise material of her own, to bring such pupils up to the levels of which they are individually capable. 

3. Use of book list. The customary use of the book list will be made, with the teacher and pupils adding to the list such books as are available in community or school libraries. 162

SECTION SEVEN-WORLD STORIES AND POEMS 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading the kinds of stories and poems the world has long enjoyed pupils will gain a better understanding of the meaning of literature. The section consists of the following selections: 

World Tales (p. 438) 

The Lowliest Friend (pp. 439-441) 

Look to This Day (p. 442)

Odysseus Meets the Cyclops (pp. 443-456) 

Hebrew Poetry (p. 457)

How Alexander Won His War Steed (pp. 458-460) 

Androclus and the Lion (pp. 461-463) 

The Rime of the Ancient Mariner (pp. 464-486) 

On the Track of Moby Dick (pp. 487-497) 

The Battle of Blenheim (pp. 497-498) 

Let's Make a Play (Speech) (pp. 499-500)

Jean Valjean and the Bishop (pp. 501-515) 

The Father (pp. 515-517) 

Get Up and Bar the Door (pp. 518-519) 

Whoopee Ti Yi Yo, Git Along Little Dogies (pp. 519-520) 

Blow the Man Down (pp. 520-521) 

Song of the Rain Chant (p. 521)

Traditional Negro Spirituals (p. 522) Section Review (pp. 524-526) 

Purposes. In presenting Section Seven the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste.

 1. Use of the introductory selection-"World Tales." In reading this introductory sentence to and with the pupils the teacher will point out its obviously perfect choice as an introduction to the idea that stories and poems have always had the power to hold the attention of readers or listeners. Through the use of the pictures in the border the teacher will give added information regarding the type of material to be read in this section. 

2. A preview of the section. In making the customary preview of the section the teacher will indicate briefly to the pupils the nature of each selection and its contribution to the section theme. She will want to indicate also that these selections that people have loved and admired 164

for many years originated in various places, and that this fact in itself is another proof of how general the appeal of literature is, and how necessary people everywhere have found it. 

She may point out, for example, that "The Lowliest Friend" is a dog story that comes from the ancient Hindus of India; that the narrative poem, "Odysseus Meets the Cyclops" is a part of a far longer poem of action and word pictures translated from the ancient Greek language; that the story "How Alexander Won His War Steed," by a famous Greek biographer, Plutarch, tells about the boy who, when he grew up, regretted that there were no more worlds for him to conquer; that "Androclus and the Lion" is an animal story translated from a famous French writer; that "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner" is a magical narrative poem of strange action in weird places; that "On the Track of Moby 

Dick" is taken from a long American story which some people call the finest novel ever written in America.

And thus the teacher may proceed through the section, pointing out pertinent facts about each selection and encouraging the pupils to look forward with interest to reading them later. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. Only a few of the selections in this section have a close correlation with other school subjects. These selection titles are listed at the end of this third book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will direct the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, making sure that all pupils have an adequate understanding of the background of what they are to read. The 

Notes in this section are more extensive than they have been in other sections where the material was more familiar. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The teacher will direct the customary use of the Study Exercises throughout this section, presenting them as supplementary means of helping pupils understand what they read and of adding to their efficiency in reading. At the end of each section exercises are listed in connection with the skills in which they give specific training. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher will keep the pupils aware of the particular goals that they are seeking through their reading. She will indicate from time to time the connection between the specific selections and the section theme or meaning. This awareness is calculated to lessen the possibility of reading in isolation material that should be considered part of a basic thought pattern. 

 In other sections it has been possible to point out that part of the joy of reading was in the discovery 165

that familiar things were frequently the subjects of writing. It could be expected that some pupils would be to some extent familiar with the background of much of what they read. It was possible in dealing with such material to make use of this helpful familiarity. In this section, however, it is not at all likely that pupils will feel this natural acquaintance with the material. Here it is possible to encourage an acquaintance with a new kind of genuine joy. One aspect of this joy lies in the pleasure readers may have in realizing that stories based on the truth of the thoughts and actions of human beings may bring deep satisfactions. This aspect of enjoyment is not limited to the material in this section, but here it appears in such concentrated form that it may be discussed effectively.

It may be pointed out, for example, that the power and beauty of the story "The Lowliest Friend" lies in an understanding of the human fact that people are devoted to whatever is faithful to them. In the poem "Odysseus Meets the Cyclops" one finds the natural joy to be had from narrative poetry and in the Hebrew poetry one finds joy that is present in the lyric spirit. In the article "How Alexander Won His War 

Steed" it is possible to see how through the use of his understanding a youth succeeded. The basis of the brief tale "Androclus and the Lion" is similar to that in the first story concerning the dog. In the long poem "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner" readers may find reverent joy in the moral thought which comes out in the last words of the mariner. 

And so throughout the section this aspect of joy will be developedthat genuine pleasure is to be found in the works of writers who deal with the hopes and trials and actions and accomplishments of human beings. 

2. To guide and elevate literary taste. This is an extremely important purpose, but it cannot be taught with entire objectivity, nor can its achievement be measured with certainty. The teacher may well prepare the pupils for their work toward the accomplishment of this purpose by discussing some of the terms and goals of literature. 

She may, for example, speak briefly of the need that civilized people have always felt for expressions of the truths of their times, and that as skill in literary expression increased, people, even in ancient times, came to require that literature be not only the truth, but the truth expressed with beauty. Beauty, it may be indicated to the pupils, can be and is, in literature, of many kinds. 

The teacher may in this connection touch lightly upon the subject of style, and point out that the styles of great writings by various authors are different because the authors were different, because each man expressed himself in the way which to him seemed best. She may point 166

out in this connection that although subject matter is always more important than style, real literature is never created unless a successful style accompanies a worthy subject. 

By way of contrast she may point out that the only purpose of an article on how to make a box is to give instructions to that end; but that the purpose of a poet describing the sunrise is to interpret an act of nature in terms of beauty that can be appreciated by readers who never before have seen such beauty in the sun. Literature calls upon readers to see and hear and feel, to use all their senses and their sensibilities to appreciate all aspects of what the writer says. This appreciation demands literary taste.

By way of further contrast the teacher may remind the pupils that those in the group who know the fine points of football, basketball, or baseball, find special enjoyment in the fine and skillful plays of the best performers, whereas those who have only a casual acquaintance with these sports appreciate only the average performances; thus also it can be shown that those who are not familiar with some qualities of style and beauty in literature miss all the fine points of the best writers. Through this contrast, and others that may be made, it can be shown that it is desirable so to increase one's capacity that he can enjoy the best wherever it may appear, in literature or in life. This is the basic motive for the seeking of the goal of improved literary taste. 

And so the teacher will find it possible, by drawing on her own experience and observations, to bring this subject close to her pupils through comparisons and contrasts with everyday aspects of life. 

But it is possible for pupils to ask, and many of the keenest of them will ask, “What is the difference between literature and just plain reading?" That is a proper question, and the teacher should have a convincing answer. The answer may be constructed along these lines: that "just plain reading" has an immediate purpose dealing with instruction or superficial entertainment, whereas the purpose of literature is to interpret world truth and supply lasting assistance in thinking and feeling; that "just plain reading" deals with a subject that has definite and observable limitations, whereas literature deals with subjects whose meanings have universal appeal and whose extent is without limit; that “just plain reading," though it has many merits, lacks usually the merit of style, whereas literature invariably possesses at least a distinctive style, if not a distinguished one.

In support of such ideas, marking off the basic differences between good reading and literature, the teacher will want to make use of definite examples. Considering the story "The Lowliest Friend," she may point out that here is the story of a man and his dog, and yet it is much more 167

than that. We are accustomed to tales of sentiment showing the mutual love of people and animals. However, the shedding of a tear or the longing of the heart is considered adequate to satisfy the requirements of the average story about a man and his dog. Here, however, the whole subject is elevated into the beauty of imagined action and feeling. 

Here is one who without tears is willing to refuse the opportunity for eternal bliss rather than accept it without his dog. This is not thought to be true actually, of course, and literature is never bound to literal truth. This is the kind of philosophical truth and moral truth to which literature is bound. Therefore when the man refused entry into heaven, he does so not merely for a four-legged creature, but to establish the symbol of unwavering friendship considered worthy of the greatest sacrifice this man could make. The fact that the dog was considered not only unimportant, but unclean and unworthy, makes it all the more significant that the dog's master should think of him as a friend and speak of him in the end as "the lowliest friend." And, finally, through this story the universal truth emerges that justice and mercy and faith and sacrifice are everywhere admirable and everywhere sought in all ages by all people who are noble of spirit. By developing the story along these lines, the teacher can indicate how much more there is in it than the bare narrative of a man in India who tried to get into heaven with his dog.

And so the teacher will find it possible to indicate by such means the differences between literature and good reading. The use of a few other examples found in this section or elsewhere will serve to convince pupils that these differences are legitimate ones, and that they will do well to increase their appreciations so that they may gain finer values from their reading in literature.

The teacher will be aware that many other aspects of literature are easily discerned by pupils when they are pointed out-such matters as word pictures in poetry, characterization in poetry and prose and, in regard to the folk poems at the end of the section, naturalness of rhythm, honesty of sentiment, and simplicity of poetic scenes. 

The speech selection in this section, "Let's Make a Play," can add to abilities in the oral interpretation of literature through showing how stories may be converted into creative dramatics. Pupils will find that this kind of dramatics does not require the automatic learning of lines, but does require a thorough understanding of background and honest portrayal of character on the basis of such background; that it requires an understanding of dialogue, the manner and content of the speech of people. Through use of this speech selection pupils will find suggestions for adding to their many techniques of enjoyment this one which makes 168

possible, through oral interpretation, a more intimate acquaintance with nearly all the stories they have read and will be reading. 

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. The teacher will make the customary use of the section review, reading to and with the pupils the three paragraphs above the section study exercises, making sure that pupils appreciate and understand the meaning of the section they have read, and are aware that this reading should lead them to an interest in further reading along the same lines. The use of the section study exercises will contribute to their definite conclusions regarding this material, and clear up any doubts they may have had regarding specific learning in the section. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making this customary check the teacher will make sure that her pupils are not accumulating deficiencies either in attitudes or reading skills that will cause them trouble in their future reading. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher and pupils will realize that books contributing to the thought and content of this section are unusually numerous, and they will find it interesting and helpful to add to this list titles of books that they know are available in their community and school libraries.

SECTION EIGHT-FRIENDSHIP AMONG PEOPLE AND NATIONS 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through reading of friendship among people and nations pupils will realize the importance of the ideals of world peace and justice. The section consists of the following selections: 

For Peace and Understanding (p. 528) 

Falcon Feathers (pp. 529-542) 

On Friendship (pp. 543-546) 

The Mystic Borderland (p. 546) 

The Good River (pp. 547-555)

America on Parade (Speech) (pp. 556-560) A Message of Peace (pp. 561-566) 

Brotherhood (p. 566) 

The Christ of the Andes (pp. 567-571) 

The Citizen (pp. 572-585) 

Ellis Park (p. 585) 

The Silver Mine (pp. 586-597)

Hidden Wealth in Poetry (Speech) (pp. 598-603) 

John Anderson, My Jo (p. 604) 

A Prayer (p. 605) 

Mia Carlotta (p. 605) 

Jaffar (pp. 606-607) 

A Prophecy (p. 607)

Section Review (pp. 608-610) 

170

Purposes. In presenting Section Eight the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

 1. Use of the introductory selection-"For Peace and Understand ing." The teacher may encourage a discussion of the statements contained in the first clause of this sentence by Franklin, inviting the pupils to contribute their thoughts regarding the importance of truth, sincerity, and integrity in their everyday affairs. Then she may lead the discussion toward the transfer of these qualities to the conduct of nations in their dealings with each other, and encourage pupils to come to some preliminary conclusions regarding the value of such qualities in such connections. She may use the pictures in the border to supplement preliminary thinking along the lines of friendship. 

2. A preview of the section. The teacher will follow the customary procedure in making the preview of the section with the pupils, continuing to point out to them the core of interest that each section has and to give them a hint as to the way in which each selection contributes to the section theme or meaning. 

3. Use of helpful correlation. In this section several of the selections have a close correlation with the subject of civics, a correlation which it is helpful to develop in teaching a section of this nature. A list of selections showing this relationship appears at the end of this third book division of the monograph. 

4. Use of Preparatory Notes. The teacher will make the customary use of the Preparatory Notes, making sure that all pupils receive adequate background preparation, either through the notes or through supplementary material in the few cases where that may be necessary. 

5. Use of Study Exercises. The customary use of the Study Exercises will be made to encourage the pupils to acquire accurate and comprehensive understanding of what they have read, and to increase their abilities in appreciation. A list of skills to be sought, and exercises which lead toward their achievement, appears at the end of this section. 

6. Awareness of goals. The teacher will herself remain aware of the goals and purposes of this section, and keep the pupils reminded of these goals and purposes. This awareness serves to provide direction and cogency to the reading, which might otherwise be done in isolation, without regard to the section pattern of thought. 171

It is not difficult to help pupils see joy that may be had in reading about friendship among people and nations. It is the tendency of children to be friendly, and they find pleasure in reading of others who are happy friends. Through the use of the selections in the section, the teacher may make it clear to the pupils that one of our greatest possible joys lies in the satisfaction we may have in appreciating the importance of peace and justice, and in doing what we can, even through fighting, to bring it about. 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. The teacher will indicate that many of the stories and poems in this section make it easy to see why people act as they do, and thus make it possible for readers to understand the motives and character of people. The teacher will want to point out the basic truth that underlies all understanding of people that you must know what they are thinking and why they are thinking as they do, what they have and what they wish they had, what they feel about others and what others have. 

Pupils should be helped also to realize that humanity as such is more important than geographical divisions, and that although geography to some extent governs the activities of people, the basic truths of people are the same throughout the world. An acknowledgment and understanding of this basic truth can be of great assistance in promoting social understanding and developing tolerance and largeness of character. 

The little Chinese girl, Lan Ying, for example, showed characteristics which any nationality would be happy to sponsor, and which any nationality is capable of producing. The splendid Americans depicted in the speech selection “America on Parade,” who show their willingness and capacity to contribute to the welfare of their country, have their counterparts in other countries.

Pupils should be aware that not only in our country are such brave words spoken, but that in all countries such words are spoken by those who love their land as we love ours. There are times when countries are at war, and all energies are directed to the national victory. This fact does not, however, preclude the dream of peace, does not obscure the fact that eventually, through social understanding and character, the world may be a better place. This pupils should learn. This should be their potent guide toward gaining for themselves this increased understanding, toward building for themselves this improved character. 

An added point may be brought out in connection with the second speech selection, "Hidden Wealth in Poetry," that in all countries people sing and love poems which “give them an inside feeling of happiness.” 172

This selection gives pupils assistance in expressing this inside feeling of happiness. The importance of shared aesthetic pleasures should be stressed among the possible methods of assuring understanding. 

Appreciation of literature admits no national boundaries. Many of those admired most in other countries are those who have achieved in literature a distinction which is not merely national, but indicative of the beauty and truth that human beings anywhere can produce. Through providing better methods for interpreting and appreciating the writings of others, better understanding is encouraged. 

In the two selections presented here chorally there is no serious thought, no significant theme, but there is the technique for enjoyment, the technique for oral interpretation, that may be applied to all poems. 

Such application may be made, for example, to the poems in the subsection of love and friendship. The oral interpretation of these poems, with their various backgrounds, will help pupils see that people in different places have the same good, permanent thoughts concerning love and friendship.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. In directing the customary use of the section review the teacher will read to and with the pupils the two paragraphs above the section study exercises, help them understand these paragraphs, and encourage them to look back over their section reading and arrive at certain conclusions regarding what they have read. In doing the actual exercises the teacher should emphasize the fact that these exercises are expected to help them summarize their knowledge and so fix it in their minds that they may make use in the future of the basic thoughts that they have gained here. 

2. Check on section goals and purposes. In making her check at this time, the teacher will realize that the pupils have now finished the last real section of varied selections in their book, and that from this varied reading, it is expected that they have gained various increases in their abilities of appreciation and skills in reading. The teacher may profitably review with pupils at this time the general and specific purposes toward which they have been working throughout the book. She may discuss with them the aspects of joy toward which they have been working; the importance to them of finding worth-while reading interests; the opportunities they have had to guide and elevate their literary taste; the experiences they have shared vicariously with others; the systematic work they have done in establishing attitudes and habits of reading and speaking; the constant, though unannounced, work they 173

have done to increase their efficiency in both oral and silent reading; the awareness they have had of the need to promote in themselves social understanding and to develop character; the evidences they have seen of the real correlation that exists between good reading and good speaking. 

She will, in conclusion of this phase of the pupils' work, want to point out that one more section remains in their book, a section to which, for enjoyment and appreciation, they will want to bring all the fruits of their directed experiences in reading, and that this section contains the play "Julius Caesar," by William Shakespeare. 

3. Use of book list. In making the customary use of the book list the teacher will be able to indicate which of these books are available to pupils through community and school libraries, and to add with and for the pupils titles of other books that are available.

SECTION NINE-JULIUS CAESAR 

The basic thought contributed by the content and study of this section will be that through the reading of this play pupils will deepen their understanding of people and events and increase their appreciation of 

Shakespeare. The section consists of the play, "Julius Caesar."

Purposes. In presenting Section Nine the teacher should work toward the accomplishment of the following purposes: 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. 

Most pupils have an acquaintance with Shakespeare and his works. 

If they have used as a text in literature the volume preceding this one, they have read the play "A Midsummer Night's Dream." If they were taught this play along the lines recommended in that volume for its teaching they have no fears of Shakespeare. If they are now for the first time meeting Shakespeare, or if they have through previous experiences come to fear him, the teacher must make every effort to acquaint them with the possibilities for enjoyment that are offered in Shakespeare's plays.

It is likely that at this time in a pupil's career, the best truth he can learn about Shakespeare's plays is that they are readable, that they have values for him. In guiding pupils toward their experience with 

Shakespeare, and through their reading of his play, "Julius Caesar," the teacher should make every effort to indicate that Shakespeare does not sit on a pedestal above the reach of readers, but that he mingles with them and has the common touch.

It is not advisable for the teacher to stress too much the literary aspects of Shakespeare's excellence. Pupils will be helped in later books to study this phase of his writing. It is essential now in helping the pupils form the right attitude toward Shakespeare that they realize how much he knew about life, how clearly he pictured the ways in which people acted and the reasons for their actions. This is the kind of life truth that pupils have had experience in observing throughout this book. 

They will appreciate Shakespeare when they see what a master he is in depicting the ways of people. 

Throughout this book pupils have been helped to appreciate the value of the oral interpretation of literature. It should be brought to their attention now that the reading of this play can be considered conclusive proof of the values of oral interpretation. There are always enough 175

reasonably competent pupils in any class to take the parts of the players who speak frequently. These participators may be given opportunity for special preparation so that they may read their lines in such a way as to convince themselves and their listeners that they are reading a good play. Further suggestions for presentation will appear later. 

1. Use of the introductory selection-"Strange Sights in Rome."

Through reading the introductory selection to and with the pupils the teacher will indicate that this passage speaks of unusual things, and that it will be interesting to find out in what connection this speech by Casca, one of the characters in the play, is used. This thought may be supplemented by the pictures in the border, all of which contribute to the mysterious meaning of this passage. 

2. A preview of the section. The preview of this section will be different from the previews made in previous sections. Here all subsidiary aspects of study will be considered as a whole. 

The teacher will read to and with the children and discuss with them all the preparatory material on pages 613, 614, 615, 616, 617, and 618.

In connection with the subsection "Shakespeare in the Modern World" the teacher will point out the importance of realizing that Shakespeare's plays are still presented, that they continue to be a living force in the theater. If the teacher has access to advertising material for Shakespeare presentations in her locality, it will be extremely helpful in arousing pupil-interest. If she has phonograph records of famous passages from 

Shakespeare's plays, and facilities for playing them in the classroom, this activity also will be helpful. If she or her pupils have ever heard broadcasts of Shakespearean plays or excerpts from them it will be interesting to discuss such experiences. The teacher should use every opportunity to emphasize the facts that are brought out in these first two paragraphs of the introductory material. 

In connection with the material that tells of England, the theater, and Shakespeare, the teacher should discuss the naturalness of Shakespeare's excellence and the naturalness of his youthful activities. Supplementary material that pupils may find in other reading, or through their other studies, such as history, concerning the England of Shakespeare's day, will be helpful if it is voluntarily sought and reported. Pupils should not be required to seek these further facts as a matter of laborious research. In most classes there will be pupils who are genuinely interested in a project undertaken to bring the past more vividly to view. Such pupils should be given this assignment and invited to carry it out for the benefit of the entire group.

The material contained in the subsection "In the Days of the Romans," should be sufficient to supply pupils with adequate under- 176

standing of the historical background of the play, "Julius Caesar." If pupils, through their reading in ancient history, feel that they have contributions to make through discussion, they should be encouraged to make them. Otherwise the discussion of the material actually here should be sufficient.

The teacher should make certain that the pupils read the two subsections on reading the play with particular care. They should gain from them the general and specific information which will help them to appreciate the play as a whole.

After all the preparatory material has been presented by the teacher and understood by the pupils, the final and most significant step in the preview will be taken. This will consist of the telling of the story of the play, preferably by the teacher and preferably in brief form. In such retelling the teacher should attempt to make entirely clear for the pupils the main action of the play as a whole, and the main action in each act. At this time she should stress only the progression of the action and the broad characterization of the main characters. 

Through such broad indications of act content the teacher can lead the pupils to feel the sweep and dramatic power of the general outline of the play. All this procedure takes place, of course, in the preliminary telling or reading of the play. Later, when the play is actually read by the pupils, the reading should proceed with a minimum of interruptions for explanation.

The teacher will find that at the end of each act a brief but helpful summary is provided, and in nearly all groups this summary, with the exercises following it, will be sufficient to fix clearly in the minds of the pupils the action and characterization which has taken place in the act concerned. Such interruption, coming as it does only at the end of each unit within the play, does not interfere with the general progress toward complete understanding and appreciation.

Thus, to sum up, the general procedure should be that pupils are prepared for reading "Julius Caesar" by, (1) knowledge of the times in which the play was written, (2) an understanding of the naturalness of Shakespeare's position in English literature, (3) an understanding of the Roman ways on which the play is based, and, (4) through preliminary narration of the play, an understanding of the play itself. When such preparation is complete the teacher should begin the actual class reading of the play designed to accomplish the following specific purposes through oral interpretation. 177

In encouraging pupils to find joy in the reading of "Julius Caesar" it is necessary to omit excessive discussion of the literary excellence of the play, and to omit all technical terms of playwriting that would be merely tedious and unhelpful for pupils on this level. Their enjoyment must come through appreciation of character development and the action of the plot. When pupils realize that this play is basically concerned with the theme that crime does not pay, they will be able to appreciate it in terms of many of their ordinary everyday interests. 

Perhaps to some this approach tends to belittle Shakespeare, but it must be remembered that people of his day accepted all this drama as a matter of everyday interest, and that they were not handicapped in their appreciation with the weight of three hundred years of reverence. The teachers can contribute more to the success of Shakespeare in the classroom by making his works enjoyable than they possibly can by stressing the aspects of his work which to them are magical but which to pupils are obscure. Gradually an awareness of literary excellence will, of course, be developed, and from time to time the teacher may point out ways in which Shakespeare shows his great distinction. But in general the teacher should remember that if pupils can learn to look upon Shakespeare as one whom they can read with pleasure, a great educational victory can be won. 

2. To promote social understanding and to develop character. This play contains unusually effective material for the carrying out of this important purpose. The men who conspired to kill knew that what they were about to do was not only criminal, but also dangerous to themselves. Pupils will realize that people do not lightly engage upon such activities, but do so only under the compulsion of powerful motives. This fact the teacher should bring out.

In this connection it should be pointed out that the motives of Cassius and Brutus were not the same- that Cassius was the man of malice who used the story of Caesar's ambition merely to build up desire on the part of others to kill him for the good of the state. Cassius himself was propelled by reasons of personal jealously and enmity. Brutus bowed to what he thought was the necessity of killing a tyrant. Brutus thought that what he did would make it possible for Romans to remain free, and eliminate the danger of their becoming bondsmen under a tyrant. 

Thus through a contrast of these men's motives, pupils will be helped to gain an understanding of the social situations which gave rise to the possibility of such motives. 178

Another aspect of this difference between Cassius and Brutus that is interesting to bring out lies in the fact that in almost every issue where policy was required Cassius in his suggestions was right, and Brutus in his was wrong, and that the whole final failure is traceable to this fact. 

For example, Cassius wanted Mark Antony murdered along with Caesar; 

Brutus would not permit it. If Antony had been killed the mob would not have been swayed against the conspirators. So it may be shown through many instances where these two men are in argument, including the activity of the final battle, that Cassius with his hard intelligence knew what should be done in their interests, but was denied by Brutus, whose decisions lead to defeat. Knowledge of all this leads to understanding of social situations, and leads also to an appreciation of character.

Nothing can be more fruitful or interesting by way of discussion in this play than the characters of Caesar, Mark Antony, Brutus, and Cassius. The characters of all these men are so clearly drawn that pupils can be helped to see that actual personal acquaintance could scarcely improve upon the pictures they have of these men. No man among these four was perfect; each of them is portrayed with all his good points and bad points brought out. Pupils may see here how character can be warped to the purposes of strong-minded men; how such a man as Brutus can be enlisted in murderous enterprise; how such a man as 

Antony may, through his cleverness, shift public sentiment completely from its course; how such a man as Caesar, stupendous in the field of battle, can appear futile and childish when others are unimpressed by his grandeur and importance. No better lessons can be taught in connection with the development of character than the development which 

Shakespeare creates in this play.

Thus without undue stressing of the ways in which these purposes may be carried out, the teacher should show how in the hands of a capable writer people grow on the printed page and live their lives for the appreciation of readers.

Summary Activities 

1. Section review. There is no actual section review in this section. 

However, as has been mentioned, each act in the play is followed by a summary and a series of exercises based upon the act. These pupil-aids will be of great assistance in helping pupils grasp the progress of the act and the development of character within the act. At the end of the play there are general study exercises, and these should serve to help pupils come to conclusions regarding the play as a whole. 179

2. Check on section goals and purposes. The teacher will, of course, find that some of her pupils have not gained from their reading of this play as much as they should have. It is likely that she should do no more in helping these pupils appreciate the play more fully than to clear up for them any confusions that they may have. To require them to go back over their reading and labor over an understanding they have not gained, would be to instill in them a dislike of one whose work they may later appreciate more fully. It is better to curb the possibility of complete dislike for Shakespeare than to make certain of constant and specific understanding of all the elements of his play. 

3. Use of book list. The teacher will make the customary use of the book list, making sure that all pupils who show a real interest in further reading along these lines are helped to find the books, either as listed here or found in local libraries, that will help them fulfill their interest in this subject.

STRANDS OF APPRECIATION 

Attitudes of thought and feeling resulting in habits of conduct are the outgrowth of appreciation. Appreciation, itself, is based upon awareness and understanding.

To call the pupil's attention to opportunities for appreciation, and to lend cumulative emphasis to desirable attitudes and habits of conduct, the selections in Expanding Literary Interests have been assembled into strands of appreciation. For the convenience of the teacher the table of contents thus arranged appears below. (It should be noted that in the book itself the table of contents is arranged in centers of interest.)
